   Ajahn Amaro

                BEFOREWORD   nce upon a time… In the time before time – when before and after did not mean what they do now; when here and there were not as they are within this world; when self and other, alive and not alive, overlapped and blurred – there was no good or bad, no light nor dark, no one world or another.  Then as things happen, light came into being. The light was very bright to those who could see, but as soon as it appeared it cast shadows, and they were dark indeed. The light knew itself at once and it was free and infinite, filled with delight; so the shadows began to enjoy the art of getting in the way. Thus it was that the dance began. The pulses of the universe took shape as crystal germs of being. The light informed every such seed with the joy of its own reality, but the shadows ever played and took their pleasure in proving themselves stronger than the light – or ever vainly trying to do so. The deepest of all shadows was the fact of ending, the death that comes to all that has begun. But was Death a single presence? Or did it change, divide? Sometimes it seemed quite solitary, total; at other times it had five faces, flowing, growing out and round each other – now ugly, dim and violent, now reasonable, now bright.  Mara was Death; was one yet also five: ‘I am the Ender, the Killer in the shadow’; ‘I am the Liar, who fulfils all desire’; ‘I am the House-builder, crafting mind and body’; ‘I’m the Thinker, who makes opinions true’; ‘A n d I am the Holy One, who shines brighter than the heavens.’ ‘I am the creeping evil, a snake, a golden deer; I am a goddess; I am your friend, your palace of reason and your nemesis.  ‘I am your birth and your certain death. Do you really think you can escape me?’

4 
Chapter 1:  The Hell of Molten Copper    hirsty?’ jested the hell warden, ‘’ere, try some of this.’ He yanked viciously on the woman’s hair from the back, cricking her neck and forcing open her mouth. The stone scoop in his hand glowed with a sick green sheen as licklets of ﬂame darted over the surface of the viscous liquid it contained.  ‘Drink up! Hyeh heh heh,’ the demon’s throaty chortle kept going despite being drowned in volume by her screams. His bird-claw hand held the scoop with practised steadiness as he poured the molten metal down her gullet. ‘WossamaNer?! Lost yer taste fer drink?’ Within seconds she was silenced by the lethal heat of the draught burning out her throat and her voice. It was scarcely slowed as it hit her stomach and seared her innards to a stinking crisp. The waves of pain pounded through her being, mounting in a rapid crescendo as the ﬂesh burned oﬀ her bones.  She felt the merciful blackness of unconsciousness rising in the wake of pain, but, as is the curse of all those condemned to such hells as these, no sooner had the bliss of oblivion been promised than she was wide awake and her body had been made quite whole again, ripe for torture once more.   * * * The reek of singed ﬂesh and smouldering hair was everywhere, blended with the fume of burning hoof. The stench bit the nostrils and so, even though every hot breath was gasped for, when it entered it chewed her nose and left her wincing.  Streams of molten copper swung lazily through the land around her; she had no memory of rural riverbanks and darting dragonﬂies amid the reeds but something in her knew that the gentle wending curves of blazing orange and green bore a resonance of another world. Here, now there was only heat, pain, clamour and stench.  There was no space or possibility for thought as the rhythms of pain and strain pounded on without relent.  The hell-dwellers were driven to hack ore-bearing rock from the cliﬀs around them. The wardens, beast-headed and rough-limbed, lashed any and all at every sign of laziness or pause. Weak or strong, young or old, female or male, all the Damned had to swing their picks and load the ore without respite.  ‘Wanna rest, do yer?!’ She heard the roaring of the wolf-faced overseer behind her, to the left. She risked a glance and saw him twisting an old, straggle-headed

5 
man by his ankle. ‘Why not take a dip in the river while yer at it?’ His scaley arm held the squealing man over the swirls of liquid metal. Vermillion-orange light coloured his features, now twisted in paNerns of dread. Purple shadows danced with amber and scarlet across his skin.  Wolf-head dunked him deep into the stream, paused as if counting to an accustomed span of seconds, then hoiked his victim from the current again. His ﬂesh was burned away and only the sinews held the skeleton together as it dripped molten metal on the bank.  Even as he held him there – like a dish-rag among a load of laundry – the ﬂesh began to reform on the bones and the howls of boNomless pain emerged from the new-made throat-ﬂesh.  If she could have remembered the sight of a buNerﬂy emerging from its chrysalis and drying its new-formed wings in the morning sun, perhaps this miraculous spreading of new ﬂesh would have brought this to mind. As it was, the piercing pick of her fellow suﬀerer’s screams slammed into her ears and her own terror of being caught pausing and watching too long made her jerk her eyes away and bury herself in her task of loading her steel cart full of the copper ore.   * * *  Once their waggon was fully laden, she set herself behind the hauling bar with her work-gang partner and set to, pushing it to the smelting vats far across the valley.   The eﬀort was wickedly painful, as the weight of the rock they had to pull meant that their feet were forced with greater intensity against the burning ground. Flesh was roasted oﬀ their feet as they walked, the tang of their own scorching skin being left in a trail above their pathway. She could feel her bones grinding against the boiling rock and the slicing pain as the ﬂesh reformed between each step.   Her partner was gibbering ﬁtfully. He started by murmuring the usual litany of the Damned: ‘I can’t go on… I can’t go on…’ This was a familiar cry and the plea of all of them but, since they all knew there was no other possibility than to submit to their tortures, they contented themselves with the pale satisfaction of bewailing their fate and carried on. There was nothing else.   But his murmurs had turned to ﬂailing cries, his eyes were swimming wildly in their sockets. Now his legs began to crumple and she had to jerk him back to his senses.  ‘Don’t give up. Don’t drop – you know what they’ll do to you. Get a grip!’ She barked at him, trying her best to pierce the fog that was clearly mounting in his mind.   She had vivid memories of the last time he had swooned. As he lay in a crouched heap the hell-wardens – one with a boar’s head and thick-bristled arms, another sporting a horse’s face but with ﬂesh-eater’s fangs – laid into him with their ﬁre-whips and tore the ﬂesh from his bones. He had huddled there whimpering

6 
miserably, pleading for mercy, while they had waited for his ﬂesh to reform and then they did it again – lashed him until the threads of muscle hung smoking on his bones and laughing with each other all the while. Her heart broke to see it but she was powerless. Her tears boiled and burned her smuNed cheeks as they rolled down.   Sometimes she was surprised at the strength she found within herself, although she only noticed it when she saw how much more easily others succumbed to the pain and heat of torture. Also, that strength mostly showed itself as anger at her tormentors and a deep resentment of their treatment of the Damned. Now her pulling-mate was spiralling into madness and seemed certain to collapse again beside her.   ‘It’s never going to end… the horror, it’s never going to end.’ He fell to his knees and looked up into her face, ‘I can’t… I… see… even the Buddha has come to mock us… we’re damned and it won’t end. No one… Don’t laugh at me!’  She saw him looking past her in a strange haunted way and, as she desperately tried to heave him up, she glanced over her shoulder, to where her companion was looking.   Framed in ﬂame against the high rock walls, a tall robed ﬁgure stood, seemingly ﬂoating in the smoke-threaded air. In his right hand he held a blazing walking staﬀ; crimson and mauve ﬁre licked around his body as gold and rainbow-coloured light radiated from his heart.   It was the Buddha, and his face, wriNen with the gravest compassion as it was, also carried a distinct and gentle smile.   She had no time to think of what to make of this apparition – or whether this being was truly mocking their misery – she had only the will to act, and now. This was her whole concern.   * * *  Her partner had fallen and the burning rock beneath their feet now scorched the skin of his arms and face, his body. Acrid smoke rose as he smouldered there. Her sinews strained as she pulled his inert form up and draped him over the pulling bar. In the same breath she pressed all her weight against the drag of the loaded cart and pushed with every ounce of strength she had, grimly determined to do the work of both of them.   Sweat boiled on her forehead in sizzling rivulets. Her hands were seared by the bar but her pain was by now beyond a feeling.   ‘Push! Just push. Don’t think. Just do.’ She muNered aloud, ‘They will not get him. I will not let them.’ Her will was a bright diamond, a seed of blinding radiance, impervious to heat and pain, untouched by reek and racket. In that moment her being was drawn into a single blazing point. All else fell away and she knew that she had won – the waggon was underway and she was unstoppable. Her friend had been saved from further torture.

7 
 As the ﬂush of success dawned within her breast – its thrill almost rapturous amid the gloom and threat – she seemed to feel her own strength faltering simultaneously. The landscape shimmered and shifted around her, everything started to feel thinner, distant, as if she was losing her purchase on the world, although she still seemed to be pushing the cart along with ease.   A collage of images ﬁlled her vision as it began to swim, dream-pictures that linked with each other yet were drifting apart; they had reached the smelting pools so she was stopping the cart; her ears were ﬁlled with a soft rushing sound, like waves shooshing across a shore and soaking into the sand; her partner raised his head and smiled; the heat that pounded relentlessly from the ground below was there but it caused no pain; the scarlet ﬁres and molten pools seemed transparent, shell-like, and a golden light rose around everything she saw.   It was cool; there was softness; she was fading, rising, ﬂoating in a gently giddying spiral.   She was disappearing into a single point but that point was everywhere.   ==*==

Chapter 2:  Forbidden Fruit  don’t know, Krishna, it doesn’t feel right to me. It isn’t honest.’ Maggot’s brow furrowed around the rose-coloured circlet that she habitually wore. ‘I don’t like it at all, hiding away, in secret.’ ‘But Maggot, most people can’t see you anyway – you’re permanently hidden from almost everyone,’ Krishna glanced at his partner sideways, checking to see if his point had been convincing. It had not.   ‘If it’s not a secret, why have you not told the other monks about me? Why do you have to sneak away to spend time with me and make up excuses to fool the other monks and novices? You’re even proud of how clever your lies are. They can’t see me because I’m a kinnari, but they can see you and besides, whether they can see me or not, I’m sure they would not approve of a monk having a girlfriend.’  ‘But that’s just it!’ Krishna grinned, ‘Since you’re not a “girl” but a “celestial being” it’s not against the rules – at least I’m preNy sure it’s not – well, not for monks anyway. I’m only nineteen Rains old, so I’m not a full monk yet and I’ve never heard anyone say a novice should not have a partner who’s a deva.’ As he was saying this, he was aware that he had made sure that he had not told KhujjuNara about his relationship with Maggot. She was a much respected lay follower of the Buddha, and a friend of Krishna, but she had no abilities to see devas and Krishna had not wanted to risk her disapproval. On account of this he had ‘neglected to mention to her’ this partnership with Maggot. His other elder mentor, the ragged monk Dusaka, who was from some remotely related spiritual tradition, seemed to be aware already that Krishna and Maggot were lovers but for his own reasons had not scowled at or scolded them for this. He was always diﬃcult to read and could never be second-guessed.  ‘Well that’s not a surprise,’ she grumbled, continuing to be unimpressed, ‘how often does the subject come up in an average conversation? And isn’t the monastic life based on “brahmacariya” – everyone in the monastery living as brothers and sisters and not having husbands, wives, partners?’  ‘You know, Maggot,’ Krishna, fully determined to push his point, now tried a diﬀerent angle of aNack, ‘there’s this theory about the word “brahmacariya”– it can mean “divine conduct” and they like to translate that as “celibacy”, no love-partners and so on, but it also means “walking with the gods” or “going with the holy ones” – don’t you see? You are one of the divine ones, you are a goddess, so if I “go” with you, I am in fact following the “brahmacariya”. It’s right there in the word.’  ‘So, why do you have to pretend to the other monks that you are doing other things when we are spending time together – I don’t get it.’   ‘Well, it’s a philosophical problem,’ he looked at Maggot sideways again, testing the eﬀect of this proclamation.

9 
 ‘Oh,’ Maggot stretched out her legs meaningfully across the width of her nest. She leaned back on the weave-work of branches, looked up through the leaf-canopy of the Ghositarama forest and laced her ﬁngers behind her head. ‘This sounds like it will be interesting. Please go on,’ she ﬁxed Krishna with a stare that was equal parts innocent ﬂower and hawk surveying a rabbit, ‘I can’t wait to hear this.’  Krishna seNled himself into a niche of branches, his dark face alight with the relief of at last talking this all-important subject through with his belovèd, as well as with some trepidation that it might go in a direction that he was keen to avoid.   ‘You see,’ he leaned close to her, ‘the truth is…’  ‘The truth?’ Maggot raised an eyebrow, well-knowing Krishna’s ability to make presumptions and to be ‘certain’ where a guess would be more realistic.   ‘Alright, how I see it is that the Master only keeps up a front about all this “abstaining from sexual desires” stuﬀ because that’s what’s expected of the traditional monks’ life. The Buddha secretly approves of seeking sensual pleasure but only pretends that the standard is celibacy and renunciation for monks and nuns because that is what society expects. It’s because he has to get along and work with all those life-negators that things appear to be set up in the way they are. Those who are in the know realize this and are aware that the Master in truth sees that such life-aﬃrming ways, like the pursuit of love and pleasure, are all beautiful and good.   ‘After all, what about his teachings on “The Middle Way” and his rejection of the old ascetic path of self-torture – doesn’t this all shout loudly that he is a lover of life and not one who would deny or oppose it?  ‘The Master’s message is the very spirit of freedom, breaking through the tight shell of austerity and life-denial.’ Krishna warmed to his theme. ‘That spirit, that urge towards enlightenment, is the life-pulse of the universe – how could it go against our love for each other? Don’t you see how our being together helps us to drink in that spirit, helps us to emerge from our small-minded habits? In fact we should always reach for that spirit, that life-impulse – to have it sooner, to always seek for more – this is the Way, the Great Mangala! I know it!’ Krishna’s eyes were alight with inspiration.   ‘But I don’t like to feel that it all has to be secret,’ Maggot’s sincere and open gaze met that inspiration and followed it back to Krishna’s heart, ‘don’t you understand that?’  ‘I do. But, you know, I like the excitement of it – don’t you? You are my gorgeous, succulent forbidden fruit – Jambu-sirivanna, “Beautiful Radiance of the Rose-Apple” is your proper name – see: “Jambu”. It’s even there in what you are called,’ he smirked.   ‘I prefer being a “maggot” to being “forbidden”, thank you.’ She smiled at Krishna, enjoying his artless, excited innocence if not his message. ‘It makes me feel bad too, the idea that the Master might be deliberately deceiving people.’  ‘No, it’s not lying,’ Krishna jumped in, leaning forward with his hands now on Maggot’s knees, ‘it’s what we call “skilful means”, as it has a noble cause in mind. It’s not the whole of the story but more like a tale made up for children, like the

10 
Tooth Fairy. When they’re ready for the truth, they will be let into the secret.’ He felt he had ﬁnally got his point across but could see there was disquiet still wriNen through the look on Maggot’s face.   ‘One thing I don’t understand,’ his tone changing as he leaned in, ‘is why you have become all worried about this now. We’ve been together here at Ghositarama for more than a year and you’ve been ﬁne with the coming and going in my monk’s robes and spliNing my time between you and the routine of the monastery. What’s brought this on, about how you and me are together?’  As his words formed, the eyes of his celestial partner looked downwards. ‘I’m not happy mainly because it’s not just the two of us any more.’  ‘Why should that be a problem?’ Krishna seemed genuinely puzzled. ‘The fact that your kinnari friends, Ant and Bee, and Gumby the yakkha, are around all the time now? Hardly any of the monks and novices can see them either, and even if they could, why does that make diﬃculties for you and me? I don’t get it…’  ‘No, you don’t get it!’ Maggot now glared at him and, stretching the rose-pink ﬁlm of her gown tightly over her (slightly-larger-than-usual) midriﬀ, she arched her back and brought her bowing, navel-free belly up level with his nose.   ‘Speaking of shells and emerging from them – look! It’s not just the two of us now; soon we’ll have a hatchling to think of as well.’  * * *  To their mutual delight Sugandhi and Jayanta had been married in Kulluta, on a day rejoicing in many marks of good fortune, not long after Krishna had left the village. The wedding had cost her parents a large slice of their fortune, the bulk of it going on the elaborate and extensive puriﬁcation rituals needed so that a girl from the lowest ranks of candalas, the matangas, the animal slaughterers, could wed a member of the sudra caste. The boy was the Headman’s son too so that, in addition to the priests, the dowry to Jayanta’s parents had been extortionate. Even then his mother – known by all as Mata-ji – still twitched an uncomfortable nostril at the ‘arrangement’. There were the appropriate smiles and inclinations of the head but the signals below the orders of propriety were that: ‘The girl is disgusting and probably spiritually polluted, despite all the ritual cleansings, but never mind… I have no objections to the match.’ The gold coming from the girl’s family stemmed from her father’s role as the appointed Royal Slaughterer; he was responsible for the care and eventual dispatch of warhorses injured or too old to ﬁght, as well as the pasturing of brood mares and foals for the King’s stables. Caste-wise the family could not have been lower but the gleam of coin had won the argument when Jayanta had declared the girl to be the one he wished to be his bride.   The house that Sugandhi and Jayanta lived in was at the north end of Kulluta, on the steep western bank of the rushing Vipasa River, so it was one of the ﬁrst ones to catch the morning sun as it climbed above the Himalayan foothills to their east. The air was fresher here – well away from the rank fume of the knacker’s yard

11 
where she grew up – but still it was tinged with odours of conﬂict. Mata-ji was always on her back correcting her clothes, the food she cooked, her speech, even the way she walked – nothing she did was ever right. Things had eased up a bit, Sugandhi admiNed, when her fertility had been proven by motherhood now being imminent. The endless clucking litany of corrections were now interspersed with occasional self-satisﬁed crowings, which surprised her as it seemed that Mata-ji had forgoNen that it was her daughter-in-law that was the mother-to-be, not herself.   The improvement of mood around the house was tempered in Sugandhi by a concern that only she was party to. She was anxious about the forthcoming child, as any mother might be, but for reasons unbeknownst to her husband or anyone else:– That ‘goodbye kiss’ with Krishna turned into a liNle more than she had planned, and his skin was darker than baked-on blood at the boNom of the vats. What colour was her baby going to be?  It was with great relief, when her daughter was born, that she saw the infant’s skin was not blue-black like Krishna’s. Rather, in what she took to be a small miracle – and perhaps revealing her own wished-for royal ancestry, as she had often fantasised that she was in truth ‘The Lost Princess of Taxila’ – the baby girl was blessed with the fair complexion her mother most admired.   ‘I think I’ll call her HariNaca-Kumari, “Princess Golden-Skin”,’ she reﬂected. For the sunny, big-eyed child, who now smiled up at her, had the golden colouring of a mango.   * * *  Maggot was not the only kinnari in the Ghosita forest with an unusual partner. Her friend Bee had seNled into a colourful and occasionally raucous relationship with Gumbiya, a yakkha who had once threatened to eat them but who had, in the process of helping to rescue her sister Minti from a grisly death, saved Bee’s life and won her heart. Then there was Ant, the third of the kinnari companions who would have loved to make a nest with Ninka, an exotically beautiful deva from the heights of the Heaven of the Thirty-Three Gods, had she been given the chance. Ninka’s duties in the Thirty-Three meant she needed to make an appearance there from time to time; this should not have been a problem had not time itself been problematic: when Ninka ‘slipped away for a few minutes’ from Ghosita’s forest, those ‘few minutes’ in the Thirty-Three were several seasons long, even a whole sun-turning, in the Human Realm.  Gumbiya was a few cups of nectar-mead into the retelling of one of his favourite war stories – the volume consumed matching the likelihood of him forgeNing that he had told Bee the tale before – when Ant arrived at speed at their nest among the trees.   ‘Bee, come quickly. Maggot’s egg is on its way – now!’ With impressive agility, Gumbiya dropped his cup, stepped outside the broad branch-woven nest Bee had made with his help, and expanded his form to almost

12 
tree-height. Gently picking up both Ant and Bee in his rough-clawed hands – without pause in the ﬂuidity of motion – he sprang aloft and shot across the forest to where Maggot and Krishna had their place, buried deep in the undergrowth at the far edge of the trees.  The moment of the ‘ﬁrst birth’ had come whilst they were on their way. An aura of weary peace hung over Maggot and her nest; Krishna sat beside her wide-eyed and open-mouthed, while in her lap, nestled and swaddled in layers of petal-ﬁlm and ﬂower-down, lay the opalescent jewel of Maggot’s egg.  Kinnari are called the ‘twice born’ not because (like the brahmins) they have a physical birth and then a ceremony to make them ‘proper brahmins’ but because like birds, or ﬂowers, they are born twice. Flowers and trees produce seeds or nuts, birds lay their eggs and so do the kinnari.  The child remains egg-born for the ﬁrst three Moons, while it quickens and grows within the shell, then when it is fully formed and ready for the open air, it breaks the shell and is born its second time.  Gumbiya put Bee and Ant down with as much care as he could, then shrank to as compact a human size as possible. He was never going to be trim but he could ﬁt into the narrower space with all the others if he tried. They crowded around.  Maggot glowed with joyful pride, with Ant and Bee either side of her, stroking the radiant colours of the eggshell together. Krishna was still agape; despite being very aware that his beloved was an earth-deva, a kinnari, part of him was expecting a child to be born like other children. The uNer strangeness of Maggot producing an egg, and then the iridescent beauty and size of it, fully boggled his mind. And what made it even more amazing was how normal it seemed to Maggot and her friends:– He loved her and he loved… it… but it was an egg… which was preNy strange… for him at least. Love and confusion.   * * *  ‘What is it?’ Loni, one of the smaller children asked.  ‘I don’t know,’ the eldest and tallest, Dinka, replied, trying to edge up a tree-trunk to get a beNer look. ‘It’s bright and shiny, but its colours keep changing, like a rainbow on a bubble of soap. It looks like a stone – its shape stays the same…’ Bhima drew close through the bushes, looking down for snakes and giant centipedes as he made his way.   ‘I can’t see!’ complained Dido, ‘someone lift me up,’ this was a common occurrence for the smallest of the friends from the village, out on their daily mission to gather grass and leaves from the forest for animal-feed.  Dinka, the leader of the gatherers hoicked tiny Dido onto her shoulders so he could see through the dense shrubbery and tangled vines.   ‘Oh,’ now Dido had the best view of them all, ‘it looks like it’s ﬂoating on air. It’s a big coloured stone and it’s in a kind of huge nest – and it is in the air, like a bubble…’

13 
 ‘Let me see,’ Loni elbowed her way forward, past her sister, trying to peer through the confusing mass of leaves and branches. ‘Look! The way it changes colour – they’re all moving around. What is it?’  ‘It’s an egg,’ a ﬁrm and friendly adult voice spoke from behind them. ‘It’s a deva’s egg, to be more precise, that of a kinnari – one of the ﬂower spirits.’ The children all turned, ready to be scolded, but the wrinkled and ragged monk smiled broadly.  ‘Be quiet and respectful, and maybe you’ll be able to get a liNle closer. Come this way.’ Dusaka led them around the side, to Krishna’s secret entrance to the nest. He was there with Maggot on a visit but siNing oﬀ in the shadows, and the two of them had been chuckling at the wonder-ﬁlled conversation of the village children. It was Maggot herself who had suggested Dusaka invite them in.   ‘Come on,’ Dusaka beckoned them into the narrow open space within the tangled bower. He sat himself down on a branch to one side and the ﬁve youngsters gathered round the mysterious presence. To their eyes the opal-coloured egg ﬂoated – its shell shimmering and glistening with ever-shifting hues – about a hand-span above the soft ﬂower-down and moss-like lining of the nest. In reality it sat cradled on Maggot’s lap but as the children could not see her, it seemed to hover like a strange jewel from another realm, like one of the nine gems of the Bejaratana, which in a way it was.  Maggot smiled in warm appreciation at the ﬁve wide-eyed, bedazzled children. One or two of them glanced at her as if they could almost, from the corners of their eyes, discern her outline and see her face. She lifted the egg that contained her precious child and gently moved it towards them.  Dusaka asked, ‘Who would like to oﬀer a blessing to the new baby? It will emerge from its egg very soon.’ Five faces turned to him. ‘Any of you?’   ‘Us?’ Dinka was shocked.   ‘We can’t give blessings,’ her brother Bhima chimed in, ‘we’re not brahmins or monks and nuns who follow the Buddha. We’re just farmers from the village.’  ‘According to the customs of the kinnari,’ explained Dusaka, ‘all beings are considered to be of equal nature; all friends and visitors alike are invited to oﬀer a blessing.’ One by one, in order of age, the children came up and laid their palms on the warm shell of the egg: Dinka, Bamba, Bhima, Loni and Dido. Close up, the egg was fragrant as well but none of them could say just what that scent reminded them of – a ﬂower, spring mist, the earth after rain – petrichor – there was no name for it but they would not forget it either.  Five pairs of grubby hands, ﬁngernails dark and broken, were laid in turn upon the nascent child, blessing and being blessed in return.   * * *

14 
Krishna had his arm round Maggot, the two of them transﬁxed by the gurgling newborn in her arms. The nest was crowded. Ant and Ninka sat on one side while Bee held up a ﬁnger for the baby to grab on to.  Gumbiya sat behind her, wedged on some thicker branches while Rhamba and Salassa, of the lineage of heavenly dancers and musicians, wove protective charms and spells to ward oﬀ all ills, spinning and undulating through the air around them.   ‘What shall we call her?’ Maggot was asking herself as much as Krishna beside her, looking into the child’s new-hatched yet ancient eyes. ‘What’s your name, liNle one? Half-human and half-kinnari that you are.’ Her skin was tawny and her few tufts of hair were copper-lit in the morning sunbeams, sieved by the bower. ‘Her hair is just like my sister Tamba’s was – almost red when it catches the light,’ said Krishna.   ‘Should we call her that, “Tamba”? Or would that be a bad mangala, inauspicious?’ Maggot smiled as she mused.   ‘How about “Tambaka” then?’  ‘It’s close enough to honour her memory but diﬀerent enough too. She was my sister, after all, and even though she did a lot of bad things it wasn’t all her fault, and she had many wonderful qualities too. I’d like to remember her in a good way and this preNy liNle mite can be a way for us to do that. Are you happy with that?’ Krishna was not sure that this was wise but it seemed so right at the same time. He looked sideways at Maggot to gauge her response.   ‘“Tambaka”, is that who you are?’ Maggot touched her ﬁnger to the baby’s lips.   The newly-hatched girl lifted her hand to her mother’s and smiled, her eyes ﬂashing bright ﬁre, like shining metal.   ==*==

15 
CHAPTER 3:  THE STOLEN CHILD  ou haven’t shaved your head yet,’ remarked Ananda. ‘Wha…?’ responded Krishna, surprised to see the Elder coming down the path, at this back part of the monastery woods. ‘It’s the full moon – look – the sun is setting there in the west and the moon is rising in the east. By the time it’s above the forest we should be at the hall for the Recitation.’   Krishna realized he had completely forgoNen this fortnightly head-shaving but he could not reveal to Ananda that it was because he was taken up with caring for Maggot and the new baby. That was out of the question.   ‘Oh, right!’ He blustered, ‘I’ll do that before dawn tomorrow.’  ‘And,’ Ananda continued, giving Krishna a somewhat studied sideways glance, ‘as it’s the Komudi full moon, this makes it your birthday; if I remember correctly. And isn’t your proper name Komudi – that no one ever uses – on account of you being born on this day?’  ‘You remember everything!’ the impressed Krishna retorted.   ‘Perhaps not everything,’ Ananda smiled, ‘but a lot.’ He was arranging his robe over his shoulder as they spoke. ‘And, as this is your twentieth, you could request full ordination and become a monk today – a fully ﬂedged bhikkhu.’ He repeated the slightly loaded side-glance, checking Krishna for his reaction to this thinly-veiled hint. ‘Since the Master is here in the Ghositarama, if you were to request the Full Acceptance today, it could be a very short and simple ceremony; he just says, ‘Ehi bhikkhu’ and it’s done.’   ‘That would be a great honour, as well as being really convenient,’ Krishna replied, his mind now racing to calculate how long all of these unexpected (at least for him) ceremonies would take. He was now in a dilemma, torn between loyalties, as he had promised Maggot that he would take care of the baby for the night whilst she went to visit her parents over in the Simsapa grove, outside the city of Kosambi. He wondered if he had time to ask Bee or Ant, or Gumbiya to take a message to her, or to ﬁll in for him, or if it would all be over quickly so he could get back while Tambaka was still resting peacefully, as she had just dropped oﬀ to sleep before Ananda had appeared.  ‘What should I do?!’ His mind raced. Not being risk-averse he decided to gamble on the baby sleeping peacefully for the next hour – hidden away in their nest in the deep thicket – so he said, ‘Let me get my robe from my kuti and we’ll ask if I can be ordained this evening – that will be a great opportunity. Let’s do it!’  Ever since Krishna had joined the Sangha as a novice, after the tragic and dramatic deaths of his two sisters, Amba and Tamba (laNerly known as Queen Samavati and Queen Magandiya) the kindly Ananda had taken him under his wing,

16 
oﬀering solace and companionship when needed and a steady stream of small tasks to keep the young and restless spiritual aspirant supplied with some outlets for his abundant energies. It had been Ananda’s hope that young Krishna would make this more substantial commitment to the monastic life when the time came so he was pleasantly surprised now how liNle conversation had been needed to encourage the lad to see that this increased level of discipline and rigour in his endeavours would be a worthwhile step to take. It is true that part of him wondered whether the boy really appreciated what he was now asking to take on – with all the extra observances and limitations, a monk having 227 rules compared with a novice’s modest ten – but Ananda was always inclined to look on the bright side, taking the optimistic view. Thus he was delighted to be striding down the moonlit paths of the small Ghositarama forest with young Krishna, to support him in undertaking the monk’s life with the Buddha himself as his preceptor – the one inducting him into the Order.  The fat yellow moon glowed above the trees ringing the small assembly hall. Crickets and cicadas ﬁlled the warm evening air with a susurrating wall of sound. An owl hooted before taking oﬀ on silent wings and dozens of robed ﬁgures gravitated quietly to the open sides of the hall from narrow paths between the shadowy trunks and forest undergrowth.  As Krishna and Ananda approached, they saw that the Buddha was seated on a small bench, in the open area just before the hall. He was talking with a group of monks, seated on a grass mat below him. The monks turned as Ananda approached and, in a familiar ﬂuid movement, made space for the Buddha’s aNendant and his young dark companion to kneel and pay their respects to the Master.  ‘This is the novice Krishna, Venerable Sir,’ Ananda began, ‘also known as Komudi since he was born on this full moon day twenty years ago.’ ‘I remember him too,’ the Buddha responded, ‘as the brother of the two queens of Vamsa, who both died not yet two years ago.’  Krishna was ﬂushed with pride that the Master recognized him and remembered his notable background.  ‘I also recall meeting their father, the pilgrim Kamanita, who was resistant to the teachings I oﬀered him but who may yet, in future ages, come to understand the Dharma that I taught him.’ Krishna was not so sure what to make of that comment – whether to be pleased that the Buddha had met the father that Krishna could hardly remember or whether to be chastened that his father had failed to appreciate the Master’s words – so, into the ringing silence of that moment he spoke, to both get away from the awkward feeling and to express his current aspiration:  ‘Venerable Sir, may I request the Full Acceptance as a bhikkhu in this Dharma and Discipline?’ ‘Do you have your alms-bowl and three robes?’ the Buddha asked, these being the physical necessities that every monk must possess, in his community.

17 
‘I have my bowl and two robes,’ Krishna began, ‘but the third robe, the double layered robe of patches that novices don’t have but monks do… that I have not made yet… I…’ ‘I can help with that,’ interjected Ananda, reaching deftly behind him to where a small bundle was placed on the mat at the edge of the cleared area in front of the hall. ‘I believe that this double-layered sanghati robe is the same size as your upper robe, Krishna. This is oﬀered to you – I prepared it in case you were inclined to ask for ordination as I noticed that, amongst the many ways you liked to spend your time, sewing was not included.’   * * *  ‘Ehi, bhikkhu’ – ‘Come, monk’ was formally uNered by the Buddha, with Krishna as the sole candidate that evening.  At that moment his life as a fully-ordained bhikkhu began. The surge of excitement and inspiration was great but, in the back of his mind, he was looking to see how he could get away and return to his parenting duties at the nest:– How was Tambaka doing on her own?  After his ordination – which was impressively brief – the Recitation of the Rule took another hour. However, as that ceremony came to an end, he had a sinking feeling that he was going to be there quite a while longer as the crowd of lay people had thickened at the back part of the hall and the Buddha was invited to ascend the Dharma seat to oﬀer a discourse. The Master began by greeting the many visitors: locals from Kosambi as well as travellers from Magadha, Koliya, Kosala, and his homeland of the Sakya kingdom as well as the northern land of UNarakuru.  The monastics were from various regions too – nuns as well as monks – so the range of his listeners was very broad, suggesting that the Master’s discourse would cover a lot of ground, to meet the experiences and background of such a variety of folk.  Krishna sat in agony, hardly hearing any of the wise words, as he was desperate to get away but, as the lone new bhikkhu, he had no choice but to stay seated near the front, vainly trying to pretend he was keenly hanging on every word.  When the Buddha ﬁnally closed, with his customary words of, ‘Now is the time for you to do as you see ﬁt,’ the assembly of lay people began to disperse but quite a number gathered around Krishna, to wish him well with his new undertaking and to oﬀer him gifts.   He was, again, hoping this would be over soon but at the same time he was charmed by the dewy-eyed devotion that some of the women were displaying towards him. He was eager to get away but something in him was relishing the aNention and the aﬀection being shown to him. It was well on into the night, at least the end of the ﬁrst watch, before, scurrying hurriedly in a most unmonkly way, he got back to the nest and, to his horror, found that Tambaka was gone.

18 
 * * *   ‘Oh no!’ Krishna’s mind was a storm of thoughts and emotions: trying to guess where the baby had gone, and how; trying to think of a way he could get help to ﬁnd her; making up excuses as to how he could have lost her when he was the responsible adult, supposedly – it was a tumult of anxious feelings and scaNered, ﬂailing ideas.  There was no sign of any disturbance of the nest and no bloodstains so it didn’t look like she had been seized by a wild animal or a hawk.  The bright moonlight now cast intense black shadows up against the silvered fronds of the trees and the leaf-liNer on the forest ﬂoor, thus it was hard to see if there were any footprints or broken twigs to show if people had been present. There were no signs at all. The forest rang with the incessant buzz of crickets, laid over an eerie foreboding silence and stillness. There were no sounds of movement, like the baby crawling in the bushes or murmuring for her mother; there was nothing.  ‘Help. I need help… Bee and Gumby, they’re nearby!’ Krishna’s feet started down the path to where his friends had their own bower in the woods. He raced through the stripes of moonlight and roused them, shouting their names as he approached, heedless of any disturbance he might have been making to the others who might have been meditating or resting in the area.  ‘Bee! Gumby! Come quick! I need you!’ He crashed into their forest shelter, waking them from their contented slumbers.  ‘What on earth is the maNer?’ Bee grumbled, ‘It’s halfway through the night, don’t you have any respect…’ ‘I’ve lost the baby’ Tam’s gone!’ Krishna’s face was such a picture of fear and distress that Bee quickly changed her tone from disgruntled to concerned.  ‘What?! What happened? How could you lose a baby who is too young even to crawl?’ Krishna gabbled a description of events, trying not to come across as too foolish or irresponsible whilst giving them the facts of it.  ‘So, how long were you away from the nest?’    ‘It was the whole ﬁrst watch, four hours altogether, but I was sure it would only be short while.’  ‘You were sure – well that was wrong for a start,’ Bee raised an accusatory eyebrow and was about to launch into a colourful listing of Krishna’s shortcomings but his shaNered expression and the need to act fast dissuaded her.  ‘We need to get Maggot here as soon as possible – right, where’s my cintamani?’ She rummaged for the talking-stone that she and her kinnari friends used to communicate with over great distances. Gumbiya, meanwhile had roused himself to action and was about to oﬀer Krishna a drink of ﬂower-mead to bolster his spirits when he remembered that Krishna was a novice monk and thus prohibited from taking alcohol.  Krishna saw him pulling out his ﬂask and said, ‘Don’t mind me – you go ahead and have a stiﬀener. Besides, I’m not just a novice now but a fully-ordained

19 
monk – that’s part of what took so long – so mead is even more forbidden than before.’  ‘When did that happen?’ Asked Gumbiya.  ‘Just after moonrise. So I’m preNy new to this.’ Krishna thought for a moment, then added, ‘There might however be a case for calling it “medicinal” as it would certainly make me feel beNer.’ Gumbiya, despite being a warrior yakkha and not one of reﬁned morals, felt this was preNy dubious logic but responded, ‘It’s your call. If you want some, here it is.’ Krishna looked at the ﬂask, looked away, then reached out for it, taking a hearty pull as he shut down his reservations.     * * *   ‘Maggot, come back here quick. There’s no way to dress it up, Tambaka’s gone missing... Yes... Krishna lost her somehow... No we’ve no idea... Gumby will come and get you, he can go at ten times your speed. He’s on his way already.’ Bee’s tense words punctured the midnight silence of the forest. ‘See you soon. I’ll tell Ant and Ninka... We’ll start the search.’ When she arrived, crashing through the canopy of leaves on Gumby’s stout arm, she was equal parts distraught at Tambaka’s disappearance and enraged at Krishna’s thoughtless folly and his divided loyalties.  ‘How could you have been so stupid!? So selﬁsh! What kind of a father are you?! Don’t answer that!’ Ant and Ninka, her deva partner from the Heaven of the Thirty-Three, appeared and the whole group began to search the paths and undergrowth all around the nest of Maggot and Krishna.  There were traces of Tambaka’s fragrance in the nest itself but nothing remained on the ground or on any of the paths or bushes nearby. No hints of it lingered on the air as it was now several hours since she had gone, or been taken, or whatever had happened. There was no sign.  ‘Ninka, you’re the best tracker, at least your deva-senses are a bit more acute than the rest of us. What do you make of this? There are a few human footprints in the dust of the path but they could have been made any time, by anyone walking through the back part of the monastery. What do your eyes see? Any clues?’ Ninka knelt in the space around the entry to the bower but, since Krishna and Maggot had tried to keep their place hidden, it was understandable that it was hard to be sure of anything. There were recent footprints but she wasn’t ready to say exactly what time they were made or who might have made them.  ‘I think we need to do more searching,’ was all she eventually said.    * * *

20 
 The rest of the night passed as the team of friends searched high and low, calling Tambaka’s name in the vain hope there might be a response. Also fearing that they might come upon her small, lifeless body somewhere. This, thankfully, they did not ﬁnd but otherwise the hunt was fruitless. The baby had vanished completely.  ‘She must have been taken by someone, or something, but by who or what?’ Maggot’s tears were not abating, even as dawn approached. She was slumped at the edge of their nest.  Krishna cleared his throat, ‘Er... I will need to be going.’ He winced, even as he said it as he knew how out of place it was, in the midst of this crisis.  ‘It’s my ﬁrst day as a monk and I’m expected to go on the alms-round with the others this morning.’ ‘What?’ Maggot snapped. ‘Are you serious? Really?!’ Krishna made a careful study of the ground around his toes and kept quiet in the awkward ache of the moment.  ‘Just go. Don’t say anything, just go.’  She was at breaking point, the loss of her precious child pulling on her heart-strings and now to have to deal with the fecklessness of her supposed belovèd. Krishna left without a word, as quickly as he could.   * * *   Dawn light came to the forest and with it some of the children of the village. The ﬁve friends scurried up the path that led through this back part of the Ghositarama forest. From the other direction the familiar ragged ﬁgure of the monk Dusaka appeared.  ‘Guess what we saw!’ ‘Last night, when we were collecting ﬁrewood.’  ‘It was so strange – the baby from the nest, the one you showed us.’ Dusaka calmed them with a gesture and said, ‘One at a time – what did you see?’ As the children clustered around him the kinnaris, Ninka and Gumbiya, all unseen by the youngsters, swiftly gathered round to listen too. Dinka, being the eldest was the self-appointed spokesperson:  ‘We were ’ere in the forest collecting wood and kindling for the cook-ﬁres; it was after sunset but the moon was bright so we could see clearly, at least clearly enough to gather sticks. Anyway we were round here near the hidden nest when we saw the baby being lifted up in the air, ﬂoating like we’d seen ’er when she was in ’er mother’s lap. At ﬁrst we thought it was the invisible mother carrying ’er but then, when a few of us asked to look at the baby again – zoom! It was carried oﬀ real fast. Like we weren’t supposed to see or follow. Whoever had the baby went ﬂying oﬀ down the path then up into the trees and away. It was so diﬀerent from the time we came before that we all guessed that some demon or a deva had stolen the baby

21 
away. That’s what it looked like so we came to tell the dad, the black-skinned novice we met before, but you can tell him for us, and the baby’s mum too. That’s what happened. We wanted to tell someone ’cause they’re bound to be worried.’  * * *   Dusaka was some kind of a monk – and he had been known by the kinnari and Krishna and their friends for a long time – but none of them were quite sure exactly what kind of monk he was. He had a loose amalgam of wraps and shawls, scarves, malas, waistbands and bandoliers that he called his robes. His hair was long and grey and worn in a bundled top-knot that was held together by a couple of artfully placed twigs. He had large bone rings in his earlobes, that hung nearly to his shoulders, a twisted staﬀ to support his gammy leg and a small scruﬀy dog, of uncertain ancestry, called Tingri, who was his constant companion. He was very respectful of and friendly with the Buddha and there seemed to be occasional reference make to them being of the same family, or lineage, or of some type of close relatedness. But mostly, who or what he was exactly was a mystery.  The anxious group of deva friends now clustered close to him, all pressed together in the leafy bower that was Maggot and Krishna’s den. He had repeated what the village children had told him a couple of times now as they had been joined by Rhamba and Salassa.  ‘Do you think she was stolen by the fair-folk, the rukkha-devas? They sometimes take human children who they deem aNractive or valuable for some reason,’ Salassa asked.  ‘I don’t think so,’ Dusaka replied, ‘if the fair-folk take a child they usually leave a changeling in its place. There was no fairy child left to substitute for Tambaka.’  ‘Do you think it might have been Asa and Saddha,’ asked Bee, ‘The devas who were caught up in that plot to drain kinnari blood and use it as a drug. They got hold of my sister Minti and nearly killed her. They might be aiming to spite Maggot, as she helped to catch them.’ ‘Unlikely, they were condemned to smell terrible for a long time and that stench would still be here.’ ‘Well,’ began Gumbiya, ‘it deﬁnitely weren’t any yakkhas. That baby would ’ardly make a marﬀul and... oof! What?’ Bee had jabbed him sharply in the ribs and ﬂashed a glare that would have shrivelled a young oak-tree.  ‘Oh, right. Sorry, bad taste to say any think like that. Pardon,’ he blushed with the realization of what that mental image would have been like for Maggot. Abashed he mumbled, ‘I’ll keep quiet.’  ‘Do you think it could have been the kumbhandas, the pot-bellied imps from the underworld?’ Ant suggested.

22 
‘That’s quite possible,’ Ninka looked thoughtful. ‘They are greedy and impulsive too. They might have snatched her away just on a whim and simply to spite the family.’ As the conversation rolled around, exploring various possibilities, Maggot looked forlorn and dejected, head drooping. Tingri sniﬀed the path and made some meaningful yipping comments that only Dusaka seemed to understand.  It was mid-morning by then and, as the sun began to bring some serious heat to the forest again, Krishna appeared in a ﬂurry, his monks robes fallen awry from his rushing. ‘Has she been found?’ He began but a moment’s sight of the miserable expressions of all gathered there told him the answer.  ‘I...’ he was about to oﬀer some more apologies and excuses but Maggot, emerging from her saddened funk, stopped him short.  ‘I have had it with you, this place and your dishonesty. If you’re going to be a monk then do it properly. I’m ﬁnished with you and this deception. I know what we’ve been doing has been wrong and now I’m going to ﬁnd my baby.  ‘Goodbye!’    ==*==

23 
CHAPTER FOUR: ‘TO FULFIL DESIRE IS HAPPINESS’  elp me!’ Krishna stood forlornly in the track, looking at Dusaka and crying out both to him and to the universe in general.  ‘Help you?’ Dusaka raised a bristling eyebrow, ‘By that you must mean you are asking me to help find your child.’ ‘What should I do?’ moaned Krishna, not taking in what his wise friend had just said. ‘You’ve got to help me.’ A silent exchange passed between Dusaka and Tingri – as if debating whether to stay with the kinnaris and oﬀer advice, or to respond to Krishna’s plea – Tingri yipped a couple of comments but before any conclusion was apparent Krishna had taken hold of Dusaka’s arm and was tugging him along the path. A ﬁnal shrug from Dusaka and a snort from Tingri indicated that their discussion was being brought to a close, so they followed along with Krishna’s now urgent strides towards the central part of the monastery.  They reached Krishna’s kuti, in agitated conversation all along the way, and seNled in a shady spot between the stilts holding up the hut, as they had done so often before.  ‘I don’t know what to do! She’s kicked me out so I can’t help to look for our baby – she’s my baby too – why can’t she see that there’s nothing wrong with our love for each other, our relationship?’ ‘Well,’ Dusaka began, ‘she sees that if you want to be a monk, according to the way of life that the Buddha and the other monks lead here, then you can’t be a family man and have a girlfriend, or a kinnari-friend, for that maNer, at the same time. The two are incompatible. If you weren’t a monk it wouldn’t be a problem at all. The fact that you lost the child through being heedless is only the sour cherry on top of that biNer dessert.’ ‘But she’s wrong!’ Krishna warmed to the subject, ‘and you’re wrong too, if you agree with her!’ He darted a look at Dusaka to check his reaction to this accusation. Tingri snorted, clearly not impressed. ‘My friend AriNha the monk, the vulture trainer, he’s very clear about how things like enjoying sensual pleasures that the Buddha sometimes calls “obstructions” are not really obstructive to those who indulge in them. He told me how he said exactly that, to the Master’s face, when he was questioned about his views not long ago.’ ‘And did he say how the Master gave him an earful on account of that? He did not agree with AriNha’s take on things at all. He used the similes of carrying a grass torch against the wind, being dropped into a pit of burning charcoal, waking from a pleasant dream, having borrowed jewels taken away, cuNing down a tree to get the fruit, and others, to show how aNachment to sense pleasure always leads to suﬀering.’

24 
‘Yes, but he had to seem to go against AriNha’s perspective because there were all kinds of other people around too – all those “monkish” types who are convinced that the rules are to be taken literally and followed to the last detail.’ Krishna was glad of the opportunity to expound on how he saw things and what was now the driving force of his spiritual and thus monastic endeavours.  ‘I had this insight, you remember, when I met Gavinna, the old court lady who was burnt in the ﬁre at the palace. She said the way to enlightenment, the great mangala, was to keep remembering “Sooner! More!” This amulet you gave me,’ he ﬁshed it out of the pouch on his belt to show Dusaka that he still kept it close, ‘it provides physical protection from death and injury but it doesn’t help the mind. To keep up the aNitude of “Sooner! More!” is a devotion to life. It’s a rebellion against death and, in the monastery, against the prissy, frigid, rigid and sterile formalism that the narrow-minded like to take refuge in. It’s a devotion to the Goddess of Complete Being, the Mother of Nature, like the Holy Deva Dharani who rose up as a witness and vouched for the BodhisaNva at the time of his enlightenment and transformation into the AnuEara-Samma-Sambuddha. The Master could not have conquered Mara under the Bodhi Tree without her help. Isn’t that so?’ Krishna was warming to his theme. Dusaka raised each of his eyebrows in turn, indicating that he was hearing what Krishna said but without giving any sign that he was in agreement with this particular philosophy. ‘Go on,’ he urged.  ‘So, by welcoming all sensual experience, like living with Maggot and having Tambaka as our child, and living here in the forest close to the great enlightened Master – all this is embodying the spirit of being open to all of life, it’s a devotion to all reality. Death is conquered by saying “Yes!” to all of life. “Sooner! More! Give me all of it. Now!”’ Tingri cleared her nostrils, meaningfully, (she could make her snorts express an eloquent variety of opinions) to which Dusaka nodded sagely, the set of his mouth and silent wagging of his chin indicating to her, ‘I think you’re right.’  ‘So,’ Dusaka started quietly, slowly, ‘What does your friend, young Samiddhi, the monk from Rajagaha, think about all this? Didn’t he have a deva friend too?’  ‘That’s right. That really preNy deva from the Heaven of the Thirty-Three. She’s gorgeous... and yes, they’re close. He brought her to see the Buddha and I was one of the few who could discern her. She’s quite something.’ ‘I wasn’t mentioning her on account of her looks,’ Dusaka scowled, half a wry smile ﬁNing itself into his expression too, ‘it’s possible Samiddhi might have some useful thoughts about how he has handled having a devoted deva friend and how that ﬁts into his monastic life. What d’you think? Let’s go and see him. He’s in a kuti just down the way.’    * * *

25 
 ‘You’re disgusting, Krishna.’ ‘What d’you mean?’ ‘You’re calling your aNitude, this philosophy of yours, something reﬁned and spiritual but you were smirking about how you were ﬂirting with and touching up those women from the town who were visiting Udayin’s kuti the other day. You’re proud of how you positioned yourself in the narrow corridor so they would have to squeeze by you. This is somehow a spiritual thing to you!? I don’t think so.’  ‘They liked it, especially when I gave that one a tweak – she giggled.’ ‘Did it occur to you that women sometimes giggle like that when they are shocked and distressed, and don’t know what to say when they’ve been treated abusively? And what did her husband think?? He was right there too. I saw him, he was practically purple with rage.’ ‘I didn’t realize he had arrived.’ Samiddhi was geNing exasperated with his friend by this point. Krishna, with Dusaka watching from the corner, carried on explaining, doing his best to convince himself as well, as he made the eﬀort to spell out his views to his friends.  ‘It’s not just AriNha who sees things from this broader perspective, by the way, but Udayin too. He’s very knowledgeable and can quote all kinds of scriptures. Like how in the Long Discourses it says: “In as far as this self, being blessed and endowed with the pleasures of the ﬁve senses, indulges in them, then that is when the self realizes the highest Nirvana here and now.” ‘Then, in the stories of the Master’s past lives, the sage Narada says: “The following of desires is happiness; to fulﬁl desire is happiness.” And, in another life, the Buddha’s former wife, Princess Yashodhara (who has now become an enlightened nun, I might add) said:  ‘“Great is the joy of sweet desire: no greater joy than love: Who follow this aEain the bliss of paradise above!”   ‘Not only that but the BodhisaNva himself, in a former life as the King Arindama said, when being preached at by a Paccekabuddha called Sonaka, “I have no need of monkish blessings” since he saw the precious value of sense pleasures. You see – in all these places, in the scriptures themselves, this same life-aﬃrming philosophy is displayed. These sages, the great nun Yashodhara, the Master himself, all were guided by the same principles that I’m commiNed to following.’ He felt pleased indeed at his presentation and how he had managed to remember all those signiﬁcant quotations accurately:– Surely the debate was now closed, wasn’t it?  ‘Krishna,’ Samiddhi scowled, clearly unconvinced by all this, ‘you do realize that all the things you just recited are there in the scriptures as examples of Wrong View – I can’t believe that somehow you missed that! That’s the whole point; people think and say these things when they’ve got it wrong. Even the Master made many mistakes and broke the Precepts during the long long succession of lives before his Supreme Enlightenment. The scriptures even state how he broke all the Precepts –

26 
against killing, stealing, sexual misconduct and using intoxicants – except for the one against lying. A BodhisaNva cannot lie under any circumstances: “A BodhisaNva can not tell a deliberate lie; for, being aNended by deception, intentional false speech violates the reality of things.”’ ‘Yes! That’s what you’re supposed to think; these statements are presented as “Wrong View” but to me that’s a code – it’s a clue that such aNitudes are what the Master’s real message is.’  ‘You’re hopeless,’ Samiddhi shook his head, ‘What kind of monk are you and how do you think your way of life like this is going to be a blessing to others?’ ‘For one thing it encourages the inspiring and enjoyable life-aﬃrming spirit,’ Krishna retorted, ‘but how come you are being so ascetic and rule-bound all of a sudden? Don’t you have a deva girlfriend like me? And didn’t you introduce her to the Buddha yourself? How do you square that with what you’re saying now?’ Dusaka looked up from his long study of the bamboo ﬂooring of the kuti, glancing in Krishna’s direction to see how he would hear Samiddhi’s story. ‘Yes I do have a friend who is a female deva but, when she propositioned me, at the hot springs near Rajagaha, saying: “You have gone forth while young, bhikkhu, a lad with black hair, endowed with the blessings of youth; enjoy sensual pleasures, bhikkhu! In this way you will not abandon what is directly visible to pursue what takes time,’ I was not at all interested in following that path. Instead I brought her to meet the Master who, with great compassion, taught her directly, with insightful instruction on the deceptive nature of identity. When she had fully understood this subtle teaching she replied:        ‘“Having abandoned sense pleasures,       Mindful and clearly comprehending,       One should not pursue a course      That is painful and harmful.”  ‘It’s true I have a deva friend, but she is devoted to the Master’s teaching now and we have a beautiful and wholesome relationship like sister and brother, appropriate to my life as a monk. It is possible, and very liberating I might add, to establish spiritual companionship like this!’ ‘Such a bhikkhu,’ Dusaka chimed in, ‘who has freed the heart from aNachment to sensual pleasures, is called: “One whose banner has been lowered, one whose burden is lowered, who is unfeNered”.’ Krishna felt cornered. ‘These are good points but I’m not worried, even if I’m criticized for unmonklike behaviour. As I quoted, the BodhisaNva said, “I have no need of monkish blessings”’; he was doubling down on the supposed ‘hidden message’ of those refuted teachings.  ‘I’m sure the meaning is really “Don’t be worried if people criticize you or look upon you as unmonkly”.’

27 
‘But if the Master is really not encouraging monkly behaviour, why on earth do you think he bothers to live as a monk – and an impeccable one at that?’ ‘Well, he has to, doesn’t he? Because that’s the way all spiritual teachers have to appear.’ ‘I give up!’ Samiddhi, Dusaka and even Tingri shook their heads and rolled their eyes as one.   * * *   Against the backdrop of the mountains above Kulluta, Maggot sat apart from the group of friends. In deep sadness she sang an ancient lament of the kinnaris:   ‘What maEers it, O breeze, If now has come the spring When I have lost them both The garden and the nest?’  Just as two kinnari lovers were known to have wept for seven hundred years after having been separated, or the kinnari Chanda, whose cries over her wounded husband, who had been the BodhisaNva, reached the throne of Indra himself in the Heaven of the Thirty-Three, Maggot wept from the depths of her heart for her lost child.  The group had formed rapidly, in the forest at Kosambi, and had initially approached one of the rukkha-devas, a guardian of one of the great trees of the Ghositarama forest and asked if she thought one of her kind might have been the being who took Tambaka. This woody Elder, who dwelt in an agèd and gnarly sal tree, gently made it clear that abducting babies of humans or other earth-devas was not something that the tree-spirits could or would do. ‘When we practice what is called “gracious exchanging” it is always done for a good reason and we invariably replace the babe with one of our own precious oﬀspring.  ‘AdmiNedly sometimes a lost or stolen child is kept in the arms of our boughs. If a babe has been carried oﬀ by an eagle, or one of the supanna, they might tend the child in a nest in the upper branches of one of our trees, and even raise it there for many years, as was the case with LiNle Wild Rose, but that is not something we as devas can control.’ Ninka who, as a higher deva, had the greatest intuitive powers of the group aﬃrmed that this sal-spirit was speaking the truth. The sal-deva then departed and the group – comprising Bee, Gumbiya, Maggot, Ant and Ninka, Rhamba and Salassa – came to the conclusion that the kumbhandas were the most likely culprits since it was well-known that these often clumsily-formed beings loved the softness and delicacy of human children, the beauty they were often blessed with, so they had been known to whisk them away to the Underworld, to be raised as their own there

28 
and never leaving a changeling in its place. This is what had brought them north to the mountains and where they now searched for an entry-point to the mysterious Underworld dimension.  Ninka was nervous, as she had been given permission to spend time away from the Heaven of the Thirty-Three and, even though time passed very diﬀerently in the lower and the higher realms (a day and night in the Thirty-Three was one hundred years in the human world) this lower plane was considered coarse and vulgar, it was almost shameful for a deva of the Thirty-Three to abide here. It was regarded as so corrupting and foul that to get to spend any time here at all one needed to have permission and a good reason to be around for more than a few moments. So Ninka worried that she would have her permission revoked and that she would have to go back to the upper realms at any time.  She had discerned that a way into the Underworld was somewhere close by but she needed to calm herself and concentrate in order to get an accurate inner reading.  Maggot sat to one side, quietly singing her lament; Bee and Gumbiya, Rhamba and Salassa were gathering some food and Ant sat nearby, trying not to distract Ninka as she focused. Slowly the image of a huge oak of the Himalayas formed in her vision. The base of this forest giant divided close to the ground and a dark cleft opened there, like a cave-mouth.  ‘I know the place,’ she spoke aloud, almost surprised to hear her own voice. ‘It’s close. Very close.’ ‘What’s close?’ said Bee, landing just beside her, newly returned from their scavenging of the area for edibles of various kinds, to meet their diﬀerent appetites.  ‘The entrance to the Underworld. There is an open portal near the stream, at the base of an old oak only a short distance from here. I had a feeling there was one nearby.’ ‘So, once we’re in there,’ Bee continued, ‘How do we go about looking for Tambaka – traces of smell are all we might have to go on and it could be preNy stinky among the kumbhandas, from what I’ve heard anyway.’ ‘The Underworld is a strange domain,’ Gumbiya chimed in, ‘I’ve bin down there from time to time, on various missions – there was this one time...’ He was about to launch into a favourite war story when a sideways glance from Bee caused him to get back to the subject at hand.  ‘Right... well you can go in one entrance an’, even if you’ve not gone far, by yer own reckoning, you can come out in a completely diﬀerent place, far away like. It’s weird. And time – you can go in there and think it’s just a day then when you come out half your team has retired, or died. Or it can be a minute later on the same day – throws you right oﬀ, it does. Everything’s catawampous, distorted, y’ need ter watch yerself going down there – anyﬁnk can happen.’ ‘Sounds interesting,’ Bee grinned. She and Ant went over to Maggot and handed her some pollen that Bee had collected.

29 
‘Have some of this. We’re all going to eat, then what say we head straight into the Underworld? Ninka’s a good guide, and Gumby’s been in that realm before, so we won’t be completely in the dark – s’cuse the pun.’ Her aNempt at humour didn’t exactly lift Maggot’s spirits but she appreciated the gesture of comfort from her friend. The chance of ﬁnding her lost child was the main thing on her mind so she ate quickly and, without registering much of the taste, she was soon up and ready.  ‘Let’s go.’    * * *   Ninka went ﬁrst, into the dark cleft at the base of the ancient oak. Her eyes were more sensitive so she could eﬀectively see in the manky dimness of the downward winding tunnel. Maggot insisted on being right behind her, Ant, Bee, Salassa and Rhamba followed with Gumbiya bringing up the rear.  Silence reigned, but for their footfalls on the dank earth ﬂoor; down and down and down, the tunnel turned and narrowed, broadened and straightened, according to its own underground logic. Down and down they went.  As the feel of the ground beneath their feet changed from earth and moisture to solid stone a voice in the dark brought them up short: ‘What brings you into the realm of Queen Oghabala and LiNle King Lhak?’ The voice echoed as the tunnel had now opened into, apparently, a vast cavernous space. Slowly lights glowed forth from crevices in the rock walls, revealing the shape of the space and the beings gathered before them, blocking their way.  ‘I asked you a question;’ a huge rotund ﬁgure at the centre of the group of kumbhandas stepped forward. Gumbiya instinctively moved to the front of the group of searchers and, baNle-ready, expanded to his full warrior-yakkha size. He was still considerably smaller than the being who had addressed them.  ‘And, in case you were wondering, I am the LiNle King.’ ‘Why’s he called “LiNle”?’ wondered Ant, in a whisper that came out louder than she had expected, ‘He’s huge!’ ‘Wait ’til you see Her Majesty,’ one of the LiNle King’s aNendants countered, also a bit louder than he had intended.  ‘We seek a lost child – one who is half-kinnari and half-human.’ Maggot spoke up in a clear and penetrating voice, ‘A stolen child, perhaps.’ ‘A half-human child that got lost down here would have wandered far indeed. How old is this missing child?’ The King asked, somewhat guardedly, ‘Is it a bold youth or a spritely maiden?’ ‘No – a babe but a few Moons old.’ ‘One that age could not have ventured into the Underworld unaided,’ a scowl formed on the crags of the LiNle King’s face.  ‘◆SO◆YOU◆ACCUSE◆THE◆KUMBHANDA◆OF◆KIDNAPPING?◆’ A thunderous voice rumbled from the depths of the cavern. The LiNle King and his retinue stepped aside and revealed a throne against the back wall of the

30 
echoing stone chamber. A magniﬁcently ugly being ﬁlled the stone throne’s embrace; she was at least three times the size of the LiNle King.  ‘WE ARE INSULTED BY THIS ACCUSATION OF THEFT AND BY THE IDEA THAT SOME SCRAWNY CHILD OF A FLOWER-SPIRIT, WITH NO FLESH ON THEIR BONES, WOULD BE OF INTEREST TO THE KUMBHANDA, TO STEAL AND TO CALL THEIR OWN. WE ARE DISPLEASED. YOU SHALL BE HELD IN OUR CAVERNS UNTIL SUCH TIME AS OUR PLEASURE CHANGES.’  ‘What does that mean?’ asked Bee.  ‘It means you will entertain Her Chthonic Majesty’s pleasure in her dungeons.’ ‘“Entertain” – do we sing and dance for Her?’ Ant wondered. ‘He means “detain” – we are to be locked up for the foreseeable future,’ explained Rhamba, ‘no dancing or singing.’ Gumbiya stepped forward, protesting, ‘’Ang on a minute, I’m a warrior-yakkha of King Kuvera’s old guard...’ ‘◆ AND ◆ I ◆ AM ◆ THE ◆ QUEEN ◆ OF ◆ THE ◆ UNDERWORLD ◆’ Her voice rang with currents of boiling magma, the thunder of tectonic plates grinding beneath the oceans.  ‘What right have you got to imprison us?’ Ninka bravely spoke up. ‘And how long do you intend to keep us? Some of us are devas of higher realms, like you.’ ‘◆ IN ◆ THIS ◆ DOMAIN ◆ WORM ◆ MY ◆ WORD ◆ IS ◆ LAW ◆ MY ◆ WILL ◆ IS ◆ YOUR ◆ REALITY ◆ YOUR ◆ SHALLOW ◆ NOTIONS ◆ OF ◆ SPACE ◆ AND ◆ TIME ◆ DO ◆ NOT ◆ HOLD ◆ SWAY ◆ HERE ◆ GET ◆ THEM ◆ OUT ◆ OF ◆ MY ◆ SIGHT◆’  ==*==
