CHAPTER 5: THE UNINSPIRING MONK  o, what did you make of all that?’ Krishna had returned to his kuti after the communal meal and had invited his friend Surama, an Elder amongst the community of monks, to come along and continue the conversation they had begun, once the Master had departed, after he had given some forthright instruction to the community.  ‘Well,’ responded Krishna, ‘I had hoped to ask him if he could use his powers to tell me where Tambaka had disappeared to but, since he was mainly addressing Udayin about how misguided he had been with those brahmin visitors, I didn’t feel it was the right time. He even laid down a new, serious rule about not coming into contact with the opposite sex, with lustful intent, and he gave Udayin the full “Mogha purisa!” treatment – “Oh foolish man!” If I had asked him about Tambaka he would almost certainly have enquired if she was a relative, or how she was known to me; then what would I say? It wouldn’t be a good subject to raise in public.’ ‘Good point,’ Surama replied, ‘you were best oﬀ keeping a low proﬁle.’ He saw that Krishna was looking fraught and unseNled so he continued, ‘You know that I agree with you about how the Buddha has to keep up a front for the sake of the public, and that in its essence the teaching he espouses is a lot more broad and accommodating. ‘In his usual way of speaking, about the Middle Way, he says how happiness cannot come from pursuing sense pleasure as you will always want more. When the thrill is gone you just desire something else to replace it. That is one extreme.  ‘Then the other extreme is to try to supress all desires, to annihilate them, but that is doomed to failure as life keeps producing more things to want. No maNer how hard we try.  ‘He says the Middle Way is “to let go of desire” but I agree with you that that’s just the version for the masses. Really, how could that work? If I want something, if I need something and I “let go of the desire” there is still something missing, as far as I can see – you didn’t get the thing you needed and wanted, so surely you are still lacking that – no?’ ‘Absolutely, that just how I see it,’ said Krishna.  ‘So,’ Surama continued, ‘I feel it’s our duty as living beings to fulﬁl as many of those needs and wishes as possible, to honour the potential of our lives, and then to let go of aNachment to the pleasure we feel. This is the advanced path that the Master is encouraging but that he can’t speak about publicly as it would look like he was an indulgent and less-than-admirable teacher. We should not use the scanty virtue and meagre wisdom of the narrow-minded to measure the vast sagacity and compassion of the Buddha!

32 
‘After all,’ Surama continued, ‘Look at the example of how he led his half-brother Nanda to full enlightenment. Nanda had gone forth as a monk when he was on the way to his wedding, his faith in the Master having been roused to a high degree that day. Later on the mental image of his betrothed, Janapada Kalyani, brushing her hair aside and saying, “Come back soon, prince!” was frequently in his mind. The Buddha heard about this and called Nanda to him.  ‘“Nanda, I hear that you are missing your ﬁancée and that you are unhappy in the monastic life – is that so?” ‘“Yes, Venerable Sir,” he replied. “In fact I would like to return to the household life and go through with my marriage to my belovèd.”  ‘Then the Master took him by the arm and, through exercising his psychic powers, conveyed them both to the Tavatimsa Heaven – the abode of the Thirty-Three Gods. There the Master conducted him to the Nandana Grove where they saw crowds of apsaras, nymphs with feet as delicate as doves, waiting upon Lord Indra, the ruler of that heavenly kingdom.  ‘The Buddha then said to Nanda, “Who is more beautiful, Nanda, more perfect in form, more pleasing to behold, Janapada Kalyani or these ﬁve hundred nymphs with feet as soft as the breasts of doves?” To this Nanda immediately replied, “There is no comparison, Venerable Sir! The woman to whom I was betrothed has not a fraction, not half of a fraction, of the perfection of form and loveliness of these nymphs.” ‘The Buddha then continued, “Nanda, if you commit to the monk’s life and take delight in it, I will guarantee you gaining the company of ﬁve hundred apsaras, just like these.” ‘“If that’s the case,” said Nanda, “then I will indeed take great delight in pursuing the spiritual path under your guidance.” The Master then conveyed them both back from the Tavatimsa Heaven to Jeta’s Grove and Nanda renewed his eﬀorts with great vigour.  ‘When the, if I might say, prissy and narrow-minded monks heard about the agreement struck between the Master and his half-brother Nanda they criticized him for being a hireling and a sell-out, shocked at the idea of puNing in eﬀort in meditation in order to possess the crowd of nymphs that the Buddha had promised. To me the promise of a Buddha is a powerful thing, of no small consequence, so those shallow monks should not have been so swift to condemn. Anyway, before long Nanda did indeed realize full and complete enlightenment and then went to declare this to the Master.  ‘The public records of that exchange have Nanda saying, “Venerable Sir, I release you from that promise,” and the Buddha responding, “When I knew you were enlightened that promise was dissolved automatically.” However, I reckon,’ and here Surama looked from side to side, as if checking for eavesdroppers and also for dramatic eﬀect, ‘I reckon this was another of those “dressed up for consumption by the masses” accounts. If we’re right about the Master’s advanced teaching on fulﬁlling desire being happiness – and I’m sure we are – privately his half-brother is

33 
currently enjoying the company of that crowd of beauties in his spare time. What d’you think?’  * * *   Krishna had parted company with Dusaka earlier in the day. The Elder seemed to be intent on travelling further with Tingri, although he had made no indication as to which direction he was headed. Krishna was therefore free to spend the afternoon talking with Surama. It pleased him greatly to have such a supportive friend who was senior in the community, and one who didn’t give him any grief, like Samiddhi and Dusaka had done.  By the late afternoon – they had managed to chat for several hours – Krishna was thoroughly conﬁrmed in his understanding that the superior, protective mangala was the maintenance of the aNitude: ‘Sooner! More!’ when it came to sensual experience. This was the great aﬃrmation of life and reality; and to each and every doubt that he had had, or that others, like Maggot and Samiddhi, had voiced, his friend Surama easily explained how their understanding was faulty and their logic was impaired and inconsistent. The story of Nanda and the nymphs was just one of a number of thoroughly convincing examples he gave.  It also became clearer, in Krishna’s mind, how for advanced students who were ready for the higher teaching (that allowed and rejoiced in maximum sensual pleasure), part of the discipline, if not most of it, was ‘Don’t get caught’ rather than the preliminary teaching of ‘Don’t do’. On account of the general view that monks and nuns were supposed to be celibate renunciants it was necessary to be discreet; if you were an advanced student then you were free to do as you liked as long as you didn’t ﬂaunt things and thereby upset the narrow-minded and simplistic followers. As long as you were not geNing aNached to any sort of pleasure, then the energetic and joyful engagement in all the delights available was a fulﬁlment of that ‘best way to evade the King of Death, to blindfold Mara’ that the old seeress Gavinna had spoken of.  Krishna treasured that advice, but he still kept the amulet that warded oﬀ physical death and injury as well:– There was no harm in being doubly careful.  ‘Now that you’re a bhikkhu,’ Surama began, ‘and seeing that you haven’t got much to hold you here in Kosambi anymore, why don’t you come with me to Kitagiri?’ ‘Where’s that?’ Krishna asked, ‘and what makes that a good place to go? I may have had a spat with my kinnari companion but I know lots of others here and it would be a shame to just take oﬀ. Besides, the Master is staying here and, you never know, I might be able to have a private conversation with him and then I could ask about Tambaka and who stole her away.’ ‘Didn’t you hear?’  ‘What?’

34 
‘As the Master was leaving the hall, after having given Udayin an earful, Ananda asked him about seNing oﬀ on the morrow.’ ‘Oh, I missed that, I must have been wrapped up in my thoughts.’ ‘It sounds like they will be leaving if not tomorrow then very soon after. So, what do you think, shall we take to the road as well?’ Even as Surama was speaking Krishna’s mind began to ponder:– Well, why not? What’s to keep me here now and, besides, it’s been a couple of years since I trod the paths and forest trails of a wanderer. After all the struggle with Maggot, and considering no one seems to want my help in ﬁnding our baby, why not? It’ll be good to be out on the road once more, to feel the wind on my skin and the sun in my face.   * * *   The Master did indeed leave the next day, right after the mealtime, with a substantial group of monks walking with him. All those remaining monastics, together with the devotees and well-wishers in the Kosambi area, paid their respects after asking for forgiveness for any irritation they had caused him or for any negative actions by body, speech or mind, intentional or unintentional, that had caused any diﬃculty whatsoever.  Krishna noticed his heart rate increase a liNle in the communal ceremony, but, even as he recited the words, he reassured himself he had done nothing at all blameworthy. Any lingering doubts were not allowed a voice.  He was now free to make his plans with Surama. He knew him fairly well but he was hard to pin down as to his background and other personal qualities. He was quite senior, so as a junior monk Krishna could be oﬃcially apprenticed to him, in ‘dependence’, as it was called, but exactly what his original language was or his family history – the conversation always seemed to drift away from those details when the subject came up.  Kitagiri was east of Kosambi so they took a diﬀerent route to the Buddha and the great group travelling with him to SavaNhi. Krishna had been surprised at the heartfelt pangs of separation he felt as they packed up their bowls and robes and he said goodbye to his companions of the last two years and to the Gositarama forest. So many memories: the sight of the now-empty nest, where he and his dear Maggot had spent such sweet hours, and where the egg had rested almost glowing with its ethereal opal colours, and when it hatched and beautiful liNle Tambaka had appeared, tawny-skinned and her coppery hair, and as ﬂower-fragrant as her mother. ‘Jambu-Sirivanna... I never used Maggot’s real name... “Radiant beauty of the Jambu tree.”’ His eyes moistened, heart aching at the loss, but it was now time to move on.  Once away from Kosambi and heading towards the rising sun of the next morning, Krishna felt a surge of energy welling up within him. The dust of the footpath under his feet, the new types of tree and birdcalls as they covered the

35 
leagues, the roof of branches and the starlit sky overhead at night – he was astonished how much he had forgoNen about life on the road, how inspiring and invigorating it was.  ‘You know, Surama, this reminds me of how I spent more than a year working in a knacker’s yard in Kulluta. I had not realized how stuck I had got in the place; I forgot that other valleys, other hills and towns existed.’ ‘I wouldn’t say that the Ghositarama is quite as bad as a slaughterhouse,’ Surama quipped, ‘but I know how oppressive any place can be, when time for a change has come.’ The town of Kitagiri lay in the country of Kasi, to the north of Varanasi and on the road through there to SavaNhi. As they drew closer to it, and their arrival was imminent, Krishna’s stride naturally lengthened, making it necessary for the shorter Surama to scurry to keep up, to his great dissatisfaction. Every now and again Krishna would recollect that he should not be geNing ahead of the Elder so he would pause and apologize profusely. As he was only twenty, much younger than Surama, the over-exuberance was forgiven.  Along the way Surama had often mentioned how the people of Kitagiri, the lay-folk as well as the monks, were very amenable to the advanced, reﬁned philosophies of the Buddha. When they sat in the evening, at their various forest camps, Surama would expound on the excellent leadership and teaching of the two great Elders there, Assaji and Punabbasuka, meanwhile making sure that Krishna knew, ‘This great Elder Assaji is very diﬀerent from his stuﬀy namesake – who is very pleased to let anyone and everyone know he was one of the original Five Disciples’.  His clear and sonorous voice, replete with reason and good stories, described how they had nurtured Kitagiri as a haven of the higher teachings both for monastics and lay people too. Krishna loved to hear his eloquent descriptions, as they mingled with the sounds of the forest night – the ringing of crickets and cicadas, the calls of night-birds and the hoot of owls – so when they ﬁnally stepped onto the dusty main street of the town, Krishna felt he knew the place already.  The locals called out friendly greetings and Surama called back, chaNing in a most casual and familiar way. As they walked towards the monastery they came upon a group of children playing hopscotch on a paNern of yantras marked upon the ground.  ‘Come and play, sirs!’ The youngsters called out. To Krishna’s surprise, rather than Surama declining, as any Elder, or indeed any monk or nun, would do in Kosambi, he said, ‘What fun, let’s see if I remember the sequence of right and left correctly.’ And oﬀ he went, quite expertly in fact, completing the hops and steps onto the paNerned squares without making any mistakes.  The children were impressed and beckoned Krishna to join in too but, since he had not tried this game since he was a lad of seven rains, he demurred and said he was too big and clumsy:– His Elder had already shown how to do it best.

36 
‘That’s something diﬀerent!’ Krishna chuckled as they continued to the monastery.  ‘Oh, just you wait and see.’ Surama gave him a sideways glance and a substantial smirk crossed his visage.   * * *   Krishna was astonished. They did everything here: the monks grew and picked ﬂowers and made garlands for all the womenfolk of the town – wives and daughters, female slaves, everyone. They would sit and share food with women, often from the same dish; they would snuggle up close, even lie on the same couch with women or laymen; they would eat at any time they fancied during the day or night; they happily drank alcohol; they would sing and dance, play music, as well as engage in all kinds of games with the children; they practiced archery, rode horses and elephants; they would wrestle and box sometimes; and would even lay out their robes as a stage and say to the dancing girls, ‘Here, sister! Dance for us!’ Krishna was greatly impressed by it all as it ﬁNed so well with his own understanding of the Buddha’s teaching. Surama had prepared him thoroughly and the two presiding Elders, Assaji and Punabbasuka, welcomed Krishna with open arms. Everyone here seemed very liberal and no one found fault with his deep dark skin. ‘Anything goes’ seemed to be the ethic of the whole town and Krishna ﬁNed in perfectly:– Now we’re talking!  He decided to stay and did so for the next fourteen years.   * * *   After those fourteen years had gone by it so happened that a monk by the name of Parisuddhi set out from Varanasi, the capital of Kasi, on the bank of the River Ganga, to make his way to SavaNhi, to spend time with the Lord Buddha. His path led him along the road that ran through Kitagiri. He was a very innocent person by nature (hence the name he’d been given, meaning ‘Purity’) so the stories that circulated in the area close to Kitagiri did not register with him as anything of signiﬁcance if they registered at all.  He had camped in a forest glade half a league or so before the edge of the town, and he made his way through the streets after dawn, when folks were up and about and preparing their ﬁrst meal of the day. This was a familiar and time-honoured paNern that he had followed for all the many years of his monastic life.  To Parisuddhi’s surprise, rather than being oﬀered any alms-food, he was heckled and abused by many townspeople, saying such as:  ‘Who is this idiot, a fool amongst fools, a simpleton even to other simpletons. Who would give alms to such a one?!’

37 
Parisuddhi had, as he always did, made sure that his robes were on neatly, his bowl was half-concealed by his robe as the Vinaya discipline dictated. His eyes were properly downcast, looking only a plough’s length ahead, and he was walking at an even pace. He wondered what was wrong, ‘Is my upper robe stained without me knowing it? Is my lower robe falling down? What am I doing that is so worthy of this torrent of abuse?’ Someone called out, as if in answer to his unvoiced question, ‘Our own masters Assaji and Punabbasuka and their followers are friendly and chaNy, always smiling and are the ﬁrst to speak. It’s monks like them that are worthy of alms, not a stuck-up dummy like you.’ He patiently carried on walking through Kitagiri but received no alms-food at all until a certain layman came up to him and asked if he had been given anything as yet.  ‘No, nothing by way of alms-food but I’ve been given plenty of mockery and heckles.’ ‘Please,’ the man urged, ‘come to my place.’ He took Parisuddhi there and oﬀered a full meal to him. ‘I’m honoured that you have come to my house, Venerable Sir, my name is Sudhamma. Can I ask where you are headed, because I’m sure you won’t want to stay around here.’ ‘I am travelling to SavaNhi, my friend, in order to visit the Blessèd One, the Buddha.’ ‘Then let me ask you to bow down at the feet of the Blessèd One and tell him, in my name, that for years now Kitagiri has been thoroughly polluted. The followers of Assaji and Punabbasuka are shameless and self-indulgent. They engage in all sorts of practices that are contrary to the life of a true seeker of the spiritual path and the discipline established by the Master. Please ask him to send some worthy and responsible Elders to re-establish good conduct here. Those who had faith in the Master have seen that faith eroded and those who go about in his name are not following his True Path.’  Parisuddhi was moved by this polite, heartfelt request and it helped make sense of how he had been treated in the town. It had not been because of his sloppy or stained robes, or immodest conduct, but quite the reverse; his restraint and composed demeanour had been the cause for criticism owing to the long-standing eﬀects of the aNitudes and behaviour of Assaji and Punabbasuka, and their group. He would gladly carry this message of Sudhamma to the Master, to do what he could to restore wholesomeness to the town.   ==*==

38 
CHAPTER 6:  THE ENCOUNTER AT THE WELL   ife in Kulluta had become increasingly stressful and precarious over the last fifteen years. Sugandhi had been blissfully happy for the first year, after Harita was born, and even Mata-ji had been reasonably benign, but when her little girl reached the completion of one Rains the broad hints and out-right demands – ‘Now it’s time for a son!’ – began to mount. It was a mild irritation at first, and Sugandhi was otherwise very content with her new life, away from the gruesome duties and stench of her father’s slaughtering yard.  In time however, year by year, the stress mounted and was openly talked about as ‘the family problem’. No more babies came – girl or boy – no maNer what lengths they went in choosing auspicious days, fertility potions, spells by local shamans and seers of both genders. It didn’t help that, at least to Sugandhi’s eyes, her daughter might not have inherited her suspected father’s blue-black skin but the set of her features, her ink-black hair and the big dark purple eyes bore a striking resemblance to Krishna’s. Thankfully no-one else seemed to have made note of this but the growing resemblance was a worry.  When Harita turned six Rains the momentum of concern, as repeatedly voiced by Mata-ji, led to a new marriage being arranged for Jayanta. ‘Since you haven’t been able to give my son any more children, other than this girl,’ Mata-ji managing to both sneer and scowl simultaneously, ‘we have arranged that my son will take another bride.’ There was no discussion between Sugandhi and Jayanta on this, his parents had decided.  It had always puzzled Sugandhi why so many women only wanted sons and seemed biased against their own sex. She adored her girl and, now that she was six Rains, she was a friendly and helpful companion around the house and shared the village duties with the other womenfolk, both young and old.  The new bride was found, coming from a family of many children and, this time from the same caste as the Headman and Mata-ji, even a liNle bit more upscale in the social hierarchy. She seemed a nice enough girl, and when Jayanta eventually got round to talking with Sugandhi about it, he was quite apologetic and asked for her understanding about how diﬀerent things would be. She was touched by this gesture as it was plain he had had no choice in the maNer – Mata-ji had pressed the issue and her husband was also keen for a grandson from Jayanta. Their other children were all daughters, who now lived in their new family homes, three of them away and only one who still resided in Kulluta. Jayanta was also keen to reassure her that she was not being pushed away. As he said these words they had the tone of a prepared speech he was trying to

39 
remember. Sugandhi, who was quick on the uptake, suspected that along with wanting a grandson, Jayanta’s parents were also probably keen to maintain the ﬁnancial connection with her family, her father having the prestigious, and lucrative, role of being the oﬃcial slaughterer of royal horses – those too old or too injured to serve the royal household – as well as looking after the nurturing of foals and the brood-mares. The summer pastures up the top of the valley, beyond the Rohtang Pass, were under his protection and were valuable grazing during the dry summer months.  The wedding of Jayanta and Ratana duly took place but, to the chagrin of the Headman and Mata-ji, and the frustration of the young couple, no children were forthcoming from this union at all.   * * *   Another four Rains came and went and, on the unreliable but ancient principle of ‘third time lucky’, a third wife was ordained to be the solution to the ‘big family problem’. Their other children, the various daughters near and far, had produced oﬀspring – so they had some grand-daughters and grandsons – but Jayanta was seen as special. He was the heir of the family and the last one bearing the family name:– He has to produce a son! Was the now-obsessively held imperative.  Ratana duly stepped into the background, along with Sugandhi, and the arrangements were made for a new marriage. Bride number three, Sukha, was brought to the home, after extensive astrological research had been made, to match her perfectly, in blissful and fecund matrimony, with Jayanta – now well over thirty Rains and still sonless.  Yantras were drawn, mantras recited, holy beings were bestowed with oﬀerings and delicious food, gods were propitiated, seers and sages worshipped, all to multiply the benevolent auspices and to guarantee a boy-child. To no avail. Another ﬁve years passed but Sukha also remained childless. By this time Mata-ji was beside herself with grief and disappointment. She had often been a diﬃcult character to deal with and now she was lashing out in all directions. Sugandhi and her ‘brat’ (although Harita was by now sixteen Rains and no longer a child) and Ratana were barked at daily, Mata-ji having given up on any possibility that either of them would conceive, while she encouraged Sukha to engage in ever-more elaborate rituals that were demanding and uncomfortable, and to take potions and possets that would ‘quicken her womb’ as she would say.    * * *  Things came to a head with the death of Ahimsa, Sugandhi’s father. Her mother, Karuna, had passed away a few years before and Ahimsa had been running

40 
the yard by himself, with whatever help of the candala community he could muster, which was sporadic and meagre.  The ageing of his body and the extra burden of work took its toll and one day, as he was sharpening some knives, he simply said, ‘I don’t feel quite right,’ and keeled over then and there, by the grinding wheel, his heart having ﬁnally given out.  Once he was gone, and his funeral rites completed by the candala community, Sugandhi’s suspicions about her place in the family proved to be true. Almost as soon as the funeral was over, and before the burning pyre had cooled, Mata-ji rounded on Sugandhi and Harita. ‘You two do not belong here anymore! You are never going to bear my son a child – considering the age you are, you are an old woman, like me.’ (This was a liNle unfair as Sugandhi was thirty-seven Rains at this point, hardly a crone). Mata-ji continued, ‘I’ve had enough of you low caste types around this house – you’re useless to my son and useless to my husband and me too. You’re both just dead weight. I want you and your garish scarlet hair, and your oh-so-preNy daughter, out of this house before the next full moon.’  She was also increasingly suspicious of the fact that her son had failed to produce any children other than the girl who was born so soon after his ﬁrst marriage. She refused to think of her son as infertile but, if the girl was pregnant by someone else when they married, that might explain some things:– If Jayanta isn’t the father of the brat, then these impure vermin belong here even less.  In a way it was a relief, as Sugandhi had been wondering what would happen when her father died. She knew the responsibility for the royal horses would be passed on to an older man, skilled in the ﬁeld of caring for the old horses and in despatching them when necessary, and it would not remain in her family. So it had only ever been a maNer of time. Someone from the palace stables and stockyards would be sent and that would end the family’s connection with that lofty role, albeit necessary for a low-caste person to occupy it.  As Sugandhi combed out her ﬁve-coloured hair, still rich and thick, and varying from russet, chestnut, auburn and ginger to ﬂaming orange – its own natural redness assisted by regular applications of madayantika, she mentally readied herself for the road, pondering the years here at the Headman’s house. She had always been an outsider, having had to be on her best behaviour and needing to suppress her natural cheekiness. She had played the demure wife as best she could and Jayanta had always treated her decently. She had had sixteen years to bring up her girl, now on the brink of womanhood; this home had provided food to eat, shelter, and the clothes she wore – although many of those had been paid for by her father’s income – so she, at heart, felt grateful and was now ready for the test of the unknown. She would set oﬀ with Harita on the morrow, after saying goodbye to everyone, then they would see what the universe had to oﬀer.    * * *  Krishna was now well-accustomed to life in Kitagiri. After more than a dozen years there he was part of the furniture, known to everyone, and was reckoned as a

41 
good sort who was always ready to join in with the games with the village children, picking ﬂowers and making garlands, and happily rollicking along with the singing and dancing with the local men and womenfolk. At the beginning it had been great fun, after the narrower conﬁnes of the life expected of him at the Ghositarama, even as a novice. He felt like he could truly break out of his shell here and develop his spiritual life, his journey to enlightenment, according to his life-aﬃrmative principles.  It had been great fun at ﬁrst but now that he’d been here almost ﬁfteen Rains he had to acknowledge some misgivings about the way Assaji and Punabbasuka had led things, and the kind of behaviours they endorsed and rejoiced in. It had been enjoyable to pick up a bow and arrow, and to practice archery again, but when he and some of his fellow monks shot animals and birds, just as targets and not even to eat them, he felt a quavering in his heart. It had been fun to listen to music and to ﬂirt and dance with the local girls, but he saw some of his brother monks force themselves on to people’s wives, daughters and betrothed ones, and press them into embraces and intimacies when the women were not so willing. Even unaNached young girls were propositioned by some monks and, more embarrassingly, he had found himself a few times, after having had a drink or two, either overdoing it with one of the young wives on the couch or casually teasing, and then dismissing, one of the women who had plainly got strong feelings for him. ‘Even if I am not aNaching (hopefully) to the pleasure I feel, it doesn’t go just one way. Those birds we shot wanted to live. We were seeing them solely as a target to aim at but their pain and fear was very real to them. These towns-women we ﬂirt and play around with, we monks might enjoy it but those feelings, of being taken advantage of, that some of the womenfolk have had and then the way we persuade them not to be aNached and uptight – that’s not honest or noble, even though we might be able to justify it somehow, philosophically.  ‘To play with some young girl’s feelings too; let her know you ﬁnd her pleasing and then to just ignore her. It might be a spiritual exercise for us, but for some of them there are a lot of tears, and there was even that young wife who hanged herself because the monk she was in love with made it clear, even though he had played around with her, that he did not really care for her beyond occasional entertainment. That was cruel.’ These kinds of thoughts were coming into Krishna’s mind more and more often, though he did what he could to supress them or distract himself from them. They were on his mind again as he was in the town one day and watching some younger monks racing around the town square, playing at piggy-back ﬁghting with some of the young wives, with a crowd gathered and cheering the antics on.  ‘I used to enjoy things like this,’ he pondered, ‘but, honestly, this sort of activity makes sense for children but what’s it got to do with enlightenment, really?’ He was geNing up to depart when he saw a steady stream of people heading down the main thoroughfare of the town, all moving towards the northern gateway. He

42 
found himself joining the ﬂow and, as he drew close, asked, ‘What is it? Why is everyone going to the north gate?’ ‘It’s the monks,’ was the ﬁrst, not very informative reply. He drew closer to the edge of the town and saw that a large assembly of monks were indeed arriving, walking silently and with gravitas. At their head were SaripuNa and Maha-Moggallana, with Ananda and many other well-respected Elders following closely behind, including other enlightened bhikkhus. The serenity and peacefulness of their arrival hit Krishna like a hammer. As he was now one of the more senior Kitagiri monks present, he stepped forward and knelt in the dust of the street. He saw the group had paused and so he bowed three times, head to the ground.  ‘Venerable Sirs, I am honoured to greet you. What brings such a large and august assembly of the Blessèd One’s disciples to this humble town?’ SaripuNa, ever the dialectician, replied, ‘This town may be humble in its physical proportions, however we have been reliably informed that the Sangha here has been behaving in a far from humble manner for a very long time. Please ask the Sangha to convene at the monastery as soon as possible. This assembly, which has travelled from SavaNhi, will request accommodation there. If that is not possible, or not agreeable, we will camp in the wild forest. Either way please inform the Elders Assaji and Punabbasuka that we wish to hold a formal assembly tomorrow.’  * * *  ‘That meeting was intense!’ declared Krishna, now walking beside Ananda as they made their way north. They had traversed the hills that gave Kitagiri its name, had passed Alavi, and were heading towards the River Sarayu.  ‘There was a certain tension, I agree, but for enlightened ones like SaripuNa and Moggallana, they are aware when there is an awkward mood, such as was present at Kitagiri, and they take steps to cater for it, but it is not burdensome or personally stressful for them.’ Ananda had a wry smile on his face, as he often did, ‘but for the less-than-fully-enlightened, like you and me, it was intense, and I was concerned.’  ‘They were so cool though, in presenting the Master’s message and stipulations – SaripuNa with his ﬂawless logic and Moggallana with his penetrating gaze and quiet yet daunting authority – I was very impressed.’ Krishna, although by now thirty-six Rains in age and ﬁfteen Rains as a monk, easily reverted to his youthful exuberant manner when with Ananda; his company seemed to draw it out of Krishna, taking him back to his early days in the Ghositarama.  The message from the layman Sudhamma had been relayed to the Buddha in the Jetavana by Parisuddhi. The Master had immediately summoned SaripuNa and Moggallana and asked them to travel to Kitagiri with a substantial number of other wise and experienced Elders, in order to establish the verity (or not) of the stories and, if accurate, then to carry out an ‘Act of Banishment’, a serious formal

43 
procedure, compelling all those Sangha members who were followers of Assaji and Punabbasuka to leave Kitagiri forthwith.  That Act, when carried out, had unsurprisingly met with all kinds of protest and reaction from the over-liberal, unrestrained and unruly monks of the area. In short, most refused to mend their ways, they did not ask for forgiveness but rather abused the monks from SavaNhi and argued with them. Many went away and left the Order, a few declared they would stay on with their avowed masters Assaji and Punabbasuka, but a few saw the virtue and goodwill of the great Elders and accepted their Act. Krishna was one of these laNer so he had been glad to be banished from Kitagiri, to start anew.   * * *   It was getting close to the end of the day, the ‘hour of cow-dust’ as it is called, and the group of travelling monks drew close to the edge of a village. There was an inviting forest, with towering trees rising at its heart, a little to the east and already they could hear the distinctive sounds of the roosting calls of many of the local birds as they settled in the high branches for the night.  ‘I will go to the village,’ said Ananda, ‘my throat is very dry – probably from talking with you, Krishna, so much – and my water gourd is empty. I will see if I can find a well close to the edge of where the houses are so I won’t have to wander its lanes at this time of the day.’ As was often the case, this village had a well dedicated for wanderers and outcastes, candalas and matangas, that was situated at the edge of the inhabited area. Ananda got directions from a farmer with his small wooden plough on his shoulder, returning from his fields at the end of the day. ‘Jes’ over there, zur,’ he pointed down the track. ‘Y’ can’t miss it.’ Krishna tagged along, mostly because he liked Ananda’s company and because they had been talking for a while up to this point. As they approached the well Krishna saw that there was a young woman there who had just raised a bucket of water and had it perched on the rim of the well-head. Ananda approached her and asked, in his usual gentle and friendly way, ‘Young lady, would you be kind enough to give me some of your water?’  One glance at Ananda was sufficient to tell her he was of the warrior-noble caste. He was wearing robes but, in her limited knowledge of caste laws, and remembering the punishments she had received in the past for disobeying their rigid stipulations, she was taken aback and a little frightened that she was being tested and could be beaten, or worse.  ‘Sir, Oi can’t possibly give y’ any of this water. Oi’ve touched it. Oi’m a candala, a matanga. Oi’ve worked at the most filthy and ’orrid tasks. Y’re a kshatriya, a warrior noble, Oi can see that just from your looks, y’r way of talkin’. If y’ drink of this water it will surely pollute y’ and Oi would be punished sternly for tha’.’

44 
Ananda was moved by the politeness and sincere kindness of the young woman. He replied, ‘I did not ask you for your caste but for water.’  He walked over and opened the top of his gourd so that she could pour the water carefully in, although her hand was shaking a little. This was as much from her heart leaping with rapturous joy at being treated so respectfully and kindly, as it was from caution not to spill anything. She could scarcely believe how matter-of-fact this great being was in his demonstration of friendliness.  She offered to fill Krishna’s water gourd as well, which he gratefully accepted. Since he was so dark-skinned, unlike the complexion of Ananda, she felt a little less intimidated by him and asked, ‘So, what does your manner of dressin’ mean, and where are you all a-goin’?’ ‘These are the patched ochre robes that are worn by the disciples of the Lord Buddha, the Enlightened One. Have you heard of him? He’s also known by some as the Samana Gotama,’ Krishna explained.  ‘As for where we are going – we are with a large group of other monks, who are camping in the forest outside this village, and we are all heading for the Jetavana Monastery, near Savatthi. That’s where the Master is residing at the moment.’ The young woman was suddenly struck by an outrageous thought, ignited mostly by her new-sprung adoration of Ananda. ‘Can Oi come with yez?!’ She pleaded, her big eyes glancing up cautiously into their faces, hardly daring to hope. She felt magnetically drawn to Ananda and longed to be close to him always.  ‘Don’t you have a family here? Can you just walk off like that without letting your loved ones know where you are?’ The caring heart of Ananda never took such things for granted.  ‘No. Oi don’ ’ave any family ’ere. Oi’m a lone traveller these days. Oi go from place and find whatever job Oi can that is open to being done by a candala woman. Oi ’ave a small bundle that is at the place where Oi stay at the moment – the kitchen shed where they store the dried cow-dung for fuel – but ’part from tha’ Oi just have what Oi’m standin’ up in.’ She pointed to the somewhat threadbare sari she was dressed in. ‘Can Oi?’ She implored, ‘Come with yez? If it won’t break any caste laws. Oi won’ be a problem, Oi promise, and Oi can ’elp with chores, like finding firewood or cow-dung for fuel along the way; and Oi can sew, if y’r allowed to touch what’s come from moy ’and.’ Her eyes moistened with hope and fear that these gentle men might say no.  ‘What would you do when we get to Savatthi?’ asked Ananda.  ‘To be honest, Oi’m so impressed by ’ow kind and polite you both are, Oi’d like to meet y’r teacher and learn what Oi could from ’im. So, if that’s possible, Oi’d like to stay at this Jetavana, or som’ers nearby, and help out where Oi can. Oi know Oi’m a lone woman, and young and Oi’m an outcaste, but Oi might be able to be of some use there.’ ‘One of the Master’s wisest disciples,’ Krishna reassured her, ‘who lived in Kosambi when I knew her, is a lay-woman who was a slave and is nearly as dark-skinned as me. She has been more than useful, to many many people, so I’m sure a

45 
place could be found for you with her. That woman’s name is Khujjuttara; by the way, what’s yours?’ ‘Oi’ve gone by many names over the last li’le whoile; mostly Oi’m just known as ‘Matanga’, which is what they shout when they want me to empty the out’ouse pit or find more cow-dung. My mother passed on a year ago so, since she died, Oi’ve jes’ been Matanga, which is fine by me.’  * * *  ‘’Ow long d’y’ fink we’ve bin ’ere?’ Gumbiya asked nobody in particular.  ‘I thought you were the expert in this territory,’ sniffed Bee, by now even she was beginning to despair of ever leaving those dungeons. All seven of them seemed to be being held close together but it was so dark and the sounds of their voices so variable it was hard to know if they were in separate cells or all together, and where the walls and the door were, as everything physical and temporal seemed very fluid and unpredictable.  Sometimes they seemed to be shackled and chained to the wall, or the floor or ceiling; other times they could move freely for a few paces, then they were paralysed again. They never saw any kumbhanda guards but somehow food and drink, suited to their various needs, appeared. Bee tried counting the number of such meals they had had to try and keep some track of time, but then her mind would temporarily lose its sense of number, ‘Is seven bigger or smaller than four?’ she asked one time, quite seriously at that moment, and was roundly teased for it. She joined in the laughs with the others – it was a brief interlude of humour amidst the grimness – but when all had gone quiet she still wondered, ‘But, which is bigger?’ After she stopped trying to track the time, her numeracy came back again – this was a very mysterious prison they were in.   * * *  Some indeterminate time later Ninka remarked, ‘Is it just me, or is it getting brighter in here?’ ‘It’s not just you,’ said Maggot, ‘I can see all of us, and it’s definitely getting clearer.’ They were all stirring by now, from their various reveries and meditations, and looking at where the light was growing strongest. ‘I bet it’s Dusaka, pulling some clever trick,’ Ant offered.  ‘Or it could be an obhasa-dhara of the Buddha, come to give us some teachings,’ suggested Ninka.  The glow increased and a form began to crystallize within it. The seven each had a different impression of what was taking shape in the shimmer and glint of the brightening. At last it took shape as a male celestial, which Rhamba and Salassa recognized immediately. ‘It’s a gandharva!’ They declared excitedly. ‘I don’t

46 
recognize his face but he’s one of us – how did he get in here and how come he can light the place up so gloriously? This is most excellent!’ A few moments passed and this visitation finally took distinct form. ‘My friends, it’s so good to meet you. Honoured fellow beings of the gandharva’s Eastern Realm, Lady Ninka, your Serenity,’ he bowed particularly to her, ‘noble yakkha of the Northern Quarter, honoured kinnaris all – at last I have found you! Please allow me to introduce myself, my name is Muñca, I am gandharva of the Eastern Quarter, a subject of King Dhatarattha, majestic and divine ruler of that domain, as are Rhamba and Salassa here, although I am more of a musician than a dancing apsara.’ He chuckled:– He was even making a joke in this dire prison they were locked in! He looked at each of them in turn and, unhurriedly, began a short speech. ‘You are all surprised to see me, I can tell. I, like many others, were impressed and inspired by the group of you doing such a sterling job of fending off that war between the devas and the asuras over that trivial issue of the invigorating perfume, amatagandha. To be honest I became something of a fan, from a distance, and always asked for news of any of you. I heard about Krishna and Jambu-sirivanna or Maggot as you call her, having a child and I also heard that Lady Ninka and Sugandha-kanavera, Ant,’ he smiled graciously at them, ‘had grown close, as had the warrior-yakkha Gumbiya and Maha-paduma-sundari, or Bee, as you’re affectionately known.’  He smiled warmly at all of them, then continued. ‘When the child of Maggot and Krishna went missing, I did not hear of it straight away but when I did, and then when I asked about you all, it seemed you had gone to hunt for her and were last seen in Uttarakuru in the foothills of the mountains then – boof! – you were all gone, disappeared from view. No one heard a word or saw a sign, in any realm. I’ve been looking for you all for three sun-turnings, in the human reckoning, although a lot less than that in gandharva time.’ He went on to explain how he had found them owing to certain abilities with concentration and the Divine Ear. He was glad that the prisoners all talked together as that gave his psychic hearing, enhanced with his musician’s training over long years, the chance to pinpoint them, even in the fluctuating spaces and timescales of the Underworld.  ‘I also have the ability to carry beings to different realms so, hopefully, I will be able to get you all out of here without having to wait upon Her Chthonic Majesty’s Pleasure. ‘Yes!’ Maggot shouted, ‘Yes, right away! I don’t know what spells we are held by or what enforces our confinement here but yes! We must try. From what you say my child is three Rains now and I haven’t seen or held her since just after she was born. We have to try!’ ‘All of you, please gather around as close as you can and take hold of my arms or my body. Lady Ninka, if you would take my right hand; Miss Maggot my left; off we go.

47 
* * *  The apsaras Rhamba and Salassa cried out in unison as they recognized their home in the Gandharva Realm. ‘We made it!’ Salassa burst into spontaneous dance, whirling his sister Rhamba with him. The seven had all made it through to the Gandharva Realm, which was part of the Heaven of the Four Guardian Deities.  ‘Thank you, Muñca! Thank you from the bottom of my heart!’ Maggot shed a few tears of joy. ‘Now let’s see if we can find my girl.’   ==*==

CHAPTER 7:  MATANGA’S DEVOTION    ’y think they will le’ me travel with you?’ Matanga anxiously asked. ‘Oi’ll walk far behoind and not cross nobody’s shadow nor touch no food Oi’m not supposted to.’ She glanced up from having tried to keep her eyes downcast, as was expected of the lower menials, and for a moment looked directly at Ananda’s face, meeting his gaze.  ‘Once again, friend,’ Ananda began, ‘for those who follow the Way of the Buddha, concerns of caste and rank are put aside. For lay disciples, when they are in their own homes, they will observe the customs and duties of their position in society – like King Pasenadi and Queen Mallika of Kosala – but when they are visiting a monastery, or in the company of nuns or monks, they conduct themselves as humble lay-devotees, upasika and upasaka, and will respectfully converse with and share the food of all other disciples, whether nobles or farmers or candalas like you.’ ‘Is ’at true?!’ Matanga was amazed – firstly that Ananda had called her ‘friend’ but even more at this strange philosophy. ‘If the teaching of this Buddha can bring about such a change in people, such a diff’rent way of bein’, I definitely wan’ ter meet ’im. So does this mean Oi can come with you? With all of you, Oi mean.’ Matanga’s face flushed a little at having let it slip that she mostly wanted just to be close to Ananda but, her complexion having been sun-darkened by so much work in the outdoors and from rough living generally, it was hardly discernible; besides, Ananda was used to being attractive to people as he was so genial and easy to get along with, he was also a handsome man although by now well into his middle years.  ‘Yes, I think you can come with us,’ Ananda reassured her. ‘There are about sixty monks in our group but there are also a few laywomen and laymen walking with us to help with any needs along the way. You should be able to join with them but I will check with the other members of the Sangha, just so that people will know who you are and out of politeness to the group.’  ‘Thank you! Thank you!’ Matanga stopped trying to look at the ground and smiled up at Ananda, her eyes locking onto his. ‘You are truly the koindest person Oi’ve ever met – thank you again! Oi’ll just go and collect moy bundle and Oi’ll find you all at the forest, and Oi’ll ’ope that no one objects to me comin’ along.’   * * *  To her great relief there were no complaints about the idea of her joining the troupe of lay disciples and she soon got into the rhythm of the road. They left the village, which sat between the River Gomati and the River Sarayu, after the Sangha had walked through it for alms in the early morning. She was glad to be on the road

49 
again, and in this utterly different mode – now there was little if any fear of attack by wild animals or bandits or by men who wanted to take advantage of her, all of these having been a daily concern during her travels. It was blissful, to be passing through forests and fields, to feel secure for once, and to have such noble company.  She tried to get as close to Ananda as she could, whenever it seemed appropriate, but since he was one of the Great Elders he was often near the front of the group, with Sariputta and Moggallana.  Krishna, with the other couple of Kitagiri monks who had opted to travel to Savatthi – rather than to disrobe or stay on as holdouts at Kitagiri – was usually at the very back of the assembly. So, again, whenever they stopped or whenever there was a time of conversation as they walked, and it seemed allowable, she would chat with him. The darkness of his skin had a natural effect of putting her at ease as often candalas and low caste folks were as dark, or nearly so, as him.  During one such time of conversation, after they had all forded the Sarayu River and were drying out the robes and gear that got sodden during the crossing, Krishna sat down close to the group of lay followers so she asked him, ‘’Ow come you and the other Kitagiri monks are always walkin’ at the back?’ ‘We’re in the doghouse,’ Krishna gave her a smile, ‘we’re the badly behaved ones and we broke a lot of the rules while we were there. So we’re on probation for a bit, which means we become like the most junior for a while, until we can be reinstated – do you know what that means?’ ‘Oi may be a country girl but Oi’m not completely ignorant. It means you get back to where you was afore.’  ‘Quite right,’ Krishna smiled at her warmly. ‘I liked a lot of the way we lived in Kitagiri but I now see we took it too far sometimes and we sank into ways of acting that were harmful. There were a few really bad seeds and they seemed to spread their rot to the whole bag of us. I still felt comfy with the basic attitude we had but it all went too far – what I like to call “life affirmation” became “selfish indulgence”. You start out with what feels like having some innocent fun but then it drifts; somehow you keep making excuses to yourself and you end up losing the Path altogether. So, I recognized that, and I’m off to meet the Master, hopefully to rehabilitate myself, and to get onto the proper Path once more. You know what “rehabilitate” means?’ ‘Same as t’other word,’ Matanga snipped, with half a smile on her young face too.  ‘So,’ a tad chastened for his patronizing manner, Krishna changed the subject, ‘are you originally from round here, and have you really got no family in the world? You said your mother died not long ago. What about her family or your father’s?’ ‘Oi’m from nearby, ’round ‘ere,’ Matanga began, ‘but the family said it was me bringing trouble on the rest of ’em. See, was my birthdate, that was what was wrong, ’ccordin’ to them. The family was strugglin’ and they blamed me as the cause of the blight. Moy ma protected me and spoke up, wouldn’t let my dad and the aunts and uncles harass me, but soon as she was gone – “Out you go!” Oi was

50 
chased away like a goat that’s had all the bad luck of the village tied to it, driven out into the wilds. Oi’ve been on moy own since then.’ Krishna was impressed at how well Matanga had fared, considering how young she was and how badly the family had treated her.  ‘What about you?’ Matanga asked, ‘Where you come from? You’re very dark but you speak wi’ a posh accent – tha’s odd.’ Krishna was about to launch into his whole family history but decided to keep it simple. ‘I’m from Ujjeni originally, way to the south-west of here. I’m very dark but my parents were rich merchants and fair-skinned. I had a proper education before I left the household life, I can even read and write, believe it or not.’ Matanga was impressed by this, never before having known anyone who was literate.  ‘Oi never knew anyone who could read, ’cept maybe some of the priests who did ritu’ls in the village. Do you ever get mistaken for low caste, loike me?’ ‘Like I said, in the Sangha, caste is not an issue but before I joined the Buddha’s Order it happened a lot when I was on the road; people wouldn’t believe I was from the merchant caste. Actually both my sisters married up and were queens of Vamsa.’ ‘Now y’re pullin’ moy leg! Queens? What, both of ’em?’ ‘Yes, they both married King Udena but they didn’t recognize each other; they got separated when they were young and… it’s a long story.’ ‘Oi like to be taken for a different caste sometimes – not a matanga for a change – and sometimes Oi’ve tried it on, y’know pretended to be a worker or a merchant girl when Oi’ve come to a new place. But somehow people know. Even though Oi can smarten up if Oi’ve got moy ’ands on a newish sari, an’ ’ad a good wash ’n’ bin out of the sun for a whoile. Oi can look quoite toidy. They always know though; some’ow they figure it ou’ quick and then – what a beatin’ y’ get! Oi was bruised all over an’ moy ears was ringin’ fer a week one time Oi troid it on. Thought Oi ’ad some broken bones. Oi shouldn’t ’ave dun it; Oi i’n’t a very convincin’ liar.’ ‘Is that why you go by the name Matanga?’ ‘Aye, that’s roight. Keeps it simple, ’n’ reminds me to be honest and ’umble, which is the best protection – don’ y’ reckon?’  * * *  The journey to Savatthi had been a delight for Krishna; to feel the open spaces of the hills and the rich life of the forests, with their many animals and bird calls, to have the company of quiet and friendly monks after the raucous friends he had been with in Kitagiri, was all deeply refreshing. He had not left the environs of Kitagiri since he had arrived, fifteen years before, so the contrast of the wild places, the smells of strange flowers, the changing landscapes all had a liberating effect.

51 
They were now close to the Jetavana; just one or two more days and they’d be there, where he would hopefully be able to work out his period of probation and formal ostracism, and perhaps be able to have a private conversation with the Buddha:– Since he was so friendly with Ananda now, perhaps he could pull some strings and arrange it. Matanga had been well-received into the group of lay disciples. Her caste was no issue and her natural kindness and humble manner endeared her to everyone. She was very hopeful that she would find some sort of place helping the community of Jetavana and perhaps be able to listen to the words of the Buddha himself too. In the meantime, as she was accepted more and more fully into the community of lay supporters, she was able to get closer to Ananda and she managed, by the end of the journey, to be spending time in his company every day.  Ananda’s conduct was beyond reproach, he was known as a Non-returner (one level below full enlightenment and therefore incapable of lustful conduct), and before his ordination as a bhikkhu he had never married or been a womanizer.  He was born on the same day as the Buddha and he had already reached the age of thirty-seven Rains before taking on robes. He was thus not criticized for spending so much time in the company of Matanga, who had indeed ‘tidied up’ well, but a few of the monks and lay-folk took it that she was over-attached to the Great Elder and tutted at the way she would look at him sometimes with big mooncalf eyes.  Ananda was not oblivious to her attentions – she was just one of the dozen layfolk travelling with them and she seemed to spend hours talking with him every day – but he felt the contact was essentially harmless and she seemed to have a great natural appreciation for the Dharma teachings as well as his company.  When they arrived at the Jetavana the first order of business was to pay respects to the Master and to report to him the events that had unfolded in Kitagiri. He received this news with a grave expression on his face, tinged somehow with a wry smile beneath it, as if bemused by the capacity of people to get lost in their own versions of reality.  On hearing how many of the monks had refused to accept admonishments, had argued and prevaricated, and had snubbed the proceedings, denying the validity of the properly performed Sangha legal procedure, he laid down a new weighty rule, to encourage honesty, respectfulness and trainability. Of the forty or so monks residing at Kitagiri about thirty had left the area and disrobed, either returning to family life or taking up other forms of wandering monkhood. Three had come to the Jetavana to ask for rehabilitation, while the remaining six had decided to ignore the Act of Banishment and had stayed with Assaji and Punabbasuka, as diehards determined to justify their unskilful ways.  Once all this was completed and the Sangha had dispersed, to her surprise, Ananda called Matanga forward from the back of the hall, where she had been sitting with the other lay people, to join him before the Buddha.

52 
‘Venerable Master,’ Ananda indicated Matanga sitting politely beside him, ‘this lay-woman has something she would like to ask of you. She travelled with us from the region of Northern Kasi and has proven herself to be interested in the teachings and to be of much practical help along the way.’  Ananda was then a little taken aback when, rather than Matanga simply saying that she would like to be helpful to the community of Jetavana and to live close by, she blurted out, ‘Master! Please ’elp me and let me live close to Ananda, so that Oi may see ’im and be the one to look after ’im. Oi love Ananda, Master, please let me be with ’im.’  It seemed that along the road from Kasi, she had picked up a substantial portion of Krishna’s views about the compatibility of romantic love and monastic life. He had mentioned one day how he had fathered a child, while he was still a novice, and that though his partner was (supposedly) a kinnari, he felt that there would be nothing wrong with a romantic partnership of this kind between a monk and a lay-woman, especially with such a pure-hearted monk as Ananda – the rebukes from the Great Elders who had come to Kitagiri had sobered him a bit but he still, in his heart, held fast to his notions that to fulfil desire was happiness. This small encouragement from Krishna had informed a lot of her heady eagerness to draw close to Ananda, in the hope that her love would be reciprocated.  The Buddha looked into Matanga’s face for a long time before he responded, his eyes filled with compassion and a warm regard. ‘Matanga, your heart is full of love but I discern that you do not understand your own feelings. As I perceive it, it is not Ananda the person that you love but rather his kindness, which is vast, universal and unconfined, non-personal.’ He paused. ‘And you love what his kindness brings forth from within you – is that not so?’ The Master’s gentle gaze seemed to reach into the very core of her being and to fill it with light. ‘Please take to heart the kindness that he has demonstrated towards you and, if you are able, extend that same kindness towards all beings, near and far, both great and small. Can you do that?’  In Matanga’s heart it was as though something that had been sitting just out of place shifted and fell into perfect alignment. An inner brightness blossomed and grew, and she knew that the perspective of the Master was absolutely correct. It was like hearing a completely new song that was somehow intimately familiar at the same time. She thought, ‘Oi never saw that before but Oi knew it all along,’ but she was lost for words. With tears in her eyes she started bowing, as she had learnt to on the journey, over and over again.  ‘Three is enough, Matanga,’ both the Master and Ananda were smiling, ‘We are happy to give you permission to help the Sangha here at the Jetavana, as I perceive that is also your wish.’ The Master paused again, then said, ‘Even though you are a matanga by caste as well as by name, I declare to you that you will be a model, a shining example to those born to the nobility. You are of low caste by birth but brahmins and warrior-nobles will be able to learn lessons from you. If you

53 
follow the path of the Dharma, as you now recognize it, you will outshine the royal glory of queens upon their thrones.’  He lingered again, allowing his words to sink in.  ‘Ananda, please introduce her to Khujjuttara. She can find her a place to stay nearby and show her this monastery and the town.’  * * *  ‘’Xac’ly what’re we doin’ ’ere?’ enquired Gambiya of the group generally but of Muñca in particular. ‘I’m glad you sprung us from the dungeons – that were a neat move – but ’ow do we carry on the search?’ Muñca began, ‘Well, I brought you all here because it was the easiest destination for me, being home territory, but of course it’s up to you all.’ He looked around the group to see if any one of them wished to suggest a plan of action. A breath passed in silence. Bee and Muñca then both spoke at the same moment. After a spell of, ‘Please, what do you propose?’ ‘No, you,’ ‘No, please,’ Muñca said, ‘Miss Paduma, I insist – what is your idea? You have been much closer to all this than I have; you know Miss Jambu, Maggot, the mother, and you know the child.’ ‘I reckon,’ Bee began, ‘Since we did not pick up any leads from the Human Realm, and the kumbhandas in the Underworld were convincingly clear that it had nothing to do with them, we should make a search as best we can of these other realms, especially since we are now here. Perhaps one of the gandharvas, yakkhas or nagas took her. It might be revenge for the amatagandha plot we foiled, and the individual nagas or yakkhas or whoever that got punished, maybe their families have a grudge, and kidnapping Tam is a way to get back at Maggot and thereby the rest of us – maybe we are walking into a trap but if some beings are holding her in this realm I don’t care. We can walk into their ambush and deal with it there and then; I’m not afraid.’ Gumbiya glowed a little with pride at this courageous speech by his partner and grunted, ‘I agree!’ ‘I was going to make the same proposal, as it happens,’ said Muñca. ‘Lady Ninka, you I believe are the most skilled tracker in this group, how do you feel about this idea?’ Ninka, who was as keen to help as any of the others, was also delighted to be able to spend more time with Ant. ‘Yes, I agree as well. Rhamba and Salassa know the Gandharva Realm, as do you. Gumbiya is at home in the Yakkha Realm. We have a dear friend in Queen Samuddaja, of the Naga Realm, and I feel we have already tested the Kumbhanda Realm, where we have few friends and would now be considered fugitives, so I concur that that one has been dealt with already.’ The decision was thus made and, whilst the conversation unfolded, the kinnaris began to fully appreciate the sounds and forms of the land they were now in – an unfamiliar upper realm they had never experienced before.

54 
The air was filled with music, tones and harmonies both exquisite and fully natural. Whispering harps like a breeze in the grass; soft chords on vinas and reedy pipes blown in eerie cadences as if a dance made into sound; endless interchanging melodies, woven in harmony that passed beyond hearing, into the depths and into the heights.  Colours of shimmering hue bent the air, curving it into forms that threaded around and amid the dancers – apsaras like Rhamba and Salassa – whom they realized had been drifting and whirling by even as they clustered and had their urgent discussion. They were in some kind of forest glade, the trees vast and strange in appearance, not of any species the kinnari knew, their trunks laced with gold and silver sheens. At the edge of their vision they spied a mansion or a palace of some kind, this also seeming to be covered in silver, or built of it, with rippling banners flying from the turrets and pinnacles.  Ant, steadily but inexorably distracted from the conversation, managed to summarize her wonderment at the Gandharva Realm. ‘This is impressive. Awesome. Wonderful!’ And that was all she could manage.  Muñca then took the lead, ‘We know many of the beings of this realm and can easily visit them to seek information but, since a number of you are visitors from an, ahem, lower realm, and therefore foreign in the eyes of custom and law, we should seek out King Dhatarattha first and ask his permission to search the realm and make enquiries of his subjects. It’s the way things are done and can’t be avoided.’ ‘We have to see the King!’ Bee was aghast, ‘how long is that going to take to arrange? We’re in a hurry here!’  * * *  Matanga had become well used to adapting to new places quickly. The elder lady Khujjuttara had taken her easily under her wing and it was plain she knew everyone and was admired and respected by all. The fact that she had once been a slave, as Krishna had recounted, mattered not a whit here. This all meant that Matanga found her feet swiftly at the Jetavana and, within a Moon or two, was fully involved in helping out with food and fuel and repairs, and was able to sit in on teachings at the hall very often.  On the full moon nights in particular, many of the citizens of Savatthi and the local villages gathered at the assembly hall, the sala, of the Jetavana in order to recite the teachings together, meditate, and listen to the Dharma discourses of the Master or other Elders. During this day, the full moon having arrived, Khujjuttara came round and reminded Matanga, ‘Don’t you go promising to do any jobs this evening, young miss. It’s the Observance Day and the Master will be sure to offer a teachin’. In fact, come to think of it, let’s you and me go early so that we can get a good place to sit. My eyes are still sharp but these old ears are getting worn out so, even though the Master’s voice is so clear, it’s handy to sit as close as possible.’ Matanga readily

55 
agreed and moved to finish up the repair work she had been doing, fixing up the little shelter they had found her situated not far outside the monastery gates.  The sala was mostly empty when they arrived, just a few monks were there arranging lanterns and candles and tidying up the Dharma seat for the talk they were expecting.  The two women took their places on a mat near the front as, when there was a large crowd of lay devotees, the Dharma seat would be placed in the middle of the hall and the Sangha would sit beside and behind the Buddha; the lay people would then fill the space in front of him.  Khujjuttara had given Matanga her first instruction in meditation and she seemed to take to it like a duck to water, even though she had never had any kind of guidance on this aspect of spiritual life in the past. They sat quietly and the hall slowly filled around them as the rising clarion of the cicadas mounted in the forest, the daylight rapidly dimmed and the fat golden moon laid its first beams along the sala floor.  Later in the evening, when the moon was higher and had shrunk and turned to ice, the Master ascended the Dharma seat and began to speak.  ‘Four asankheyyas and one hundred thousand kalpas ago, I made the vow to be a Bodhisattva at the feet of the Buddha Dipankara. I was a brahmin called Sumedha at that time and I dwelt in the city of Amaravati. Since that day, so long ago, in all the various lives that have been experienced, the Bodhisattva never consciously told a lie.  ‘They who forsake the truth and tell deliberate lies, even though they might sit in the sacred enclosure of the Bodhi Tree, cannot realize Buddhahood. A Bodhisattva and a Buddha, all Arahants too, have to speak the truth. In certain cases a Bodhisattva may deliberately take life, take what is not given, engage in sexual misconduct and consume intoxicating drink or drugs, but they cannot tell a deliberate lie – for any speech or action based upon deception violates the reality of things.’ He let the words hover in the stillness and cast his gaze for a moment around the sala. ‘When giving the novice Rahula, my own son, his preliminary instruction in Dharma, I picked up a water-dipper with just a little water left in it and asked him, “Do you see this little water left in the dipper?”  ‘“Yes, Venerable sir,” he replied. ‘“Even so little is the spiritual virtue of those who are not ashamed to tell a deliberate lie.”  ‘Then I threw away the water that was left and I asked, “Do you see that little water that was thrown away?” ‘“Yes, Venerable Sir,” he replied. ‘“Even so, Rahula, those who are not ashamed to tell a deliberate lie have thrown away their spiritual virtue.”  ‘Then I turned the dipper upside down and asked, “Do you see this dipper turned upside down?”

56 
‘“Yes, Venerable Sir,” he replied.  ‘“Even so, those who are not ashamed to tell a deliberate lie have turned their spiritual virtues upside down.”  ‘Then I turned the dipper right way up again and asked, “Rahula, do you see this hollow empty water vessel?” ‘“Yes, Venerable Sir,” he replied.  ‘Even so, hollow and empty are the spiritual virtues of those who are not ashamed to tell a deliberate lie.’  ‘This is the advice I gave to the novice Rahula, out of compassion and desiring his welfare. For the same reasons, for all those gathered this evening on this full moon Observance Day, I offer this same advice to you.  ‘Lastly, although I have spoken so far of lies in the realm of speech, more insidious and often completely unnoticed, are the underlying tendencies, the anusaya, whereby the mind can lie to itself. The mind can be influenced by: sense-desire; aversion and grudge; opinions and views; skeptical doubt; the conceit of identity; craving for continued existence and defined being; and, lastly, ignorance, not seeing reality clearly. These seven are consummate liars, causing the mind to not see and know the truth of the way things are, and to thereby create suffering and disharmony for the individual and other beings. Be aware of the deception that these seven are able to cause as they can be subtle, insidious, yet deeply harmful.’  The Master paused again and it was clear that this was the completion of the teaching. Matanga’s heart was singing – she had relished every word, it all being so much in accord with her own intuitions about honesty and humility.  ‘Oi’ve got a long way t’ go but this is won’erful.’ And when the assembly all said ‘Sadhu!’ three times, with a single voice, she heard herself almost roaring it:– Yes! This is it, yes! Krishna, meanwhile, was in amongst the large community of bhikkhus, in the shadows between two lanterns. He too was deeply moved by this exhortation but tears were flowing with remorse at just how much lying he seemed to have been doing to himself. ‘I’ve got a long way to go,’ he thought as well, ‘a long long way, but what else is there to do?’    ==*==

57 
CHAPTER 8: KAPILAVATTHU –  THE CITADEL OF THE SAKYANS    ing Pasenadi was feeling torn. He deeply loved his queen, Mallika, but in all their years of marriage she had only borne him one daughter, the Princess Vajiri. She, in turn, he loved very much but she was not a male, and according to the laws and customs of the warrior-noble kshatriyans, only a son born to the anointed king and his queen could inherit the throne of Kosala. If he died without a legitimate son to inherit, it would inevitably fall to bloody disputes between nephews, cousins and boys that had been born from liaisons outside the royal marriage.  One such was Prince Jeta, who was a fine young fellow, and a wealthy and well-regarded personage in Savatthi. He had sold the park he owned outside of town to Anathapindika so that he in turn could gift it to the Sangha. He had not only made a fortune from that but he had had his name enshrined in the minds of the manyfold disciples of the Master since, through no wish of his own, the monastery had become known to one and all as the Jetavana – Jeta’s Grove. If he had been the son of Queen Mallika, all would be simple, however his mother was one of King Pasenadi’s concubines, the kshatriyan Lady Varshika, a minor princess, descended from a fourth son of his great-grandfather. All of this meant he would need to find another wife and to put her on an equal footing with Queen Mallika, and then hope that this new bride would provide him with the wished-for princely son. It was a worry.  Part of what he dreaded was the need to inform Queen Mallika for he wished always to respect her and, publicly as well as he did privately, demonstrate that he held her in the highest regard. She had come from a humble background and had only been the daughter of the chief garland-maker of Savatthi before King Pasenadi chanced to meet her and had fallen immediately in love, both marrying her and anointing her as Queen that very same day. There had been grumblings that she was a commoner but the King had always come to her defence, asserting his power as an absolute monarch to raise her up to His Royal Level.  When he finally brought the issue to her attention he was taken aback by her responding with the words, ‘I was wondering when you would realize that this was going to be a necessity. I’ve been waiting for you to bring it up for quite a while.’ ‘You really are a lot wiser than me,’ the King smiled warmly, ‘I have been chewing on this for the last few years but I didn’t want to risk hurting your feelings.

58 
We have had disputes from time to time but your happiness and well-being are of paramount importance to me. I’m so glad that you seem to find this idea agreeable.’ ‘Well, it’s not ideal but it’s the way things are, so why create grief around it? Have you thought about who the lucky girl might be?’ She raised an eyebrow.  The King reached for some sherbet water and leaned back on his cushions. The weather was getting hot and even the open-sided chamber they were sitting in, high in the palace as it was, did not seem to provide any cooling breezes. He noticed that Her Serene Highness had a distinct smirk etching her visage.  ‘You look like you’ve been not only tolerant of this idea but that you’re ahead of me in having given it some thought.’ He lowered his eyelids a fraction, as if he would be better able to scrutinize her expression that way.  ‘You see through me as though through clearest water, oh Royal Husband,’ Mallika replied. ‘You know how you have grumbled recently – excuse me – have expressed Royal Displeasure at the way that sometimes, when you make an invitation to the Sangha to receive a meal offering here at the palace, you are told that no monks are available to come?’ ‘Yes…’ he was intrigued to see where this might be going, ‘… go on.’  ‘This circumstance has been noticed by quite a few of us and, once or twice, when the palace victualler has bought in large supplies in the expectation of a large group of monks coming to receive a meal here, the excess food has had to be given away to the widows and the needy of the city. The poor folk were, naturally, very happy about this bounty, however it made me think, ‘Why is this happening? The Sangha knows we have deep respect for the Master; the food we offer is certainly nourishing and abundant; we arrange it so all the palace staff are modestly clad when the monks or nuns visit so, what’s the cause?’ She let the mystery hover for a moment, enjoying her husband’s Royal Perplexity. ‘Go on.’ ‘It seems that we have neglected to consider what is the best kind of food.’ She smirked a little again, knowing that the King was inordinately attached to matters of the palate and had once even been put on a diet by the Master. He was mightily proud of the food his kitchens produced.  ‘What do you mean!?’ His patience now beginning to wear thin.  Sensing the Royal Mood, Mallika cut to the chase.  ‘When I went to the Jetavana, a while ago, when you were off at the borders, I asked the Master about this. He responded by asking me, “Great Queen, what do you think is the best kind of food?” ‘I was a bit nonplussed by this question and simply raised my hands in añjali, trusting that an appropriate answer might arise, because it was clear it was not a question about flavours, textures, ingredients or cooking methods. Before any words came to me, the Master answered his own enquiry.  ‘“The food of friendship is the best, Great Queen,” he said, “even sour rice gruel given by a friend becomes sweet.”

59 
‘“In that case,” I asked, “with whom are the members of the Sangha finding such friendship?” ‘“With their kin, and other Sakyan families here in Savatthi. Many of the community at this monastery are from the north, from the Sakyan kingdom that sits below the Himalayan mountains.” ‘This made perfect sense to me, as the words of the Master always do, so the thought then occurred, “How can this Royal House become kin with the Sakyans and thus feel more of a home to them?” It came to me surprisingly quickly:– Perhaps if the King married a Sakyan princess we would then become related to those Sangha members, and if she produced a son then the succession would be secure and great bloodshed and strife averted. What do you think, Your Majesty?’ ‘Brilliant, is what I think!’ And thus plans began to form.  * * *  ‘It’s much cooler here!’ King Pasenadi declared, ‘Savatthi in the hot season is Exhausting, enervating, I am glad indeed that I made the journey of this embassy myself.’ ‘We are deeply honoured, Your Majesty,’ Prince Mahanama replied. He was the head of the Sakyan people and had been scrambling to prepare adequately for this unexpected visit. Riders had come ahead of the Royal Progress, so they had had a couple of weeks’ warning, but it was a challenging task to put it all together and on so many fronts.  It was not just a matter of arranging food and lodgings, fit for a kshatriyan king and his entourage, but there was also a substantial faction of the proud Sakyan nobility, and even among the servants and townsfolk who, having got wind of the impending visit, felt, ‘Why are we making all this fuss and to-do about some Southern royals on a trip – they didn’t even ask permission from us!’ This reticence was by no means universal but it was voiced, unabashedly and liberally. Mahanama was thus tasked with a diplomatic balancing act along with everything else.  ‘Please, Your Majesty let me conduct you to your chambers. I trust they will be adequate to your needs and, I beg you, please do not hesitate to let me know if anything can be improved.’ It might be cooler in Kapilavatthu but Prince Mahanama was now sweating. As they walked through the palace grounds, and seeing he was nervous, King Pasenadi endeavoured to put him at his ease, making small talk and asking about the welfare of Kapilavatthu and the Sakyan kingdom. By the time they reached the chambers that had been prepared for the royal visitor, Prince Mahanama was more relaxed and felt ready to ask the next big question, ‘Er... how long would Your Majesty like to reside here? Of course we would be honoured to provide for and host you and your party for as long as you would wish.’ ‘That, honoured Prince, depends a bit on a question I have for you.
