This is in fact the reason we have come this distance from Savatthi; I have something to ask of

60 
you. Agreed it’s only eight or nine leagues, barely half a moon’s journey, but in this season the elephants and horses get thirsty, as do the people. One has to find shade where one can and the leaves have fallen from many of the trees so it’s been a short road but hard.’ ‘What is your question, Sire, that you have taken such pains to come and ask?’ Even as he said this, Mahanama had an inkling of what was coming next, knowing as he did that Pasenadi had fathered no legitimate heir, as yet. It was a question he was dreading as he knew it would be a contentious issue among the Sakyan nobility and in the marketplace as well.  ‘I would like you to give me one of your daughters in marriage for I wish to bind our houses together. I am, furthermore, in need of a son to inherit the Kingdom of Kosala. If a Sakyan princess became my bride I would raise her up to be Queen, on an equal basis with Queen Mallika, my Royal Wife of many years. In addition, since my kingdom is large and prosperous and Sakya is smaller and less wealthy and since I am making the request of you, and even though it would mean your grandson would become the head-anointed Warrior-noble King of Kosala, I am offering to forgo any dowry. The girl on her own would be enough. What do you say, good prince?’ The Prince was sweating again and somewhat lost for the right words. He couldn’t say, ‘I’m not sure if everyone will agree’ or ‘Let me consider it’ – which were the first things that came to mind. He was thinking quickly and, to his relief, in a flash it all clarified:– I need time. I can’t say no as he’s the King and we are a vassal state. There has to be a way to work it… Oh!…! I think I’ve got it; we’ll need at least one Moon.  ‘Your Majesty, we are deeply honoured that you would call upon the Sakyan people in this way. There will be many things to discuss and prepare. I have a big family, with many daughters, and it is not immediately clear which one would be most suitable to offer to Your Majesty as a bride. May I have the period of one Moon to consult with my fellow princes, my wives and my daughters, and then we will have a suitable girl to present?’ ‘That sounds very agreeable, please take all the time you need and I will enjoy the cool climes of this kingdom and the breezes off the Himalaya.’  * * *  Prince Mahanama had been correct in his assumption that the idea of a Sakyan princess marrying the King of Kosala would not go down well. There was uproar in the court.  ‘We are Adicca-bandhu, Kinsmen of the Sun God, descended from King Mahasammata of the First Age and, after him from King Okkaka – his descendants became the Sakyans and the Koliyans, we never ever take spouses outside these sacred clans!’

61 
‘We’re oak-hearted Sakyans!’ Another of the nobles interjected, ‘How dare this soft Southerner presume to ask for one of our princesses?!’ ‘Will you please listen!’ Mahanama barked, now seriously on edge. He did not have absolute rulership, like King Pasenadi, for Sakya was governed by a form of collaborative leadership. He had to come to agreements with his fellow kshatriyan princes before important decisions were made. ‘Will you please be quiet and hear me out.  ‘Point one: Sakya is a vassal state of Kosala so we cannot refuse the request. ‘Point two: We cannot break the customs of our clan and let a true princess marry outside the Sakyans and the Koliyans.  ‘Point three: The words of His Majesty’s request,’ there were a few haughty snorts at the use of the honorific and some cries of, ‘“Majesty”! Pah!’  Mahanama continued undaunted, ‘... of His Majesty’s request were: “Please give me one of your daughters.” Now I have a lot of daughters and it came to me that, if we schooled one of my, ahem, natural-born daughters in the manners of the court, to a convincing degree, she could be given to Pasenadi, King of Kosala, and I would not have broken my word, and no true princess would be breaking our sacred codes.  ‘Your views on this plan, honoured sirs?’ He cast his gaze around the thirty or forty nobles gathered in the Santhagara Hall for this discussion.  ‘I don’t like it!’ Prince Sinipura jumped in, ‘Even if she was a daughter of one of the non-royals, like your favourite Lady Piya, I feel that would be an almost equal disgrace. I don’t agree.’ There were murmurs of assent to this view all around.  ‘Yes, good point,’ Mahanama continued, ‘that’s exactly why I’m thinking of the daughter of a slave.’ There were more murmurs but quieter, one loudly questioned, bemusedly, ‘Does he keep track of his by-blows?’  ‘Who are you thinking of?’ Prince Sinipura asked.  ‘Some of you may know the slave Nagamunda, who works in the kitchens. She’s a handsome woman, to my eye, and I’m not ashamed to say we have... er... enjoyed congress from time to time over many years. She’s got a daughter – well several actually, and many sons – who I think could play the part well.’ ‘Is she a kitchen girl too?’ ‘As a matter of fact, yes, she is.’ ‘And you think she can shed the smells of the kitchen and pass for a princess in, how long have we got? One Moon? It’ll never work.’ Mahanama let half a smile lighten his face. ‘This is where it gets interesting. Not long ago I was passing some... er... convivial time with her mother, once again, and the daughter, who is now sixteen Rains, was around the chambers too. We fell to playing the lying game – you know, where each person in turn tells a story and you have to guess whether the person is lying or not. I invited a couple of her other daughters to put down their work and join us; I was in my cups at the time and was feeling magnanimous.

62 
‘This one daughter, Vasabha, who is quite good looking too, was astonishing. She could lie with complete conviction and no matter how hard the rest of us tried she always had convincing evidence for what she was saying, she backed up everything she uttered with completely natural ease. She won every time and she could guess the double and triple bluffs of others. I think she’s the solution to our problem.’ Silence hung in the hall for several heartbeats, a rare occurrence for a meeting of Sakyan princes. It was a momentous decision to make.  ‘I like it,’ Prince Sinipura said, nodding his head. ‘Similarly,’ followed Prince Nipuna. Murmurs of agreement were emerging all round. Mahanama let it ripen in people’s minds then spoke up, ‘Any disagree?’ He looked around the hall – no movement or sound showed any dissent at all. ‘Then I take your silence as approval.’  ‘One moment,’ Prince Jali quietly cleared his throat, ‘are you sure that this girl Vasabha is your daughter by Nagamunda? I mean, these matters can be hard to determine at the best of times, and in the most reputable families too.’ ‘Another good point – I have considered this myself and, to be quite frank, I was with her mother a lot in those days and, for our purposes here, I feel it’s likely enough. I have pondered this and I feel comfortable looking King Pasenadi in the eye and saying, “May I introduce my daughter.”’   * * *  When Nagamunda and Vasabha were summoned and told of this plan they each felt a small storm of emotions. The mother, recognizing that this would make for a totally different and wonderful life for her girl, was simultaneously afraid if, somehow, the façade slipped and Vasabha was revealed as a fraud, what would happen then? It could only be disastrous. Her girl could be tortured, maimed for life or executed on the spot. Her heart quailed at the prospect but, being slaves, they had no more say in the matter than the pots they cooked the rice and barley in had about their role in the world.  Vasabha, meanwhile, could only think, ‘Yes! Yes! Now I can get out of the kitchens and the smoke and steam I live with every day. The fires to tend and the pots to clean… Yes!’ At the same time, another more rational part of her mind was thinking, ‘I’ve got a month to learn how to be a princess – they must be jokin’. I’ve never had a hair-do like they have, no idea how to make one. I don’t even know the names for half their clothes, let alone how to put them on; I can tell a good story, and I can imitate voices – I done that, makin’ fun of the high-ups and the snooty girls in town – but the eatin’ customs, the dainty way of walkin’ – how am I...?’ It was baffling. Still, the benefits looked mind-boggling, if she could pull it off.  Vasabha knew as well as her mother that she had no real choice in the matter, being one who was owned as a slave. She also had to notice that the attention she was now getting, from the princes and princesses gathered around her, was thrilling.

63 
When she won at things like the lying game, or on those rare occasions when the palace cook grunted approvingly at her work in the kitchen, that gave her a warm glow but this, this prospect of becoming queen of a huge country like Kosala and the mother of the next in line to the throne – this was a thrill like no other she had known in her whole life.   * * *  That Moon was a cascade of new experiences, many of them painful or at least uncomfortable for Vasabha. Her mother joined in, where she could, to lend a hand with fetching and carrying and offering whatever reassuring noises she could make. The vast bulk of the many aspects of the transformation were looked after by a huddle of the Sakyan court ladies and a cheerful, one-handed maid called Kesini. It was exhausting, exciting and exasperating, but the whirlwind nature of the process meant she had no time to think and would collapse onto her pallet at the end of each day and fall straight into a dreamless sleep; that was until Lady Jantu and Lady Piya decided she needed to learn to sleep on a ‘proper’ bed and to learn the names and protocols, customs of the bedchamber, and she got moved out of her lean-to that she had shared with her mother and siblings for years.  On her first night in the bedchamber it was just before dawn that she fell asleep – she had never slept alone before, always having had a puddle of sisters and small brothers around her – and it was only after Kesini looked in on her late in the second watch of the night, and came to lie on the bed beside her, that she finally could drop off.  The days were filled with, amongst other things, the experience of bathing – and in hot water! A week of such scrubbings and rinsings was scarcely sufficient to rid her skin of the kitchen reek and the dust of charcoal and cinders that seemed embedded everywhere. It was hard work but Lady Jantu eventually, begrudgingly but almost proudly remarked, ‘You know my dear, you actually smell quite sweet once you’ve been uncaked from all the soot. Who gave you that perfume?’  But there was still more to do.   ‘Those hands!’ Lady Jalini declared, ‘Callouses and cuts, burn-marks, we’re going to need a magician to conjure all this away.’ Nevertheless they had the skills and set to work.  Then there was her hair, needing delousing along with copious washes to shed the grease and grime.  And her clothes; all the fittings and the names of things.  And jewellery; she felt like a bangle-tree with all the trinkets and rings, armlets, anklets, browbands, coronets, earrings, pendants, necklaces, navel gems, kamarbands around the waist:– Was there any part of her body they could not contrive to hang filigreed metal from?  Then the names of jewels, precious and semi-precious, and the designations and nicknames of how they were cut.  And the walk, to be dainty and demure.

64 
And the voice; ‘How kind of you to let me come to your court, Your Majesty’ – though to be fair the voice was one of the easiest of her challenges.  And the family history of the Gotama clan, since Mahanama, apparently her father, was a Gotama and the cousin of the Buddha, the most famous of the Sakyans in these times.  And then the knowledge of the basics of the Dharma, since King Pasenadi was a devotee of the Buddha too, she would have to be ready to join in with discussions. She was brilliant at faking stories but she needed to get some essential terminology down too, but not too much, to keep it convincing.  And her nails, which she’d never thought of as having any use but to prise open a nut, or scratch or pick apart the various ingredients of various foods, and to nip head lice. And her toenails too – who would have that that anyone in their right mind would consider their toenails as part of their beauty scheme?  And lastly eating – how to share a plate properly with the nobs and with the King.  There was probably more but, as the crowd of ladies kept saying, ‘Just the essentials, just the essentials’ – this was the bulk of it.  By the time the Moon was complete she hardly recognized herself and, one time, a younger sister of hers came to the chamber and, not recognizing Vasabha in her finery, sparkling with silver and jewels, she asked, ‘’Scuse me y’ Ladyship, d’y’ know where Miss Vasabha is?’ ‘Her Ladyship’ stifled her giggles for a moment and then, striking a pose, said in her poshest accent, ‘Why, foolish child, she is here,’ and grinned, watching her sister’s dawning recognition wash over her grubby face:– This was fun!    * * *  The Presentation was to take place over a formal meal with King Pasenadi. Rumours had circulated that the King had dropped a comment, without malice, that had been overheard by a Sakyan servant who was knowledgeable about the ruse being pulled. Apparently the King had remarked, to one of his own courtiers, ‘These Sakyans are desperately proud, especially in matters of birth. Suppose they should send a girl who was not one of them, not a royally born princess yet say that she was so?’ ‘Sire,’ his companion replied, ‘one sure way to tell is by eating together. If the girl eats from the same dish as her father and he eats with her in the same way, I feel it will be safe to say she is exactly what they say she is. A Sakyan prince would never eat from the same dish as anyone lower in rank.’  The mindful servant hurried to pass this on to his masters. They, in turn, doubled their efforts to keep the King distracted by hunts in the hills, large banquets in the region of the palace away from where the training and decorating of Vasabha was taking place, and elaborate picnics, with music and dancing, as well as more lavish food, in locations both beautiful and conveniently remote.

65 
The day arrived and Vasabha, fully bedecked in her finery, was brought into the dining chambers. Prince Mahanama was there with his senior wives, Princess Bhatta and Princess Maddarupi; King Pasenadi and two of his attending lords were already settled at the far end of the low table. Cushions of bright colours were spread liberally around to sit on and the cool breezes from the Himavant forest and the high mountains rippled the fine draperies. Clusters of servants hovered in attendance, ready to bring in the food once the royal party was all gathered.  Vasabha moved demurely and gently into the space and Mahanama introduced her, ‘Your Majesty, this is my daughter Vasabha, she has been selected from all those vying for the opportunity to be your queen. I trust she pleases you,’ he was sweating profusely and trying hard not to show it.  ‘Enchanted,’ said the King, almost in a whisper, and it was plain to all present that he was enchanted indeed.  Vasabha was just about to ask where she should sit but Mahanama drew her to his side and then let her place herself between him and the King. He then glanced over to the servants and, with a discreet gesture, indicated that the food should be brought in.    * * *   Vasabha had been fully briefed on the deception that was planned. Once the food was in front of them all and had been put onto dishes, they began. Prince Mahanama and Vasabha had a single dish between them, along with the others spread around the table. Mahanama reached across and took some food from that plate then put it in his mouth. In a fluid motion Vasabha followed, almost simultaneously but just after, and also picked some food from the same platter. She popped it in her mouth and, just as Mahanama was reaching for another mouthful, a minister appeared in a harried state and begged leave to speak. The Prince made a point of resting his hand on the dish as Vasabha continued to eat from it.  ‘Your Highness, a messenger has arrived from the ruler of Koliya; the matter requires your immediate attention.’  ‘Really, can’t it wait until we’ve finished eating?’ He endeavoured to sound as convincing as possible and put some genuine fret into it.  ‘Immediate Sire, I regret. The messenger is waiting, and he has a fresh horse saddled and ready to carry your reply.’ ‘Very well,’ he clambered to his feet.  ‘Your Majesty, please excuse me. Needs must.’ ‘Quite understand,’ the King replied with a mouthful of the delicious offerings that had been prepared.  ‘I’ll be back as soon as possible.’ Which of course he wasn’t. He returned in a feigned rush as the dishes were being cleared. ‘I’m so sorry. So rude of me, on such a special occasion; Your Majesty must think of me as the poorest of hosts.’

66 
‘Not at all, not at all. You don’t need tell me how affairs of state can interrupt the best of plans and the most sacred of duties. Will you have some more to eat? You barely started.’ ‘No need, Your Majesty, really. I can have something later. What I’m most concerned about is my daughter Vasabha. Is she acceptable?’ ‘Acceptable!?’ King Pasenadi had been thoroughly charmed and, having seen the Prince and his daughter literally eating from the same dish, any doubts that had been lingering were dispelled. ‘Not just acceptable, honoured King. We have already been discussing possible names my agèd granddam might offer for our son.’  ==*=

67 
CHAPTER 9: A BRIDE OF THE KING   ewildering and bedazzling as the preparations were, Vasabha was determined to stay focused. One slip would be enough to cause disaster; even though she was quick enough to make excuses, there were reasons and there were reasons why she might get something wrong. To use some northern vernacular term, or using the kitchen way of counting by numbering her finger joints, might be something she could get away with, but to pick something up with her left hand, when a kshatriya would never use anything but their right, would bring the edifice of pretence crashing down.  Prince Mahanama was relieved that the palace coffers would not have to provide a dowry which, for a Royal Wedding, could be ruinously expensive. The cost of hosting King Pasenadi and the Kosalan embassy for over a Moon had already drained the resources of himself and many of the noble houses. One aspect he was still concerned about was how to sustain the integrity of the deception over time. A number of nights had passed in fretful sleeplessness, trying to consider who to send with ‘the Princess’ and how secrecy could be maintained in Vasabha’s court, especially if she had the customary crowd of supposed relatives and familiar servants from home around her:– This really is a problem! People talk, and make new friends. How can it all be arranged to prevent the risk of slippage? And how are the Sakyan monks in Savatthi now to be encouraged to accept the invitations they were declining? To his surprise it was in an unguarded moment when, happening to see Vasabha being adjusted in her newest sarong and jeweled kamarband by Kesini, it came to him like a perfectly formed and well-wrapped package. He saw how he could send her with just one reliable servant and yet make it seem like this was a sign of great virtue and faith in the King, and in the security of Vasabha’s prospective new life in Savatthi.  It was the suspicious and supercilious glances that both Lady Jantu and Lady Piya gave Kesini as they helped Vasabha with her accoutrements that had given him the cue. The girl had just one hand, having lost the other in a tragic fire, and moreover she was from Kosambi in Vamsa, to the far south. The high-class women, and even the other servants, tended not to befriend her, this being on the superstitious assumption that she must have done something really evil in a past life to have had her right hand and forearm burned off. The fact she was a foreigner simply made the off-putting quality of this woman more complete.  She had come to Kapilavatthu with an embassy from King Udena, sent to open up more trade opportunities, with her being one of many maids along to serve

68 
the needs of the courtiers and their wives as they travelled. After her belovèd charge Gavinna had passed away she had been at a bit of a loose end in King Udena’s court, and being one-handed didn’t help. The embassy to the north and the chance of a change of scene was like a beacon of possibility and she had jumped at the opportunity. She was a self-reliant person by nature, confident and unflappable in most circumstances, but still (understandably) was reactive in proximity to fire. The memory of Gavinna holding her, as the heat and flames intensified in the burning seraglio in Kosambi’s palace, the tears running down her face from the smoke and the terror of it all, was something that she suspected would be with her all her days. When one of the Kosambi lords had fallen ill in Kapilavatthu, he and his wife had stayed on with Kesini and a few others, as he was too sick to travel by the time the embassy was due to head south again.  Kesini found she was very comfortable in the north and, when his lordship had recovered and the family were preparing to head back to Kosambi, she was quite happy to have had an invitation from Lady Jalini, who was much less superstitious about bad karma than her friends, and who was not so bothered by national pride and the belief that Sakyans and Koliyans were somehow special and sacred.  ‘Please consider joining my household,’ she asked Kesini one day. ‘You are a person of great good qualities, I can see, sprightly and energetic, cheerful, yet you can be assertive when you need to be. And you are conveniently unwed, so your duties would neither be interrupted by children arriving and needing your care, nor by a husband who wished to have your attention. I do not consider your loss of a hand to be a problem,’ she rolled her eyes fractionally as if to comment on the irritating folk-beliefs of her fellow Sakyans. ‘What do you think?’ ‘I will have to ask the master and mistress but I can’t see them objecting,’ she was surprised and delighted at the idea. ‘They’ve got plenty of help for the road to Kosambi and they’ll prob’ly be glad to see the back of me.’ No objections were raised and thus it was that Kesini joined Lady Jalini’s household. She was now the ideal person to help fulfil Mahanama’s plan.   * * *  King Pasenadi had left the practical arrangements concerning the journey back to Savatthi to the various ministers and court advisors. The day before they were due to travel, Prince Mahanama came to see the King and, as best he could, check that all had been agreeable with the Royal Visit and that all the necessities for the procession back to Kosala had been provided.  ‘Splendid, honoured Prince, splendid. It has been a most agreeable sojourn. And I am very pleased indeed with this daughter of yours that you have offered. She’s a fine girl, young, but very poised, I think this will all work marvellously to our mutual benefit.’

69 
Mahanama was relieved to hear this and continued. ‘Sire, as a gesture of our faith in you, and out of respect to the vassal nature of Sakya to Kosala, we would like to commit the Princess Vasabha completely into your hands.’ ‘I thought you were doing that already. Had you been thinking of this as a loan?’ Pasenadi chortled at his own wit. ‘What do you mean by “completely”?’ ‘No Sire,’ Mahanama was now anxious to explain, ‘not a loan or any kind of partial or temporary arrangement – perish the thought! In fact, quite the opposite. We know that often, when a delicately raised princess has to leave her family and move to a new household, commonly never having met or even seen a likeness of her new husband, she will be accompanied by a group of maids and familiar friends, companions she’s grown up with as well as perhaps a cousin or an aunt as well. This is taken to be the best way to alleviate homesickness and to provide a bubble of people, and daily customs and routines, that sustain the taste and textures of home.’ He paused to level his breath for a moment; this was the crucial idea he had to sell. ‘Go on.’ ‘We Sakyans are proud by nature, our womenfolk as much as the men, so we would like to demonstrate our keenness to honour you – and to show our faith in your wisdom and kindness since you are an eminent disciple of the Lord Shakyamuni, my Venerable cousin, the Buddha – by sending Princess Vasabha to you with but a single servant. We will dispense with the need for the Princess to have her own support system in your court as we are completely confident that Your Majesty will provide all the support, spiritual and emotional, as well as material, that she might need.  ‘Just as you magnanimously offered to forgo a dowry for the Princess, we would like to commend her fully into your care. She’s a proud Sakyan and she too is eager to forgo the safety net of family and friends, in order to give herself into the secure embrace of your Royal Protection in as wholehearted a way as possible.’ He finished there and endeavoured to lower his shoulders, breathe out, and relax his drum-tight stomach.  The King looked pensive and took a moment before responding. Despite his efforts to relax, Mahanama’s heart began to race:– Everything depended on this moment – everything! ‘I’m deeply touched,’ the King replied at last. ‘Very touched indeed.’  Mahanama restrained himself from grinning, as he wished to, and managed to confine his rejoicing to a long and steady out-breath:– It was all going to work.   * * *  The grand procession formed at the gates of the palace. Gaily caparisoned horses and elephants, carriages and carts clustered, and were efficiently manoeuvred into formation for the Royal Progress. Final gifts had been exchanged and flowers offered, banners of many hues streamed in the Himalayan winds, billowing and flapping their farewells. The state elephant of Kosala was brought to the centre,

70 
where Prince Mahanama stood with King Pasenadi. The Princess was ushered forth by Princesses Bhatta and Maddarupi. She was radiant, and commanding the attention of all with her remarkable dignity and beauty.  ‘You are stunning, my dear’; the King was aglow with admiration. Mahanama then formally took her hand and placed it in the King’s. ‘I offer her to you, Great King, may she bear you many sons so that the line of Kosala may continue for a hundred generations.’ Loud cheers were raised all round, both from Kosalans and Sakyans alike, and Pasenadi turned to begin the mounting of the elephant – this was going to be harder than usual as he had increased his already substantial girth through all the feasting in Kapilavatthu. After taking one pace he stopped and turned back to Mahanama.  ‘I quite forgot. I’ve been meaning to ask you – who is the Princess’s mother? I never heard that mentioned or, if I did, I failed to register it.’ A bolt of fear shot through both Prince Mahanama and the assembly of his courtiers, wives and concubines. In all the various preparations none of them had given this point any thought. Vasabha too was shocked and reflexively clenched her hands, but to her relief and to that of everyone in the Sakyan Court, in half a blink Princess Maddarupi stepped forward, raised her hands in añjali and, bowing her head respectfully, simply said, ‘Your Majesty.’ She made sure she said no more, not wishing to tell a deliberate lie.  ‘Ah, so you’re the lucky person to be my new mother-in-law. You look younger than me but never mind,’ the King, again highly impressed with his own wit, carried on, ‘We’ll leave it to the pundits to explain how the son can be older than the mother.’ He paused a little and then added, ‘I trust you’re at ease with handing over your precious and beautiful daughter like this. Is it not a pull on the heartstrings to say “farewell”? You might not see her again.’  ‘I count it an honour of the highest order, Your Majesty. I have other daughters and sons who will keep me company, and who have already brought me grandchildren to soothe and warm my heart. I commend Princess Vasabha to you with no regret.’ Maddarupi hoped that the exchange would end there but her heart sank when the King asked directly, ‘Is she your youngest daughter?’ There was no more room to wriggle or to evade, or to be deliberately misunderstood, she had to give a straight and deceiving answer, ‘Yes, Sire, she is my youngest child.’ Mara, the Lord of Lies, watched this scene as it unfolded, as he had been watching and prompting since Queen Mallika had pondered how to draw closer to the Sakyans. A bright idea planted here, a little nudge made there – it did not take much to move the world. He reflected:– Oh, this is good. This is very good. There is going to be havoc.   * * *  Bee was peeved. She could not believe how long it had taken to organize the visit to King Dhatarattha, and all the fuss and palaver involved in the audience itself,

71 
even with a high deva from the Tavatimsa Heaven in their party. ‘And how many colours and how much jewellery can one deva wear?!’ There were so many adornments, let alone the musical accompaniments, that it had been hard to stay focused.  ‘At least the niceties have all been observed and we’re permitted to carry on searching,’ said Ant.  ‘But time!’ Bee objected, ‘time’s passing. Agreed, if Tambaka has been kidnapped by someone in the Bhumma Deva Realm, or here, with the Four Guardian Deities, it’s not so urgent but in the Human Realm time is passing much more quickly; three Rains went by there just when we were trapped by Queen Oghabala in the Underworld. Agreed it was hard to tell anything accurately about time in that domain but still, if we delay too much, and if she’s in the Human Realm, she might be an old woman by the time we find her.’ She didn’t say, ‘If she’s still alive.’ ‘It’s urgent anyway, Bee,’ Maggot quietly stated. ‘She was a tiny baby, my tiny baby, scarcely six Moons old when she was taken. We have to find her soon, whatever age and state, wherever she is.’  They were all still for a moment, taking in Maggot’s aching pain and longing for her lost child, lost in so many ways.  ‘I’m sorry,’ Bee took her hand. ‘I didn’t mean to say it like that.’ Maggot reassured her with a brief smile, squeezed her hand then asked, ‘What now?!’  Muñca spoke up at once, ‘There’s a couple of places in this kingdom where almost every gandharva or apsara passes through at one time or another. They are hubs of communication and meeting – not exactly like a market place or a town square in the Human Realm, but somewhat similar, although most of the communication is through music or dance. I feel those places are the obvious ones to try first.’ ‘Let’s go!’ Bee was eager to get up and moving, the words of Maggot having touched her profoundly.   * * *  The kinnaris, being unfamiliar with this realm, had assumed that the comparisons with ‘town square’ and ‘marketplace’ implied buildings but structures like the King’s palace, and even much more humble abodes, were quite rare. It seemed that apsaras and gandharvas preferred to dwell in sylvan surroundings – forests and glades, floral bowers and by lakes decked with lotus-blossoms and waterlilies. The first such hub was in a park-like open area with glorious bright gardens and tall mysterious trees that rustled musically in the soft winds that ran between their branches. Leaves and trunks were silver and gold and every imaginable shade of green. They could just discern in the far west the distant peaks of the Yugandhara Range that stood at the centre of the four kingdoms of this realm.

72 
‘You kinnaris wait here, with Sergeant Gumbiya; Lady Ninka, please come with us of this realm while we make some enquiries. Your intuitive powers are very keen so you might be able to tell if any of those passing through or abiding here give any sign of lying or dissembling.’ Muñca was very much at home in this realm so they let him lead, for now.  ‘Honoured friends Rhamba and Salassa, Lady Ninka, let us make haste.’ The kinnaris took the opportunity to replenish their food supplies with what pollen they could gather from the celestial flowers. As they searched and collected what they could, they naturally sampled what they accumulated along the way.  ‘This is good stuff,’ Ant and Bee declared simultaneously, discovering that flowers in the higher realms were blessed with many more nutritive and restorative properties than the less exotic blossoms they were used to in the Ghositarama and in the human world generally.  It was a thankfully brief wait before the searchers returned. ‘None of us picked up any hints, unfortunately. All the gandharvas and apsaras we spoke to seemed completely unaware – no word or even a rumour of any human or kinnari babe being carried through and no hint that anyone we spoke to was hiding anything.’ Muñca looked almost mournful in his disappointment. ‘So,’ Bee was already airborne, ‘let’s try the next place.’   * * *  They arrived at a similarly configured park-like grove that was far to the northwest of their first try, this was near to the border with the Yakkha Realm. Muñca guided their arrival to an area at the edge of a thick copse of the great trees, where a large number of gaily-clad gandharvas were making their divine music together on their different instruments. It looked like a good place to begin as there were so many gathered together.  As they reached the ground, Rhamba and Salassa started to approach the assembly of musicians, prepared to interrupt their playing if need be. They had not gone two steps when Maggot called out, ‘WAIT!’ I can smell her! She’s here, or she’s been here recently. It’s her!’ She raced toward the trees and the thick undergrowth beneath them. ‘Tambaka! Tam! Are you here?’ She used all the sounds and phrases that had been the ways she spoke with her baby, the lullabies she sang, in the hope that the babe would recognize her voice. There was no sign of the child.  All of them clambered through the bushes looking for signs until Ant found a little space naturally formed between some of them. ‘I think I can smell something here too.’ Maggot reached her as quickly as she could. The fragrance of her child – so distinctive to her kinnari mother – was strongest here, there was no doubt. Someone must have brought her here, at least it seemed that way.  ‘So what’s this, d’y reckon?’ Gumbiya had been having trouble making space for his bulky body, despite having shrunk down as much as he could. At the edge of the little open space, barely an arm span across, he had spotted a strange colour on

73 
the ground. It was a thick and smooth piece of shell, its convex surface opalescent and shimmering. Maggot reached over and took it from him.  ‘It’s her shell, it’s hers!’ She smiled at Gumbiya gratefully. ‘Thank you! Tam used to like holding this, like a comforting doll or a toy that most babies play with. This was her treasure, she liked to clutch it all the time. Smell it, it’s got her fragrance too.’  ‘What does this mean then?’ Bee wondered. ‘Was it the apsaras or gandharvas who took her?’  Ant was then struck with a bright idea. ‘When we foiled the amatagandha perfume plot, and when Bee gave her evidence at Lord Indra’s court, were any gandharvas or apsaras named?’ ‘No,’ said Bee, ‘I gave them the list of what I saw; the Tavatimsa devas who were the drivers of the whole thing, some nagas, some yakkhas and kumbhandas.’ ‘Were there any gandharvas or apsaras at the trial? Maybe they were cronies of the ones who were caught.’ ‘I really can’t remember. I was at the end of my pollen bag. I think I fainted as soon as my testimony was over.’ ‘Anyway, that doesn’t really matter for now,’ Ant carried on, ‘as I’m thinking that maybe there were some gandharvas or apsaras involved in the plot and they never got caught. Perhaps there has been an agreement to punish us all by stealing Maggot’s baby to wreak revenge on us, that seems most likely to me. Since no gandharvas were known to be involved, they could move around without suspicion; they would not be in the families of those who were punished and imprisoned, so no one would be keeping an eye on them.’ ‘So what does that say about where they might have taken her?’ asked Maggot.  ‘Well, I’m not sure on that; I haven’t got quite that far in my thinking. However,’ Ant was on a roll now, ‘perhaps this was some kind of meeting point that they used and Tambaka was handed over to some others. We’re very near the border of yakkha country,’ she risked a quick glance at Gumby, ‘and if they wanted to hide her away securely, the yakkha community is much more known for its muscle-power and weaponry than the apsaras and gandharvas – no offense meant,’ she glanced at Rhamba, Salassa and Muñca. ‘I still think we should search some more here and ask as many of the folk as we can but I’ve got a strong suspicion that your baby’s been hidden amongst the yakkhas.’ Maggot heard this with a measure of dread as it sounded plausible if not highly likely. Before they made any more moves, though, she wanted to double check.  ‘Ninka, your senses are much more refined than the rest of us. This bush here is the one where the scent is strongest, and here is the piece of shell – can you give me your impression of these? I want to find Tam so badly I don’t fully trust my own senses.’

74 
Ninka took the shell fragment, which was quite thick, and held it up to her nose, inhaling deeply. She said nothing at first and then went to the lower branches of the bush Maggot indicated, bringing the leafy fronds close and sniffing several times. She was silent for a while, looking at Maggot and Ant and around the group – their faces some anxious, some hopeful. She eventually nodded her head, saying, ‘It’s her. There’s no doubt about it.’   * * *  The Royal Progress lasted less than half a Moon and Savatthi was soon reached. It felt like they had been travelling on a golden road, out of the hills. Vasabha’s inner world was one of great excitement and enjoyment mingled with intense concentration and mindfulness of the vast array of new things she had to adapt to. She had certainly had had ‘the essentials’ drilled into her – and she was confident with what she had learned – but every day brought surprises, the first of which had been how to sit in a howdah on elephant-back without lurching from side to side like a rag-doll shaken by an angry child. Still, she had made the whole journey, with Kesini at her side providing quiet prompts, and it had all gone off without an error, as far as she was aware.  Before the wedding, which was due to take place only seven days hence, she was formally introduced to Queen Mallika who was genuinely warm towards her; she in turn brought her only daughter, the Princess Vajiri, forward to meet her.  ‘This is my daughter Vajiri; she is about the same age as you, what, sixteen Rains? I hope the two of you will become good friends. It can be lonely living in a palace, so to have trustworthy and supportive companions is important.’ Vasabha got the sense that the Queen was speaking of her own experience when she had been brought from the garland-makers shop to marry King Pasenadi, some seventeen Rains previously. ‘You are too kind, Your Serene Highness. I’m sure that Princess Vajiri and I will delight in getting to know each other – you are very thoughtful.’  Vajiri was indeed the same age and, it had to be admitted by Vasabha, that the Princess possessed a natural beauty far more refined than hers. She was a doe-eyed, golden-skinned, graceful girl who looked like she had stepped out of an ancient epic – like one of the ones Vasabha had been memorizing, such as Sita, the perfect wife of Rama, or Parvati, the powerful wife of Lord Shiva.  It was a surprise and a delight, once the formalities were over, that she beckoned Vasabha, and Kesini too, to come and sit with her in a small nook that overlooked the gardens. As if to belie her goddess-like beauty, Vajiri turned out to be an easy-going, artless person whose main concern at present was figuring out how not to be married off and sent away from her home.  ‘I’m not the slightest bit interested in marriage, despite the pressure’ and on the word she flared her eyes for dramatic effect, ‘that is being applied to me. I’d much rather stay home and go to the Jetavana and the nuns’ monastery – the

75 
Visakharama – and the Eastern Park Monastery with my mother, and occasionally my father. Who knows how long I’ll be able to get away with it?’ As she heard Princess Vajiri speaking, and recollected how sincerely friendly Queen Mallika had been – and bearing in mind how good Vasabha was at seeing people’s bluffs and pretences – she reflected:– This is looking easier than I thought; these two women, who could easily make my life into a hell realm, seem as sweet as warm ghee mixed with honey. I like it – this whole thing might really work!   * * *  The seven days passed and the grand occasion of the wedding arrived. King Pasenadi had caused the whole city of Savatthi to be decorated and, during the ceremony, he placed Vasabha upon a carefully arranged pile of treasure which he gifted to her and, with the ceremonial sprinkling of lustral waters, he made her his Queen, to rank alongside Queen Mallika. Vasabha was already dear to the King, and belovèd.   ==*==

76 
CHAPTER 10: PRINCE JETA’S GROVE   fter the wedding ceremonies at the Palace, with all the grandeur of the court around her, Vasabha was surprised to hear that they would go to the Jetavana for a smaller, follow-up ceremony as well. The actual wedding was conducted by brahmin priests since the monks and nuns were prohibited from performing marriages by their Rule. This other ceremony was a blessing by the Sangha to wish them well as a couple and to invoke as many benevolent forces as possible – from all around the cakkavala, the universal system – and to encourage the kindly, noble, unselfish aspects of the heart within to be strengthened and to bless their union as well. It was going to be another test of her adaptability and resourcefulness as this was a further item that had not made it on to the list of ‘essentials’.  ‘I’m so glad you’re going to meet the Buddha, Miss,’ Kesini was quite excited. ‘I had the chance to go to his monastery in Kosambi a few times, with Gavinna who I looked after. She was a concubine of King Udena’s grandfather and had been put out to pasture long ago. She was very old and completely blind but she saw everything – if you know what I mean. She could tell exactly what was going on in people’s minds just by the sound and feel of their walk or the tone of their voice. She had visions too that she would tell me about sometimes.  ‘Anyway, when we had the opportunity, and when the Master was in the King’s good books, we’d go to listen to his talks. It was wonderful.’ ‘How would the Buddha not be in the King’s favour?’ Vasabha took care to use her posh voice at all times, even when it was apparently just her and Kesini in her chambers. You never knew when someone might be eavesdropping or just coming up to a doorway, many of which were only equipped with a curtain rather than a wooden door.  ‘Uhh! King Udena blew hot and cold all the time. You never could keep track of his moods. One Moon we’re all to go with him, in full pomp and with armloads of offerings, to pay respects to the Master and listen to his teaching, then two Moons later he’s lost faith again and he’s banned any of the court from going near the Ghositarama. It was frustrating but, we had a secret weapon,’ Kesini gave her a cheeky side-glance.  ‘What was that?’ ‘There was a slave woman who was a maid for Queen Samavati. She had an incredible memory so – being a slave – she “did not count” as part of the court that was forbidden to go. She’d attend the Master’s teachings then come back to the seraglio and repeat it all. Most of the womenfolk in the palace would gather for these

77 
talks by Khujjuttara, the slave. That’s one very rare instance of a slave having an advantage over a queen, eh?!’ Kesini had come from quite a high-ranking family in Kosambi but, like Lady Jalini, was not at all bothered by issues of rank and birth; this had been another quality to her credit when Prince Mahanama had been pondering who to send with Vasabha.  ‘So, what do I need to know about this blessing ceremony?’ Vasabha asked.  ‘Not much, thankfully,’ Kesini replied, fishing through Vasabha’s supply of saris, sarongs and wraps. ‘The dress code for a monastery, and for a do like this, is smart, modest, top all covered up, a little bit of jewellery... well, actually, since you and the King are being blessed, the jewellery should be a little more than what the other court ladies and Queen Mallika are wearing but not by much. We should go for “small but expensive” for this.’  Vasabha was deeply grateful for these cues. ‘Kesini, you are a great friend! I’d never be able to...’ she caught herself about to talk about pretending, ‘... adapt to the customs of Kosala without you.’   * * *   The royal party made their way out of the city in the mid-morning. Carts and wagons bearing the food offerings to the Buddha and the Sangha had gone out earlier in the day for, although this event was going to be a lot smaller than the splendid affair of the wedding – with a thousand nobles gathered from Kosala, Maghada, Kasi, Malla, Videha and Vajji, as well as rows of pavilioned tents for several thousand citizens – there would still be many mouths to feed on this day.  A modest marquee, draped with royal colours and decked with flowers, had been set up at one side for the royal couple and the close family to dine in. Before they sat down though, with Kesini prompting from one side and Princess Vajiri from the other, they led Vasabha into the sala with King Pasenadi and Queen Mallika; they all then offered rice into the alms-bowls of the Buddha and the line of monks, seated at the front of the low platform alongside the Master.  On her first sight of the Buddha, Vasabha felt a flush of powerful emotions. She had never had the chance to see him before since, for many years, the Master had not taken the opportunity to visit his ancestral home, Kapilavatthu.  Her thoughts were stilled and she felt a warm glow in her heart welling up and then filling her eyes and head as well. The air seemed filled with a golden lustre and a loud ringing, a roaring sea of silvery sound, brimmed in her hearing. Yet, mingled with these joyous rapturous feelings, there was a jarring discord of intimidation and resentment:– He’s going to see right through me and know I’m a fake. I’m a slave girl, what am I doing here?! How could they treat me like this? Make me into some kind of performing animal who has to do her tricks well or risk being tortured to death?! I might be a slave but I’m human too.

78 
The thoughts came flooding through after the brief glowing silence ended. Still, the expression on the Master’s gentle face remained warm and open as he looked towards her. There was no scowl in that moment of contact when their eyes met, besides, the burst of anxiety and ill-feeling were soon overwhelmed with the joy-filled brightness once again.  After the meal-offering was over, and all the bhikkhus and novices had washed their alms-bowls, they returned to the sala and King Pasenadi and now Queen Vasabha came to the front, to sit on a pair of brocaded cushions before and below the Buddha. Three or four rows of monks were seated beside and behind the Master. All Vasabha had to do was sit demurely, with eyes downcast and hands in añjali, while the verses of blessing and recollection were recited by the Sangha.  The warm flow sustained itself within her throughout the ceremony and, thankfully for her, the agitated and fretful concerns did not return. She allowed herself, in fact, to be blessed. The idea of fakeness fell away at the unexpected realization that, in some ways, everyone is pretending – playing at roles that are not much more than a puppet show:– Yet we are all human, and we can wish each other well, and I can let their kind hearts genuinely bless me. Why not? I will need all the help I can get. Apart from the pretences and games people play there is goodness, and that is something real.  Once the ceremony was over, and the Buddha had departed to the seclusion of the Gandha-kuti – the ‘fragrant chamber’ as it was known since it had an exquisite natural aroma – there was a general movement of people getting up and preparing to take leave and go home. Vasabha was with the King but Kesini seemed to be waving discreetly at one of the monks, trying to catch his attention. After a moment the tall, dark-skinned monk, who had been sitting in one of the middle rows, came over.  ‘Is that you, Kesini? I haven’t seen you since the funeral of our dear Mistress Gavinna. We’re both getting older, eh?’ Krishna cheerfully greeted her, but was not able to restrain his glance at her missing hand.  Smiling equally broadly, Kesini held up her stumped forearm, ‘Yes, still gone, but I’m so used to it by now I hardly notice.’ She was glad to see an old acquaintance from Kosambi but she had to be going. ‘I’m a maid for the new queen so I’d better scoot and do my duty, otherwise I’ll get the sack and that would definitely ruin my day. So, you’re still a monk and I suppose you live here... oops. I’m starting a conversation again!’ She grinned at her own over-keenness. ‘Farewell, I think we’ll be here often.’  * * *   Vasabha settled into a rhythm of royal visits to Prince Jeta’s Grove. On some occasions it was a grand affair, when she accompanied King Pasenadi, sometimes it was just the court women who made the excursion together: Queen Mallika – who took any opportunity she could to visit the Master and receive his advice – Princess

79 
Vajiri, and herself. When it was just the group of women there was a lesser degree of formality and often the opportunity to meet with other members of the Sangha.  There were only monks and male novices living at the Jetavana as the nuns had their own residence at the Visakharama, up against the outer walls of Savatthi. If any of the royals wanted to talk with one of the monks, they would request the opportunity and, after the meal-offering was over, they would meet in the sala, or on some mats spread in the shade of the trees around the central area.  Kesini was keen to see Krishna and to catch up on the many years since their time in Kosambi. It had been sixteen or seventeen Rains since they had seen each other and they were now both in their mid-thirties. Queen Mallika and Princess Vajiri were taking the opportunity to ask some questions of Sariputta and, since Kesini knew he often gave very extensive analytical responses, she suggested to Queen Vasabha that she might introduce Her Serene Highness to her old acquaintance from Kosambi, who had also known her belovèd Gavinna, albeit briefly.  Vasabha greatly appreciated these informal opportunities, one reason being that it gave her the chance to mingle with a wider variety of folks than was possible at the Palace. There the protocols and expectations were somewhat rigid but here in the Jetavana, and even more so when they went to see the nuns at the Visakharama, there was a refreshing social levelling. The teachings of the Master on the irrelevance of caste and rank were made flesh – people were polite to the royals and gave them a front seat, but in essence, once inside the monastery gates, they were all just upasikas and upasakas – women and men lay disciples – no less, no more.  This meant that, even though Vasabha diligently maintained her façade, she also got to spend time around people from the poorer strata of society, sudras and candalas as well as merchants, in whose company she felt more natural ease.  When Kesini introduced her to Krishna, at first she assumed that this good-looking dark-skinned bhikkhu must be one who had come from a low-caste background as the brahmins and warrior-nobles tended to be fairer skinned and the sudras, candalas and matangas were often dark. She was as surprised as most people then when he spoke and showed that he had a highly educated accent.  Krishna was very used to this dynamic and had trimmed his standard explanation down to, ‘I’m darker than most from the merchant class.’  Kesini, despite being ‘just’ the maid and doing her best to be restrained, soon chimed into the conversation, asking about Krishna’s doings of the last many years. He did his best to give her the gist of it, summarizing his time in Kitagiri as ‘enjoyable’ but that he realized it would be good to spend some more time close to the Master after so many Rains away.  ‘You know who’s here in Savatthi now? He asked Kesini, expecting her not to be aware of it.  ‘Who?’ ‘Khujjuttara – the maid of Queen Samavati – she’s quite old now but still as sharp as a tack.’

80 
‘Really!’ Kesini was excited to hear this welcome news. ‘Ma’am,’ she turned to Queen Vasabha, ‘you remember me mentioning her to you. She’ll be a great friend for you I’m sure, once you get to meet her. She was always at Queen Samavati’s side, except when the fire happened,’ she held up her missing hand by way of illustration. ‘She was so cut up about that but she’s a wise woman, so she has not harboured any grudges. She got liberated from her slavery, didn’t she?’ ‘That’s right,’ Krishna replied, ‘she was manumitted by King Udena after Queen Samavati died, in gratitude for her service. And he gave her a royal pension of two hundred and fifty gold kahapanas every year. She moved to Savatthi to spend more time close to the Master, like me; she’s white-haired and still has her infirmities – her back has not improved – but she’s very comfortably situated. She’s got a small house outside the Jetavana gates. Perhaps you noticed it? She’s highly respected, by the monastics as well as the lay community. She’s known as one of the most learned of the disciples and is humble and straightforward with everyone.’ Vasabha did her best not to visibly light up at the news of a former slave rising so high, and validly too, in the eyes of society. She was thrilled to hear more about this extraordinary person but she channelled her enthusiasm into the remark, delivered as naturally and casually as possible, ‘Prince Mahanama, of Kapilavatthu, does the same thing from time to time. Shortly before I left Sakya he,’ and now she had to fish the new word out of her recent memory, ‘manumitted a slave, Nagamunda, and her whole family, out of gratitude for her service to the state.’ She was confident there was no shred of a hint that this was her own mother. ‘He said to her, ‘You are now a free woman. Go with your children and family on any path you choose.’ And he gave her a pension too though nothing so grand as that Khujjuttara received from King Udena.’ ‘Please send word, if you can,’ said Krishna, ‘when you next plan to visit, Ma’am. I will then alert Khujjuttara and she can be introduced to you.  ‘It’s important to have good Dharma friends. They can help us a lot, inestimably really, as I’ve found over time. Here in the Jetavana Ananda has been the best of friends and advisors to me. In Kitagiri, for the first little while at least, a monk called Surama was a wise guide and mentor for me. When I was growing up in Ujjeni, and at the Ghositarama in Kosambi, there was a monk, not quite of this tradition, who was the most wonderful teacher and companion. Anyway, I’ll be sure to let Khujjuttara know and I suspect she’ll be very happy to see Kesini again as well.’  * * *   Ninka kept getting messages she did not want to receive. Even though time passed much more slowly in the Heaven of the Thirty-Three Devas, she had been away for long enough for Prince Malabhari to be getting disgruntled. According to the customs and mores of the Tavatimsa devas, it was considered vulgar and tawdry to be ‘slumming it’ in the lower realms. Ninka had been given the permission of his

81 
Divine Highness to come and help her companions from the past, but now he was lobbying her vigorously to return to her home:– She was needed!  She did not require a cintamani talking stone to communicate as it was easily done mind-to-mind, across realms, for the devas of the Tavatimsa. Without responding she closed his message out, pushing it into an internal closet, ‘Not now. Not yet.’ By this time they had decided to act on Ant’s idea of moving to the Realm of Yakkhas. Gumbiya was put into the lead on this as it was his home territory.  ‘No need t’ go talk to the King. ’E’s bound to be off fighting somewhere and ’e would be surprised we’d gone to the trouble of askin’ if we did.’  This was good news to the friends so they set about combing the landscape and approaching the denizens, following Gumbiya’s lead. This land was a stark contrast to the Gandharva Realm. Looming castles and fortified city-walls abounded. The yakkhas they saw, on the ground and in the air, were often huge, making even the sturdy Gumbiya look slight in comparison. He had expanded to full height for this part of the search but still some of the yakkhas he sought out for information towered over him.  Most of the males were battle-clad, bristling with gold-chased armour and weaponry; the yakkhinis meanwhile were more lightly dressed but universally, it seemed, had a couple of golden-bossed knives in their belts, a throwing axe or two, and were full-fleshed and sturdily built, if not muscle-bound.  Gumbiya was plainly well-known and well-respected. That he had been personally honoured by Lord Indra, ruler of the Tavatimsa Heaven, and was a friend and companion of General Punnaka, opened doors for them but every avenue they tried proved fruitless. The General called in his spymaster, a yakkha of considerable intelligence and shape-shifting abilities, and grilled him on any rumours or hints of a half-kinnari half-human babe, either in transit or in a remote refuge in the Yugandhara Mountains or similar hiding place – nothing.  The General was one who was highly trustworthy and knowledgeable, he was not hopeful of any leads appearing in the yakkha community, in addition he was the nephew of King Vessavana, Lord of Yakkhas, and had been on the raid they had carried out together against the asuras.  ‘You should talk to my wife, Princess Irandati, she is the sister of Queen Samuddaja, wife of King Virupakkha, Lord of Nagas himself.’ Gumbiya looked to his companions, Ninka, Rhamba and Salassa, Muñca, and the three kinnaris, and was about to ask, ‘What d’ y’ fink?’ Before he could speak Ninka said loudly, ‘We should go on! Let’s try the Naga Realm.’ Nods and murmurs confirmed this all round. General Punnaka called Princess Irandati over to confer, and the whole group took off straight away. Once they were settled in the naga capital Bhogavati, which was somehow both under Lake Anotatta in the Himalayas and below the slopes of the Western face of the Yugandhara Mountains, they met up again with their old friend Queen Samuddaja.

82 
The story of their search was quickly recounted by Bee. When the Queen asked, ‘Isss Krisshna not helping with the sssearch? He isss the father, isss he not?’ An awkward silence hovered for a moment, then Maggot put it simply. ‘We have gone our separate ways,’ and left it at that.  Samuddaja had known Krishna since he was a boy, Amba and Tamba too. She knew his character and saw that it was not going to be wise to press the question of what had happened.  Irandati and Samuddaja went into a huddle and conversed urgently in their naga tongue – to outsiders this sounded only like a flow of hisses accompanied by breaths and subtle movements of the head.  ‘Dear friendsss,’ Samuddaja began, ‘sssince you do not inhabit thisss realm, any of you, and sssince sso many nagasss live in the watersss, impassable and unwelcoming to mossst of you, I sssuggessst that we nagassss look for you. Ssstay here and resst, eat, for I can sssee that you are all drained and weary from your laboursss.’ The first impulse of Maggot and her kinnari friends, and the others, was to say, ‘No, we’ll go too, somehow,’ but reason caught up with them. How would they survive?  As this realization sank in, the wisdom of Samuddaja’s advice became clear and they all agreed. It hit them then how right she was about their exhaustion too:– When had they last rested or eaten properly since they escaped from the Underworld?   * * *   Rhamba and Salassa, sister and brother apsaras, managed still to look elegant and to breathe sonorously as they slept. Gumbiya – fully exhausted – had reverted to his natural size in sleep, double the height of Bee, she was nevertheless contentedly curled in his right armpit with Maggot on her other side, still holding her hand in her slumbers.  On the far side of the pile of cushions, strewn across the green and gold chamber, with its spirally inlaid pillars, glittering with gems in the lamplight, Ant and Ninka were side by side, exhausted too, at least Ant was, but still muttering dozy words, full of endearments and warm secrets.  Irandati and Samuddaja had asked to borrow the precious shell fragment, in order to fully catch the scent. ‘Excussse me,’ Irandati said, then flickered a surprisingly forked tongue across the inner and outer surfaces; Samuddaja did the same. ‘We have nosssessss but thissss issss how we sssmell thingsss bessst,’ she explained. Then they had taken off, in dragon-serpent form, to search their community and the Naga Realm for information. Even Gumbiya’s heroic snoring had not been sufficient to interrupt the rest of the sleeping seekers. They were all out, except for Ninka – who needed little sleep

83 
generally. The two naga sisters had returned by the time they all awakened. Their search too had been in vain.  Ninka had had a sleepless night not just because of the difference of constitutions but also because of a terrible truth she had been hiding. She had known from the moment they found Tambaka’s scent, and the shell fragment, that those clues had been planted there. Her deva senses discerned immediately that the group were duped by a false trail. However, being so desperate to be with Ant and so afraid she’d be called away to the Tavatimsa Heaven and thus separated from Ant for what would be long years for the kinnari, even if it was but a short time for Ninka to be feasting with her deva companions and attending to Prince Malabhari:– She had not spoken up then, and how could she say anything now? It was too shameful to admit she had known it was false all along. She was in terrible doubt as to what she should do; make a confession and be hated by everyone, including Ant; or not admit anything and carry on with this fruitless wild goose chase?  Round and round it went.  She was on the brink of breakdown and utter despair when another urgent message came from Prince Malabhari, in the Nandana Grove of the Tavatimsa Heaven. As a weird kind of relief from her dilemma, without thinking it through further, she blurted out, ‘I have to go – back to the Tavatimsa, I’ve been putting it off but it’s not possible to stay any longer. I have to go!’ ‘Wha’,’ Ant was hardly awake yet. ‘How can you...?’ Ninka looked at her pleadingly and, with one last hug, she vanished from the Naga Realm and reappeared in the Nandana Grove.   * * *   Everyone was shocked; at the most they had seen that the beautiful, radiant Ninka was a little less glowing than usual. To Ant, all she had confided was that, ‘That annoying Prince, Malabhari, he won’t stop calling for me. It’s really irritating.’ She had had no idea that she and Ninka would soon be separated, and for who knew how long?  Ninka was good at making excuses so they been able to be together often but a few days in the Heaven of the Thirty Three was a long time in the lower reaches – a single night and day in the Tavatimsa Heaven was a hundred years in the human realm. So just part of a morning away from Tavatimsa had given her twenty years with Ant.  ‘So, what do we do now?’ Bee was up and wide awake. ‘We had a supper last night so we don’t need breakfast,’ Gumbiya’s stomach, with eerily good timing, rumbled in disagreement as she said this, ‘but which direction do we go from here? We can’t risk the Realm of the Kumbhandas and we’re pretty sure that Tam’s not there, we could go to the Human Realm but we know she was brought through here so there doesn’t seem much point in that.’

84 
Muñca cleared his throat. ‘This might seem a very long shot but, if we made a brief visit to the Tusita Heaven, I believe I could secure us an interview with Lord Maitreya, the future Buddha. We would need to keep it brief but, if we were properly prepared, he is almost certain to be able to give us unequivocally clear information on the baby’s whereabouts. He is a Bodhisattva, now awaiting his final birth in the Human Realm, and his principal quality is kindness; that’s what his name means.’ ‘How will we all get there? I mean, the Tusita is way up in the heights,’ Bee was still to be convinced.  ‘I have some skill in these areas and I think I can do it – carry all of us there, at least for a brief visit. We have to do everything we possibly can, we’ve got to try, haven’t we Miss Jambu?’ ‘Let’s do it,’ Maggot replied. ‘At least this will be decisive,’ she looked around the group, her eyes etched with both determination and despair. ‘We will find her, whatever it takes.’  ==*==

85 
CHAPTER 11: ‘TOGETHER FOREVER’   hy are you doing all this for us?’ Maggot asked Muñca before he made his preparations to get them all to the Tusita Heaven, ‘The Abode of the Contented’. Her grief-torn concerns about Tambaka had been punctuated more and more by this question over the last hours. ‘You are giving us all this time and attention, you’ve risked your own safety in the Underworld, and now perhaps the wrath of the kidnappers if you help us find my daughter – what makes this so important to you?’ ‘It’s simple, really, I didn’t spell it out before but the main reason I hold you and your friends in such high esteem is on account of the way you helped me and my dear ones. As you might be aware, musicians are often fond of their drugs – not just in the Human Realm and amongst the Earth Spirits but amongst the gandharvas too – and when they have made what they feel is their finest music under the influence, it makes it even harder to give up than an ordinary addiction,’ Muñca looked pained as he spoke of this, as if a cataract of bad memories were washing through him.  ‘A number of my family members got thoroughly hooked on amatagandha and their lives have been sad and painful since. Some have died, some have almost wasted away altogether. This is also why I think that Miss Sugandha, your friend Ant’s, supposition that some gandharvas might be involved in the abduction of your daughter is correct. It’s a grim thought to entertain, but they might be helping not just for spite and vengeance but also to get their hands on whatever diminishing supplies of the perfume might remain.’  Muñca seemed to be enduring this heart-breaking situation well and his story touched Maggot, and the others who were listening in. He paused and said, ‘So that’s the sad story but it’s what motivates me to help and to risk my comfort and safety.’ Maggot’s sombre mood was lifted a little by this impressive display of courage and unselfishness. For a while she brightened up and her countenance matched the iridescent pink and rose-adorned hues of her robes.   * * *   As they tidied their things and bid their farewells to Samuddaja and Irandati, and the other nagas of the household, Bee took a moment to check in on Ant, now alone after Ninka’s sudden departure. Ant was often very quiet, and kept her moods to herself but Bee knew that she felt things very strongly.  ‘How are you doing?’

86 
‘Alright,’ Ant replied, a little too quietly. Bee gave her a half-sideways look and squeezed her hand. ‘Actually, not good at all. I should be prepared for separation but, when Ninka goes back to the Tavatimsa, I never know if it’s going to be for a few days or a whole sun-turning, or twenty, or more. The situation of Maggot and Tam – how she was just going for a visit to her family for an evening and then never seeing her baby again, I mean maybe never – I feel it can be the same way for us. I might never see Ninka again.  ‘I know that the Master has said that, if a couple want to stay together in this life and be in each other’s company in future lives they must have the same faith, virtue, generosity and wisdom – and I know that Nink and I are super-compatible in that way but you never know.  ‘We’ve also taken very seriously the advice on “reappearance through aspiration” that the Master has referred to from time to time. If you’ve got enough of those four qualities plus ‘learning’ as well, and if you fix your mind on reappearing after this life is over on, say, the Realm of the Earth Spirits or the Tavatimsa Heaven or wherever, and you establish that thought and develop it, then those aspirations will lead to you being born there. That’s how it works. So, if we do things right we will be able to be together forever.’ Bee did not want to burst her bubble but she couldn’t resist saying, ‘Didn’t the Master also stress the point that, “This is for one who is virtuous, not for one who is naughty” – or something like that – so you’d better both be on your best behaviour,’ she was half-joking since, as a deva from the Heaven of the Thirty-Three, Ninka was a shining example of purity and gracious behaviour, and Ant was probably the most quiet and modest of the three kinnari companions.  ‘Yes, we know that!’ Ant managed to smile a little. ‘The Master said, “The heart’s wish of one who is virtuous succeeds because of their purity,” I think we can manage that.’ As they were speaking Muñca had taken himself to one side of the broad open chamber where they had all been staying. He sat himself on a flat cushion, framed by one of the wide windows that looked onto the lakes below, and through the sheen of the waters of Lake Anotatta which they seemed to be under at the same time – it was a confusing mixture of dimensions to outsiders but quite familiar to the nagas.  All was prepared now for them to depart for the Tusita Heaven. Muñca became absorbed in his meditation while the three kinnaris sat shoulder to shoulder watching him – the rosy colours of Maggot in the centre, flanked by the magenta-purples of Bee to her left and the kanavera reds of Ant to her right. Gumbiya held Bee’s hand on one side and the upper arm of Muñca with the other. Rhamba held Ant’s hand on her left and Salassa’s on her right; Salassa held on tight to Muñca’s upper arm, opposite Gumbiya. The circle was now ready. Muñca’s concentration deepened.   * * *

87 
 A year had passed since the royal wedding and, to the universal joy of Kosala, Queen Vasabha had given birth to a healthy son. As a queen she seemed to be expected to hand over most if not all nursing and caring duties to Kesini and the other maids, wet-nurses and helpers, but she contrived to spend as much time as she could with her child, and to still go along with the court’s expectations. She loved to hold her boy and breathe in the sweet mysterious, unnameable scent of his hair, his skin – everything!  She was used to babies being passed around, being cared for by older sisters and an occasional aunt, but to have to let him out of her sight so much was another test she had not prepared for. She reflected:– It’s strange but still, how can any girl really prepare for how it’s going to be to have her first baby? It’s a test for all of us.  It was a custom among the Kosalans for the eldest female member of the royal line to choose the name for the new heir to the throne. On the naming day the King sent a message to his grandmother, saying, ‘A son has been born to Queen Vasabha, daughter of the Sakyan Prince; what shall his name be?’ The courtier who was charged with this message was, unfortunately, slightly deaf. He went and told the King’s grandmother, passing on the message correctly, but when she replied he misheard her. She said, ‘Even when Vasabha had not borne a son she was more dear to the King than all the world; now she will be his utter darling.’ The courtier misheard the word ‘darling’ that is to say ‘vallabha’ in the Kosalan dialect, so what he thought she said was, ‘Vidudabha’. So, back he went to the King and said, ‘Her Serene Highness, the King’s Grandmother, has said his name is to be Vidudabha.’ The King, never having heard this name before, presumed, ‘This must be some ancient family tradition,’ and chose not to argue with his esteemed grandparent, so he named his son Vidudabha.   * * *   The fact that the little boy was away from her so much gave Vasabha a lot of time to reflect on her situation. When she had been kept going non-stop with kitchen work and the duties she had in Kapilavatthu, she had never had much time to stop and think. There was never a pause in the round of activities in daylight and the night hours had little light available for the slaves, so these were usually passed in exhausted sleep, if not in caring for one or two of the younger brothers and sisters who had the gripes or were just starving hungry.  Things were different here. Some days she would have the entire afternoon to herself – the King being taken up with some business, Vidhu being carried round the gardens by his nurse and a page boy with a sunshade – these were the first days of her life in which she had experienced such open hours.  She was now well-settled and the ruse she was pulling on everyone was slipped on daily like a comfortable pair of shoes, or some familiar gloves, it was an easy fit now. She reflected:– What am I really doing here? And what is its value? I am

88 
serving an important role in the Kingdom of Kosala which is one of the great powers of Jambudvipa. If I continue to play my part convincingly all will be well but it’s not just about doing a good job of acting... that’s one small part of it... the results of it will be good for me and for everyone else too... it’s an act of service but one which is sadly based on a lie... but if I do it right the lying part becomes insignificant. What the biggest thing is – which is hard to get my head around – is that my actual son, my baby, will be the head-anointed, warrior noble, King of Kosala. That is not just about playing a role... that’s history... that’s forever... that’s real. My actual child – me the slave from the kitchen – my child will be a ruler, and his children, and theirs too... Wherever I’ve come from, I’m now a part of a big story so it’s my responsibility, for everyone’s sake, to do this right... my life, my child, what I do, what I say... it’s all significant now... these are real swords and arrows, as the lads say, not blunted ones for practice.  She stared at her image in a polished bronze mirror, now speaking to her new self, ‘When I was little I never got the chance to dress up and play with my sisters and the other girls and boys, we had none of all that, being marked as slaves. We didn’t have any spare clothes to pretend to be someone else in and the boys would be beaten if they picked up a stick and pretended it was a sword. Now I’ve got all this finery to muck around with, I realize I do get a thrill from the costumes and the sparkles, from ordering people around – I can get giddy over all of that, it’s fun! So, if I am really going to make a go of this I need to be wary... Don’t get puffed up and proud like one of those ghastly... what d’ they call ’em... pompous nobs. Don’t get drunk on it, don’t believe the story, stay alert and we’ll be alright... Some of the maids, when they want to flatter me, say I light up the dark, as if my skin was something special… Don’t make nuthin’ of it… Anything of it…’, she corrected herself, even in her solitary monologue. ‘Come to think of it, how the Buddha talks about “heedfulness” all the time, his way of levelling everything, well at least as much as possible – I like that. That’s a great thing for everyone I reckon... How he’s happy for an ex-slave like Khujjuttara to hold such a high position in his group – the fact that she’s dark-skinned and has a crooked back, as well as starting out a slave, that all counts for nothing as far as he’s concerned. It’s all to do with good qualities – how you are, that’s all that matters to ’im, to him, the Master,’ she corrected herself again.  ‘The King and Queen, the other Queen, they all love what he does, what he stands for, without there being any danger of anyone finding fault with his choices, so that’s something I can do... Help support those levelling ideas somehow, so that there’s less, what’s it called... prejudice... and less rigid ideas about the castes... so... How can I, as a queen, help with that?    * * *  Vasabha had finally met Khujjuttara on an outing to the Jetavana with the King and many of the court. Kesini had sent word to Krishna about the impending

89 
visit so he, in turn, had made sure that Khujjuttara was informed. This way she could be introduced to the young new queen.  The royals made offerings to the Buddha and the Sangha, as they usually did, in the form of alms-food and other gifts, then, after the meal was over, the King and the Queens, along with Princess Vajiri and the assembly of palace officials, and many other lay people who happened to be visiting that day, gathered around the Master and requested a Dharma talk.  Vasabha was doubly pleased that he focused not just on the blessings of generosity and virtue (which was an uncomfortable point for her, especially the Fourth Precept: ‘I undertake the Precept to refrain from lying’) but he also spoke at length about the nature of ‘a true brahmin’. He took the usual caste designation and opened up the meaning to refer, instead of to birth and bloodlines and all that puppet-show, to the purity of heart. The words struck her deeply:   ‘One who meditates in seclusion,  Free of the stain of defiling conduct and Who has done their duty well,  One who has cleansed the heart of Corruptions and has reached the highest goal; Such a one I call a real brahmin.’  Vasabha’s feelings of inspiration and understanding of the principle of this teaching were tempered by the truth she concealed about her own life, but it was enough for her, for now, to park the disquiet and regret about the deception, the reflection that, ‘I’ll get back to that later,’ was as much as she could resolve on for now.  Once the formal talk was finished the Buddha took his leave and Ananda accompanied him back to the Gandha-kuti. Krishna, who had been sitting close to Ananda on the low platform, remained behind and indicated to Kesini to come up to the front of the hall for a moment.  ‘Khujjuttara’s here, just over by that pillar, would this be a good moment for Queen Vasabha to meet her?’ ‘I’ll check with their Majesties, but I think this’d be ideal. The King always enjoys her quick-witted wisdom. Hang on a moment.’ Kesini swiftly ducked away, approaching first Queen Mallika and Queen Vasabha, then the King, who agreed they could tarry for a while to spend some time with the sagely elder.  As they had all approved, Kesini brought her agèd friend over to where the royal party was now seated.
