Mallika, meanwhile, had simply followed her usual form of dressing in an unadorned and modest fashion and, by way of contrast, in as simple a garb as she thought she could get away with and not humiliate His Majesty by her being inappropriately under-dressed. The choices of the two queens in their modes of apparel didn’t pass unnoticed. Neither did their expressions, as Queen Mallika’s face displayed a complete freedom from anxiety and a serene radiance, quite unlike that of her junior counterpart. The King called the court to order by welcoming all who were assembled and by acknowledging his own respect for the young candala woman whom he had met on a few occasions. He reminded the court – and gave the brahmin priests, along with the warrior-noble gentry, a meaningful look, as he did this – that he respected the putting aside of caste and rank when in the company of the samanas of different groups.  ‘Thus it should be taken to heart that, within the monasteries and encampments of such spiritually committed renunciants, a king or a queen, or anyone of rank, is at liberty to befriend or learn from a monastic or a lay disciple of any caste, any station in life whatsoever.’ He made a point of seeking out Khujjuttara in the crowd and smiling to her as he had developed great respect and affection for her over the years. She raised her hands in añjali by way of acknowledgement.  ‘Similarly, the fact that the victim of this cruel murder was a candala by caste, who even went by the name “Matanga”, does not diminish the seriousness of the crime. If anyone in this court, when acting as a witness or one being accused, indicates any disrespect for her saying such as, “she was only a candala…” or “a person of high rank should not be punished on account of the death of a candala”, that person will be held as having insulted the Crown and will be punished accordingly, irrespective of rank or caste.’ His brows bristled with icy authority as he said this, ‘Am I clear!?’ Hands were raised in añjali all around with a hearty ‘Sadhu’ being heard in some quarters whilst quieter in others.  ‘To move now to the case at hand – we have a number of people who have been accused, who have graciously agreed to be present for this trial, some well-known and of high rank, some less well-known. We also have a number of witnesses that the Crown wishes to hear speak publicly. I thank those of you who have volunteered to offer what information you can. Are there any witnesses who have been brought to this court involuntarily?’ The King was using the customary words for this part of the formal procedure but those who were close by could see a sharpening of his look and a suggestion of a smile under his moustache.  ‘Yes indeed, Sire. We have two… gentlemen… from the Sukhavati Tavern, well, that’s where we caught ’em, going by the names Sattuka and Kalidanta,’ the Captain of the Guard announced, as properly as he could.  ‘Just those two?’ The King raised his brows – already knowing the answer. ‘Well, let’s call them in, shall we?’  Out of the great respect for the Master, despite being one of the accused, King Pasenadi had made it clear that he would not be called into a court of this nature.

149 
Queen Vasabha was not at all happy with this but the King had insisted. Even though he used the words ‘the chief accused’ repeatedly during the proceedings, he was unable to treat the Master as just one other individual and so refused to cause him to be physically present. Ananda was in the court as a representative of the Sangha and to retain the words of the discussions. He was assigned a seat in a place of respect; this too irked Vasabha but she had no choice in the matter. It seemed that, in the unlikely case that the Master was found to be guilty, word would be taken to him at the Jetavana of this conclusion.  * * *  The two men from the tavern were brought in, between two guards each, and the King ushered them to kneel before the throne immediately.  ‘So, good men, what do you have to say? The King politely asked.  ‘Nuffink,’ the taller one, Sattuka, replied.  ‘You?’ He asked the other one.  ‘Me neever,’ was all Kalidanta would say.  ‘If I may, Sire,’ a non-descript person in somewhat obscuring clothing then stepped up, ‘please excuse my wrappings but I am one of your undercover agents and to be unrecognized is one of my tools-of-the-trade.’ ‘Of course, go on.’ ‘Sire, whilst I was conducting researches, at the Sukhavati Tavern, these two gentlemen, who were clearly friends or… er… business associates, were deep in their cups and were arguing or debating with each other. Kalidanta seemed to be angry that Sattuka had taken all the money for a task they had both been contracted to fulfil. Kalidanta said, and I quote,’ here the witness closed his eyes to recite: ‘“You killed the girl and then chucked ’er in a ditch, wivaht even telling me you were going ter do the job that night. You took all the money for yourself and now ’ere you are, drinkin’ it all away – wha’ abou’ my share! I was in on this too!”’ King Pasenadi was scrutinizing the faces of the two. They both looked horrified at hearing those words, particularly they were shocked at how precisely the spy had recalled them. They looked at each other, silently trying to see a way forward that might be a means of keeping their heads attached to their bodies.  ‘Well?’ The King pressed them for a response. ‘It might go a little easier for you both if you told the court who paid you to do this.’ Before anyone had time to draw a breath Sattuka blurted out, ‘It were two of the monks!’ The words both condemning themselves but also deflecting the blame, if possible.  ‘And which kind of monks – the Buddha’s disciples or those of another sect? Please be clear on this now.’ ‘It was them two – sittin’ over there!’ Sattuka pointed in the direction of Devadatta’s group. Devadatta, prepared as he was for any outcome, with no hesitation looked with shocked horror at his two companions.

150 
‘Do you know the names of these two Venerables?’ The King leant into the honorific with a deliberate sarcasm.  ‘Nah, they woz cagey ’bout that. But it woz them. Kno’ ’em anywhere.’ The guards were already moving towards the groups of monastics, gathered on their mats on the north side of the hall. Devadatta had drawn away from the two monks sitting near him, increasing his look of disgust and looking about himself, as if for help. It was a convincing act.  The two Elders were hauled in front of King Pasenadi, with a little respect but more severity and purposefulness on the part of the guards.  ‘Your names?’ The King raised one of his impressive grey brows, going through the formality but knowing full well who they were already.  ‘My name is Venerable Kokalika.’ ‘And….’ ‘I am Venerable Samudadatta.’ ‘”Venerables” eh? Well, we’ll see about that, shall we not? Can you refute the statements of these two gentlemen?’ He leant forward in his throne, pressing the question upon them.  ‘Why, Sire, Your Majesty, these are a mere pair of n’er-do-wells, disreputable people. They have probably been hired to put the blame on us innocent Elders.’ Kokalika was sweating.  ‘You do remember what I said about seeming to dismiss the worth and word of the lower castes?’ ‘Oh, of course Sire.’ He was lost for words and looked desperately at his partner Samudadatta.  ‘So did you, or did you not, hire these two to kill the upasika Matanga? In the midst of this court, and before your own mentors and teachers, did you do it?’ Mara, as was his wont, had been listening to and watching the whole proceedings eagerly. He had been especially pleased with Devadatta’s feigned surprise and disgust at his accused co-conspirators. Now he waited for more deceit and blaming, relishing the ongoing chaos and division in the society that would come from all this. He was disappointed when, out of the hushed, suspenseful silence Samudatta spoke:  ‘Yes, I cannot lie any further, it was us but we…’, his words seemed to catch in his throat and he looked over to Devadatta who was in turn looking at the matting before him and shaking his head. The words would not come to place the blame with his mentor and master. He kept his silence. Whether it was through his own volition or via a mental manoeuvre on the part of his teacher, it was impossible for ordinary folk to tell. ‘Since you admit your guilt the work of this court is almost done. All that remains is the pronouncing of sentence upon you all.  ‘The two hired killers acted for pay. Their crime is murder but the motivation for that death came from you. As I most generously promised that things would go a little easier for you two if you were compliant,’ he said, turning to the cut-throats,

151 
‘you will not be tortured before your heads are struck from your bodies, which is the approved punishment for murder.  ‘As for you two – as you have deliberately caused the death of a human being I believe,’ and here he cast a respectful glance at Ananda, ‘that according to your own discipline you ceased to be monks at the moment that poor Matanga breathed her last. Is that correct?’ Ananda, seated on a platform nearby, made añjali and indicated with an inclination of his head that this was a correct understanding.  ‘Accordingly, you will go through the city, no longer wearing the ochre robes of the Master’s Sangha but as you are, as lay people, and declare: “We caused this woman, Matanga, Haritacca-Kumari of Kulluta, to be killed because of the desire to cast reproach upon the Samana Gotama, the Lord Buddha; there is no fault in the conduct of the Master or in his other disciples.” After you have passed through the city and declared this at every crossroads, you will, like your partners in crime from the tavern, be taken to a place of execution outside the city walls where your heads also will be struck from your bodies, as is suitable for the heinous criminals that you are.’ Devadatta was relieved to hear the, ‘No fault in… his other disciples’ piece so, thwarted though his scheme had been, he himself had fully evaded blame, as far as he was aware.  Krishna, despite his involvement and the risk that he might be blamed as the one who persuaded Matanga to be putting herself at risk, was so sorrowful and filled with remorse that he had been numb to most of the proceedings.  Khujjuttara was pleased to see justice done but was thoroughly unconvinced by Devadatta’s displays of surprise and horror.  Queen Vasabha was a mixture of feelings – pleased that her sister and friend’s killers had been found and would be punished but confused and regretful that she had been so wrong about the Buddha.  King Pasenadi, meanwhile, surveying the court as the guilty parties were led away, had a satisfied smile on his face. ‘My friends, citizens, Venerable Sirs. I am glad that these matters were able to be dealt with so swiftly and efficiently. Let us all go and see about some lunch.’   * * *  Krishna was devastated; he was uninterested in food and couldn’t sleep. He was visited, however, about three days after the trial, by Dusaka and Tingri who, kindly, didn’t try to chat with him or cheer him up or fix him, but rather simply took up residence under his kuti, on a low cord-strung bed and a small mat, and left him to his misery.  Finally Krishna was not able to sustain the fug of self-hatred and remorse any longer and besides, his belly was growling, so he came unsteadily down the wooden steps and joined with his wizened mentor below.

152 
Dusaka began by asking, ‘How are you?’ and Tingri came and laid her shaggy head in his lap. For a time Krishna wept and could not speak but then, as the tears dried up he moaned, ‘But she didn’t have to do that! Why did she not just tell me I was being a fool and get me to wake up to what Devadatta and the others were plotting, if she could see it so quickly and so clearly? And I know he’s as much to blame as the others even if he wriggled out of it at the trial.  ‘I don’t care that I was tricked and was made a dupe myself – that’s a familiar story to me – but Matanga, she was such a sweet girl, and then I find out she was my daughter! How long had she known that anyway?’ He was not looking for an answer, rather he was simply howling to the universe by saying this, but Dusaka, with a quick nod from Tingri, chimed in, ‘No more than a Moon or so; I gave her the news myself. You were wrapped up in Devadatta’s crowd, and we never saw you, so you didn’t hear sooner. She was looking for an opportunity to meet you quietly and let you know.’  ‘But, but how could they do that? Kill a good person so brutally just to try to discredit the Master?!’  ‘Well, sadly, perhaps part of it was that they saw that, as a candala, she didn’t really count as a “person”. It’s strange and sad but some people think that way. It’s also likely that because you are so dark, despite coming from a rich merchant family, that they also easily used you and were ready for you to be blamed and cast out. It’s ironic, as it’s only “virtuous beings in human form” that are classed as “manussa” in the Master’s scheme of things. You can have a human body – be a “puggala”– but not be “dwelling in the Manussa Realm”. If you are living a life full of lies and self-centred hates and desires, you’re more like an animal, or a hungry ghost, or a hell-demon, rather than a true person.’ Dusaka sat quietly after this, hoping that his words were sinking in and were of some small comfort to his now middle-aged protégé.  ‘As for why young Matanga was so ready to give her life in order to help exonerate the Master and cause the guilty to be revealed, sometimes there are influences from the past, the distant past, that incline us to make the choices we do,’ he looked at Tingri, who responded by cocking her head minutely, as if to say, ‘Maybe.’ ‘Come on, I think there’s a few people visiting today that you might want to see – do you feel up to a bit of company? They are people you can cry with too, if you need to.’  * * *  Queen Vasabha, Kesini, Khujjuttara and Ananda were gathered at the sala. It was late afternoon in the Cold Season now and the sun was goldening as it descended. Dust drifted through the sunbeams, rising from the few passers by on the walking paths through the forest.

153 
Dusaka, Krishna and Tingri approached quietly as the group were in deep discussion recounting the details of the crisis and the false accusations, and discussing how they felt about Devadatta and the way he had evaded justice.  They welcomed the three newcomers and Krishna set himself beside the Elder Ananda; Krishna now being aged some forty-five Rains, Ananda was a grey-haired man of over seventy. Krishna was glad to be among friends again; Queen Vasabha was particularly quick to be welcoming and friendly. The events of the trial, and her conversations with these friends, had fully convinced her that Krishna had had no knowledge of what was planned to be done to Matanga. She regretted the amount of bitter words she had spoken and the vengeful, spiteful thoughts she had nursed with respect to him – as the one who drew Matanga into the plot – and to the Buddha whom she felt she had grievously maligned over these past weeks.  ‘I hear that Matanga had a very special message for you, informing you that she was your daughter, by her mother Sugandhi of Kulluta…’, she paused and looked over at Dusaka and Khujjuttara anxiously, as if to check that she could not be making life worse for everyone with what she wanted to say.  Before she could continue, Krishna said, ‘Yes! What with everything else,’ here he caught his breath for a moment, hoping not to burst into tears again, ‘I haven’t really had time to digest that.’ ‘Well, at the time that our friend Dusaka revealed that to her, he had also just disclosed to me something about my past.’  She took a breath. ‘The fact of it is, I am your child as well – not by Sugandha – but my mother was apparently a kinnari, with the nickname Maggot, who Dusaka has known for many years.’ ‘But, you’re a Sakyan, a princess from the north, everyone knows that!’ Krishna was astounded and boggled by her saying something so outlandish, so bizarre and against all that was common knowledge.  ‘Everyone thinks that, so I am taking a risk by letting you know, and Venerable Ananda here, but I had been hoping to tell you, just as Matanga was, as soon as I could.’ Krishna found his mind now racing. He was filled with questions falling over each other, the first out was, ‘You are my lost child?! How?!?... Does Maggot know you’re here?’ Then, ‘Is she here too!?’  ‘No, not yet. I only just recently found out myself. Apparently she’s been looking for me for my whole life and I’m twenty-five Rains old now.’ The Queen’s face was lighting up with this new change in her world and the clouds of grief and anger were dispersing. Krishna too, as the conversation unfolded, went from haggard and grey to a brighter and smiling state, the years falling away from him, his eyes shining.  ‘But this is amazing! How did you…? What happened? How…? This is wonderful! How can we tell Maggot you’ve been found?’ Dusaka was grinning like a cat with a bowl of cream, while Tingri was yipping and snorting in rapid succession. Khujjuttara and Kesini both dabbed their eyes with their shawls. Amid the laughter and the tears, Dusaka eventually spoke

154 
up, ‘I recognized her by her natural scent. Being half-kinnari she is blessed with her mother’s fragrance and that goes with her wherever she goes. It’s unmistakeable so, since I had known her and held her as an infant, I could not miss it.’ As he said this Krishna realized too that he had sensed a weird familiarity about her fragrance, when they had previously met, but he had never put two and two together before. ‘All we know, so far, is that she was alone in the forest at the Ghositarama and that somehow she got taken to Kapilavatthu, capital city of the Sakyans, where the Master comes from. How she ended up in the family of Yasa and Nagamunda, a slave family serving the palace, I have not a clue, but we can find out, with a bit of digging.’ He glanced meaningfully at Ananda at this point as if to appeal for a hand in this effort.  Ananda, easily picking up the hint, responded, ‘I’m sorry my friends, I have no powers to read minds or to delve past ages, unlike the Master and Elders such as Maha-Moggallana. I have a good memory but that does not help much here. I can only recall events I’ve been present at, or retrieve information I have been told. Beyond that, I’m as in the dark as you are.’ ‘But,’ Krishna, wreathed in smiles and not really caring that much about the details since Tambaka, his baby, had now been found, could not but ask, ‘if you were a kitchen slave, how did you get to be a queen, here in Kosala?’ ‘That’s a bit of a story, and at the risk of my life, literally, I will tell you.’ Vasabha then recounted the whole tale of her being chosen, and her current deception. She ended by saying, as she had to Khujjuttara and the others, ‘I leave it to you to keep silent or to tell others, as you see fit.’ Ananda replied, ‘Great Queen, as the attendant of the Master and as a monk, I have been privy to a vast array of people’s secrets, things they have mentioned or things that the Blessed One has divined. As he has the ability to read anyone and everyone’s mind, you can understand that the Master is knowledgeable about an even greater, immeasurably more vast number of secrets and hidden memories. He chooses not to intervene or cause action to be taken on the basis of his psychic powers alone. He prefers to leave it to the choices of the individual. He does not spy on people’s minds and lives and then share that knowledge with others. He teaches the Dharma so that individuals can wake themselves up. That is all. In short the knowledge of your birth is safe with me.’  ‘And me!’ Krishna echoed, ‘of course.’  ‘At the risk of going against this principle of privacy and caution, respecting the details of people’s lives,’ Dusaka interrupted, ‘I feel it will also be useful to you all to understand how far back your connections go. Yes, Matanga and Vasabha are your daughters in this life but you might also wish to consider, “Why? Why them and me?”’ He glanced at Tingri who half-cocked her head the other way, as if to indicate, ‘Go ahead, it’s your choice,’ Dusaka then turned to Khujjuttara and said, ‘My apologies for dropping another rock into your pond but the fact is that when Krishna was born he was the younger half-brother of Amba and Tamba, the two daughters of the merchant, later the pilgrim Kamanita.

155 
‘Amba became Queen Samavati and Tamba became Queen Magandiya, both married to King Udena of Vamsa, and both not recognizing or knowing each other until the tragic ending of their lives and, in truth, Amba did not even know it at the close, only Tamba in her final and most distressed moments. These two sisters, Amba and Tamba, Samavati and Magandiya, have now been born as Krishna’s daughters; Amba reappeared as Matanga while you, Your Majesty, were Tamba; this is probably why you recently conceived such an irrational suspicion and hatred of the Master.’ He chose not to mention her stint in the Hell of Molten Copper.  As he had suspected, this particular boulder being dropped into the pond left Krishna, Khujjuttara, Kesini and Queen Vasabha momentarily open-mouthed in astonishment but soon eager for more details.   * * *  Ant had decided to remain behind in the Heaven of the Thirty-Three Gods in order to find Ninka, so the searching party was again reduced and only five of them remained. They arrived in Kapilavatthu and found it to be a quiet, smallish city beneath the foothills of the Himalaya.  ‘How long have we been gone from the human world?’ asked Bee, to no one in particular. ‘How do we find out what year this is? What would people here measure things by?’  The rest of the group were stumped by this until Gumbiya offered, ‘The Master leavin’ ’ome and then comin’ back as the fully enlightened Buddha, that has ’as to ’ave left a mark. Folk’d remember that.’  ‘Great idea’!’ Bee and Rhamba both clapped him on his back, at least the lower part that they could reach. Gumbiya glowed a little at the appreciation, but was a tad peeved by how surprised they all looked at him having had a bright idea.  ‘If Tambaka is here, living or working in the palace we should be able to find some traces of her and then we just follow them up. We don’t know how old she will be but, if we can get a reckoning from one of the local tree devas or kinnari of how long since the Master first came back to visit that should give us a lead. We got stuck in the Underworld and time was doing all sorts of strange things there so Tam could be a baby, a child, a young woman, a mother of ten or in her old age – we just don’t know yet.’ Maggot’s brain was now getting into mode.  ‘We should split up,’ suggested Salassa, ‘go our separate ways and see what we can find. The best bet is if Rhamba and I start on the tree-spirits and Gumby sees if he can find any local yakkhas, while you two comb the palace. We all need to see if we can figure out her age and, then, if there are recent sights or smells or signs that she’s here. She might be able to see us,’ he added, realizing then he might be setting Maggot up for disappointment, ‘… or she might not.’  ‘She could when she was a baby!’ Maggot interjected.  ‘But those things can change – remember, Krishna lost his ability to see us for those years he was caught in worldly things,’ Bee added, being suddenly aware that

156 
she had mentioned Krishna for the first time in a long while. Maggot looked at the paving slabs of the square where they were gathered and said nothing.   * * *  The five split up and went their separate ways as the sun moved a few spans across the sky. When they came together again at their landing spot in the square they compared notes and their discoveries.  Gumbiya found that, even though this was the place where the Master grew up, he very rarely came here so there were not the great numbers of yakkhas, devas and various earth-spirits that you found in the big monasteries, like the Jetavana, the Veluvana in Rajagaha or the Ghositarama in Kosambi. In the landscape around the city in the various clefts and woods, mountain gullies where he had checked, none of the beings he had spoken with had a precise idea of how long ago the Master had first returned.  ‘They wozn’t very ’elpful. I got “No more than five or six sun turnin’s ago” up to “Could’ve been forty, if y’ask me”. Not much use – sorry – and no smell of her to be sure.’ Bee and Maggot scoured the palace rooms and halls, especially the shanties and annexes where the slave families lived but no signs showed themselves and no one who was known as being half-kinnari and naturally sweet smelling.  Rhamba and Salassa came in last. ‘We think we’ve got a date. One of the agèd rukkha-devas, of a grand banyan tree, assured us that she remembered the first return of the Master as the Buddha, being exactly forty-one sun-turnings ago. The Master came here two sun-turnings after the enlightenment, so he was thirty-seven at that time. Add those two together and it tells us the Master must be seventy-eight right now, in human years.’ ‘He was fifty-three when Tambaka was hatched,’ said Maggot, thoughtfully, ‘so that makes her…’ ‘Seventy-eight less fifty-three is twenty-five,’ Salassa calculated quickly, noticing how the others all looked surprised at his command of numbers. ‘I know I can easily be focussed on my clothes and making beautiful music and dance-moves but, if you want to make that music, you need to know your numbers.’ ‘Anyways, she is twenty-five Rains now, by their human calculations, that much at least we know. So, how do we widen the search?’ Maggot’s mind was again racing, seeking for solutions.   * * *  Queen Vasabha had requested the opportunity to speak with the Master, as privately as possible. She came to the Jetavana just with Kesini, and very modestly clad with a white shawl and without any jewellery this time. The Buddha just had Ananda sitting with him. He gave the Queen the rare privilege of receiving her on

157 
the veranda of the Gandha-kuti. She was able to speak freely, without concern that they would be overheard or interrupted.  ‘Venerable Sir, I would humbly like to ask for forgiveness from you. During the days following the death of Matanga I was confused, sad, enraged, grief-stricken and I struck out at many people and things around me, to blame and to vent my heated, agitated feelings upon. You, I regret were chief among my targets and I spoke ill of the Blessèd One in many and various ways. Please forgive me for all I have done, by body, speech and mind that was injurious or disturbing for you.’ ‘I forgive you, Great Queen.’ The unqualified and immediate acceptance by the Master of her and her shortcomings was utterly disarming and liberating to her.  Also, since not only was she no Sakyan princess but a sham kshatriya, and furthermore that she had been the notorious and murderous Queen Magandiya in a recent life, was something she knew that he knew and yet there was not a shred of ill-will in his gaze upon her. There was only kindness and compassion. She bowed, and wept freely at the weight removed from her heart.  ‘Venerable Master,’ Kesini raised her one hand in añjali, ‘forgive me for being so bold as I’m just a servant here but can I ask about Devadatta?’ She very deliberately granted him no ‘venerable’ honorific.  ‘You may, of course,’ he replied.  ‘Since you have great psychic powers, and, one assumes, are able to read the minds and to know the actions and intentions of others, why did you not obstruct Devadatta in carrying out his plan of having our dear Matanga killed? And, further – please forgive me Venerable Sir but I’m blunt by nature – why in the court did Ananda not speak up and tell the court, and Devadatta himself, that you were fully conscious of his guilt?’ ‘These are good questions, Kesini. As I believe that Ananda has explained before but which I can state for myself now, the Tathagata works by allowing people to be the makers of their own salvation and their own mistakes. On occasion I will offer advice or guidance, up to the third time but, if the individual chooses to ignore that advice, then the Tathagata must necessarily step aside and let the person act according to their will. This is how all the Buddhas of past ages have given guidance and taken action, or stepped aside. Sometimes such allowance of action ends with great anguish and bloodshed amongst the consequences. This is the way it is.  ‘In these present circumstances, and according to this method, it is up to Devadatta to see the error of his ways and to endeavour to do better in the future. He knows the truth of the matter. It is up to him to acknowledge that; otherwise if only force or threat is used to make his behaviour change, the heart will still remain stuck to its views and habits. It is up to Devadatta to tell the truth, if he can bear to.  ‘He has wedded his heart to fame, honour and renown, to riches gained from spiritual prowess. Only when he sees in his heart that these are superficial, worldly qualities, not worth pursuing, let alone lying for, only then will he learn and be free. As I have said, “The liar goes to hell…” but when we see our transgressions as such,

158 
and make efforts to do better, this is called “development” in my teaching and discipline.’  * * *  The executions – of Sattuka and Kalidanta, Kokalika and Samudadatta – were as grim an affair as one would imagine. One part of the ritual of execution, however, was that the condemned would go to the block with a thick garland of kanavera flowers around their necks. The blood-redness of the blossoms mimicking and symbolically sanctifying the act of beheading and the blood that would surely flow forth.  Pujika was a devout regular at the Jetavana and also had the task, after her father, of preparing the garlands of kanavera for the prisoners going to meet their final punishment. It was a strange task to be entrusted with; on the one hand these were people who had committed the worst crime, that of murder, but she was duty-bound to weave the garlands in the most careful and beautiful way and to use the freshest blooms available, just as she would for an offering to the Master.  She had three young sons now and another child on the way. The boys were not very disciplined in the flower room at their workshop, making an incessant racket, and her belly felt like that of an elephant as she waddled from her mat to get another basket of florets. She heaved herself up, with an ‘oof’, and gave out another as she sad down again.  ‘In my next life I want to be a kinnari, for sure! I would get to live with flowers for a thousand years and I wouldn’t have to deal with all this noise and, hopefully, without such a swollen girth.’ She wasn’t sure about that last part – kinnaris were supposed to lay eggs and the babies would hatch  from those but she wasn’t quite sure how big the eggs were. Still, she reflected, ‘A thousand years as a flower deva would be much more comfortable, and far more delightful than this.’   * * *  At the killing ground the four condemned men were gathered, their kanavera garlands making them a matching group and levelling the social status between them – equal in death with each other as they had not been, in the eyes of society, in their lives before.  Devadatta had risked staying to see things through to the end, even as he began his next round of scheming. He reflected, as if to tell the world:– Don’t worry, I will leave. I’ll leave and go my own way, to my own place, to save my esteemed cousin the embarrassment of any suspicions about me. Gayasisa, that is where I shall reside, and perhaps there will be some who will choose to come with me. My time is not yet done.  ==*==

159 
CHAPTER 18: KRISHNA’S CHANGE OF HEART   anthic instead of bright, the whites of Krishna’s eyes matched his heart which now felt sallow and wan. He had indeed been greatly cheered by the astonishing surprise that Queen Vasabha was not only his long-lost half-kinnari daughter but also that she had been Tamba, Queen Magandiya and the killer of Amba, in the near past. Nevertheless, a layer further down, he felt lost and confused, heartsick and filled with doubts about his spiritual life. ‘Wrong, wrong, wrong… how could I get it so wrong, and for so many years?’ He was sitting under his kuti with Dusaka and Tingri. It was early morning and the day was warming up. Dusaka leant against the charpai with Tingri on his lap, dappled sunlight occasionally glinting on his bone earrings or on one of the amulets and malas tangled about his neck. His gammy leg was half-propped on his walking staff as he had settled himself in for another lengthy session of hearing Krishna’s woes.  ‘First I thought that Gavinna’s advice meant ‘live life to the full – enjoy everything’ and I made good friends with Udayin and Surama, Arittha and the Group of Six – those were good times, or at least I took them to be so then. Then,’ Krishna had well-warmed to his subject by now, ‘as you know.’ ‘I do,’ Dusaka rather pointlessly responded.  ‘As you know I saw the negative side of all that and the Master himself gave me really good advice – made me see that the idea of a “hidden teaching”, a special tantra for the advanced, was not what he had ever had in mind. I really got that!’ ‘Tremendous,’ Dusaka chimed in, with the tone of voice being more languid than excited; Tingri offered a snort which Krishna took to mean, ‘And how many years was it before you figured that out?’ Embarrassedly, he carried on, ‘Anyway, you know I’m a slow learner.’ ‘Indubitably.’ ‘But I do learn and I took the Master’s words to heart: “OK! Tighten up!” I thought, “That’s the way”. So I turned around my understanding of what Gavinna had said and decided to follow the path of austerity instead. I knew Devadatta and Maha-Kassapa were both famous for their personal standards of renunciation – despite Devadatta having received great amounts of royal patronage in the past – but since Maha-Kassapa was away in foreign parts, and since Devadatta was close by, I went to him for guidance.

160 
‘You didn’t think to ask around and see what the general impression was of his views and ways?’ Dusaka narrowed his eyes which got lost in wrinkles for a moment.  ‘To be honest,’ Krishna winced at the acknowledgement, ‘it never crossed my mind. I have a tendency to be credulous and naïve.’ ‘To be sure.’ ‘So then I get myself into this terrible mess that ends up with Matanga – my own unknown daughter – dying, before I could even appreciate who she really was and make a proper bond of father to daughter with her. I thought Devadatta’s reforms were a great way to get things back on track, both for the Sangha and for me.’ Dusaka’s pronouncedly shaggy eyebrows both rose at this, ‘Didn’t you realize, didn’t it ever occur to you that Devadatta only raised those points in order to split the Sangha? That’s why he did it. It was not out of any altruistic or spiritual intention. He made those proposals knowing that “people admire self-denial” and, more importantly, he was pretty sure that the Master would reject all of them. The rejection was what he wanted. If the Master had agreed to those changes his plan would not have worked.’ One eyebrow lowered whilst the other, over his right eye, remained raised.  ‘And he has not dropped those five suggestions either. He wriggled out of getting the blame for your daughter’s death, so now just wait and see what he does to keep sowing seeds of dissention and division.  ‘By the way,’ Dusaka gave a wry smile, I would say you needed to get back on track but the Sangha itself was and is in fine shape. Like a shady politician, Devadatta and his cronies kept promoting the big lie of “the problem of the Master getting old” – by saying it enough times they were hoping it would be taken as an acknowledged fact. However – look closely – is it really the case that the Master’s light is dimming? Yes, he has a back that pains him most of the time, but how is his mind? How stress-free is his manner? How easily does he handle thorny issues in the community?’  A light was dawning in Krishna’s mind, a realization of the degree to which he had been hoodwinked. It was sobering, embarrassing and relieving all at the same time.  He still, nevertheless, had the biggest of his doubts and debilitating frustrations to deal with. Underneath the sadness of having lost Matanga, and the joy of finding Vasabha, who was Tambaka, he was now at a loss for the direction of his spiritual life. ‘So, if it’s not the “Way of Indulgence” seeking happiness by sating the senses; and it’s not the “Way of Suppression” seeking happiness by negating and annihilating all desires, what did Gavinna mean?’ Dusaka smiled warmly as Tingri clambered off his lap and came to snuffle Krishna’s hand instead. ‘The words she spoke certainly were describing the superior mangala. Although it seems as though you were not listening closely enough at the time. Twenty-seven Rains have passed since she gave the prophecy to you but, if

161 
your mind grows less obstructed, and less prone to believing in deceptions, then things might still become clear to you.’ ‘Couldn’t you just tell me?!’ Krishna whimpered.  ‘Now where would be the fun in that?’ He compassionately grinned.  Krishna lingered expectantly hoping, almost certainly vainly, that Dusaka would shake his head, say, ‘Alright, let me spell it out for you,’ and then his troubles would be over. Even as this thought formed another took shape, saying, ‘Ha ha – very likely!’ ‘So what are the obstructions that are preventing me from recollecting and understanding clearly?’ ‘You want a list?’ Dusaka was apparently serious. ‘Alright – the general headings, as far as I can tell, are: sense-desire, grudges and ill-will, views and opinions, believing in your doubts, the conceit of identity – that you’re “this” or “that” – and its partner, the desire for defined being, lastly, and generally, ignorance – not being awake to the way things are. That about covers it.’ Krishna felt more drained and despondent at hearing such a catalogue of his painfully familiar mind-states. Dusaka saw his eyes dimming, becoming more jaundiced than ever, and his posture slumping. Tingri climbed onto Krishna’s lap and began, affectionately, to chew his fingers.  ‘The main problem, at the heart of all these, is that we so easily believe our desires, our hatreds, our opinions, our doubts, and our impressions of who we are and who we want to be. If we can stop believing in the lies the mind tells – “If I get this it will make me happy”, “This isn’t an opinion, it’s a fact” – if we can recognize the lie, the mind is well on its way to happiness and freedom.’ The voice in which Dusaka spoke this had a different tone to the jokey, wry comments he had mostly been dropping up until now – his words were heartfelt, kindly and serious.  ‘Do you want to hear about the first lie that was ever told?’ It was a rhetorical question as Dusaka was already moving his body into its ‘leaning on my staff and reciting a tale’ posture.  Krishna straightened up and Tingri perked her ears.    * * *  ‘Sing devas! Weep and learn this Gatha’s words, True they tell of the lie, the very first,  That burst upon the world, the awful stain That led the way, all untruth in its train.  *  * * ‘Upon a time, in the First Age, a king Once lived, Mahasammata was his name.  His span of life an Incalculable Aeon long – in lives past such was the norm.

162 
 ‘He was the founder of the House; his sons –  Nine generations – cascaded down to Apacara, King of Ceti, his home  Fair Sotthivati, wide-famed noble town.   ‘The King was blessed with powers, awesome strengths, Four were these: the first being he could walk Aloft, pass through the air as if on earth; Second, four deva guardians, blades unsheathed  ‘Warded him, each standing in their quarter; Third, the sweet scent of sandalwood his body Exuded at all times; the fourth, his mouth  Diffused the fragrance divine of lotus.   ‘How did the lie, the first, then come about?  The tale is complex so take careful heed.  When Apacara was a prince the royal priest Was named Kapila. He had a brother,  ‘A younger lad, friend of Apacara,  Blessed with the name of Korakalamba. One day the Prince gave promise that, when king,  His playmate Koraka would be the Priest.  ‘The old king died and Apacara then Ascended the throne of ancient Ceti.  The Royal Priest, Kapila, did not choose To step aside but then – oh, blighted day! –   ‘He gave up his post, took the pilgrim’s staff And, retiring to the forest, instead of brother,  He named his son as his successor. The  Brother Koraka was thus passed over.  ‘The King, Apacara, was not informed.  He found this out talking with Koraka,  “Are you not the Royal Priest, now Kapila’s To the forest gone?” “Nay Sire,” he replied,   ‘“He made his son the Priest; I’m set aside” “I won’t have that! The post was long promised

163 
To you.” “But Sire, how can you make that so? It is impossible, O King, for me  ‘To go against my elder brother’s choice.” “I see no block that can’t be moved, I will  From my Royal Seat, simply declare that You are the elder and that your brother  ‘Is the younger.” “But, Sire, how can you make  What is not the case into what is true?” “I shall tell a lie,” the proud king proclaimed.  Koraka knew not what this word should mean.  ‘He hedged, “When will you do this, O Great King?”  “On the seventh day from this I’ll pronounce This lie of mine unto the city folk.” Word spread like wildfire, though full many asked,  ‘“What kind of thing’s a ‘lie’? Is it yellow?  Or blue? Or some other colour?” No one Seemed to know for it was a time when the truth  Was told by all. None kenned what a “lie” might be.   ‘The many folk gathered What would this day hold?  What rash and reckless spirit Had made their king so bold?      *     *      *  ‘A madding crowd had clustered to see what  This mysterious thing could be, the King  Would now unleash upon the world. They hoped  To see the “lie” as if it were a beast  ‘Of distant provenance, or hap a spell To dazzle and impress. The King, as was His wont, clad in full regalia, stood High in the air above the multitude.   ‘To his great surprise, and anxious wonder,  The sage Kapila, airborne, joined him there.

164 
His hide of antelope he spread, quite sure His Majesty’s attention was with him.   ‘Seated on the hide he asked, “Is it true, O King, that, by a lie, you plan to make The junior the senior like that!” He snapped his fingers, looked him in the eye.  ‘“That I will shortly do, as I’ve declared,” The brazen youthful king glared in return.  “Great King,” the sage replied, “grievous indeed  Is the destruction of good qualities  ‘That such a lie will cause. A King who speaks A lie destroys right and, by right so Destroyed, that king is destroyed too, I say.” The King, undaunted by the sage’s words,   ‘Shamelessly made his statement thus, he said,  “Sir, O Sage Kapila, you are the younger And Korakalambaka the elder.” At that very moment the lie was spoke  ‘The devas four said they would no longer guard One such as this. Swords cast they at his feet. They turned their backs to him and disappeared. His breath turned foul, fume of a rotten egg.   ‘His body smelled not like sandalwood but Like an open drain. He fell, ungainly From the air and landed amid the crowd, All his powers supernatural gone.  ‘The kindly Sage Kapila, Seeing this great fall Said, “If you recant this lie Return will your powers all.”    * * * ‘Aggrieved and shamed so publicly the King Replied, “You wish only to deceive me!” Which was untrue and, so, a second lie.  Then, strange as it may sound but this era

165 
 ‘Was the First Age itself when much magic Filled the air, and fell and wondrous effects Of earth and sky and water, were not rare, More common than today. What then occurred,   ‘With the second lie, the fallen king was Pulled into the earth, up to his ankles.  He restated his first lie once again,  Again and again; each time he was pulled  ‘Deeper and deeper – to his knees and then  To his hips; to his navel and then, yes, He repeated the heinous lie again For the sixth time and was swallowed up to  ‘The breast. He paid no heed to this, a clear Message of where his lie would surely go.  One last time the Sage Kapila cried out,  “One word of truth, O King and all’s restored.”  ‘The haughty King was strangely undeterred,  Pronouncing his bold-faced lie he stated,  “You are the junior and Koraka Is the elder.” This was the seventh time.  ‘And lo! His head slipped below the earth and He was never seen again. In that life King Apacara was the liar; he Reappeared this life as Devadatta.  ‘All this goes to show that lies Unless retracted, recognized,  Lead to misery, but pain Ends when truth is realized.’   * * *  The search in Kapilavatthu continued, Maggot and her friends focused on the palace, but since virtually every human could not see them, and since most of the devas, yakkhas, and various sprites did not apparently pay much attention to the humans, it seemed to be turning into a bit of a lost cause. They knew what Hiri and Siri had heard, and they were getting to know the areas of the palace and its grounds

166 
where the slave families lived, so the field of possibilities was not too wide. One of the problems, however, was that many of the slaves had to work at the more rank tasks – emptying outhouses of their night-soil, mucking out stables, tending the cooking pots and fires in the kitchen, carrying fuel for the smiths, which often included dried cow-dung along with wood and charcoal – and the intense smells could easily mask any natural fragrance that Tambaka might have had.  Maggot’s eye, and all the others’ too, became acutely tuned to looking for a woman with a coppery skin and of about twenty-five sun-turnings or so – she was starting to see them everywhere, only to be disappointed again and again – the person, on a closer look, turned out to be too young or too old, or it was a long-haired boy, not a woman… it was hard to bear.  Finally, a breakthrough came when Bee was frustratedly endeavouring to delve into the dark corners of the kitchen. Jammed into an unswept nook, beside one of the low shelves in a store-room, Bee found a long hair, just one, but when she held it up to the light it was auburn, and the smell… ‘I think it’s hers… Am I fooling myself again?’ she wondered. She went out into the courtyard where there was fresh air and no breeze, she held it close up to her nose and inhaled.  ‘Yes! It’s hers but where is she?’ Bee raced to summon Maggot and the others and explained where she had found this special strand.  ‘So, kitchen, kitchen… do any celestials hang around here at all?’ Maggot wondered. ‘Well,’ Rhamba offered, ‘it’s not likely – it’s noisy and smelly and the virtue of the kitchen folk is not so lofty as to attract too many refined ones.’ ‘I saw a sprite or two round there,’ Gumbiya mentioned, ‘there’s some, deffo… er, definitely.’ The whole group raced to crowd into the halls and store-rooms of the kitchen. It took a little hunting but, eventually, they found a couple of kitchen-sprites nestled among the sacks of rice in one of the store sheds. They were tiny and, unsurprisingly, were both a bit overawed by these larger spirits and annoyed to be interrupted – they lived on the subtle fragrance given off by rice-grains and their repast had been disturbed.  Maggot tried to be as unintimidating as possible to these small and slightly shy, fellow bhumma-devas. They wore what looked like homespun garments woven from spider-webs or cornsilk, or perhaps (more likely) threads drawn from rice plants in their ripened season. She knelt down before them and held up the precious hair that Bee had found.  ‘Excuse us please for intruding upon you,’ she politely began, ‘but I lost my child many sun-turnings ago and, when I heard she might be here in the palace, I came straight to this place, with my friends, to see if we could find her. She was hatched perhaps some twenty-five sun-turnings ago. This is a hair from her head, so we are sure she was here, but can you tell us if you recognize her fragrance? She is half-human, half-kinnari, but when she was a babe, she had a flower aroma like mine, of the Jambu.’

167 
The two little sprites seemed to get interested quickly – it was certainly the most exciting thing that had happened all day – and they gave the hair a good sniffing. It was rope-like in their tiny hands. After mumbling to each other in their own dialect for a bit, one of them spoke up.  ‘Know ’er – sure we do. That’s from the girl Vasabha but we didn’t know she was half-kinnari – that’s a first ’ere – as far as I know… did yoo ever ’ear o’ that?’ The other shook her head, with her minute cap of rice-papery material wagging vigorously.  ‘She’s gone now, been gone about ten sun-turnings, maybe nine…’ Maggot took a sharp breath, ‘Gone as in…?’ ‘Nah! She in’t dead, not as far as I know, but she was part of the crowd of kids that Yasa and Nagamunda had – they’ve got a pile of gran’ kids now. Ain’t seen ’er since that big king from the sarf came ’ere, back ven, ’baht ten, maybe fifteen or more sun-turns ago. I ain’t seen ’er since they all left so she prob’ly went wiv ’em. Don’ know any more – I don’t mind the comin’s ’n’ goin’s of the big folk very much – but she in’t bin arahnd the kitchen or the sheds since then.’ The little being was enjoying being useful and having some real information to give them all.  ‘F’r a human she was nice – she did always smell good, come ter fink of it – not like rice,’ as if that was plainly the best scent in the world, ‘but pretty. Under all the reeks o’ the kitchen, ’n’ the ’eat and smoke, you c’ld tell when she was abaht, come ter fink of it. She couldn’t see the likes of us, though,’ he turned to his rice-capped partner, again she shook her head, ‘Nah, no sign that she or any of the kitchen folk could see us. They have an eye for rats and mice, no’ us!’ Salassa stepped closer, ‘This “big king” who came to visit, any chance you can recall a name, or some of the things they wore? There’s a lot of kings and queens south of here.’  The two sprites went into a huddle once more. The female one then spoke, ‘It were Kosala – I ’member vat. Kept goin’ on abaht how big and rich it was – Kosala deffo.’ Gumbiya was pleased to hear his own accent; the fact it was coming from a being about one hundredth of his size was even more endearing. He liked these kitchen-sprites.  ‘So! Savatthi! That’s where we need to go.’ Maggot was excited and the whole group were grinning. ‘We’re closing in… and we know her adoptive parents are Yasa and Nagamunda.’ ‘Well, ’e’s gone, the dad, died a few years back, but Naga’s ’ere, plus ’er brood, but y’ won’t be able to ask ’em any fink, she won’t be able to see or hear y’. Best off you head to Savatthi, dahn sarf, and see if y’ can find y’ girl in the kitchens there, or doin’ summat else. That’s my bes’ guess.’ ‘Thank you! Thank you so much! This is all wonderful news! Is there anything we can do for you by way of a favour, to express our gratitude?’ Maggot was amazed and delighted by all the help they’d given.

168 
‘Not really,’ the male started, then his partner whispered something to him. ‘Oh yeah. We live on plant smells, ’n we don’ see many kinnari ’ere indoors so, ’ow would you two,’ indicating Bee and Maggot, ‘like to breathe on us. That’ll be a nice addition to our rice – which is course our main – but please, give us a blast.’ Both Bee and Maggot were happy to do this for them for they knew that their breath had natural invigorating, curative powers. Where Maggot’s breath had the fragrance of fruity Jambu, rose-apples, that of Bee had the scent of the red or purple lotus. The two kinnaris leaned forward and gently exhaled in the faces of the sprites. ‘Whoah! That’s the stuff! Eh?!’ The two drank in the fragrant airs, blown into their faces like a warm wind from the south.  ‘Fank you!’ They were both a bit giddy from the blasts, ‘T’riffick – jes’ the ticket. By the way, me name’s Cob and ’er’s Green-Blossom.’  They then made their farewells and the group gathered in their now-familiar courtyard – ‘So – Savatthi and the royal palace. Let’s go!’   * * *  Krishna was sitting at the Master’s feet. It was an informal time, late in the morning after most of the Sangha had dispersed following the communal meal. Ananda was there, as were a few junior monks and novices. Devadatta and his group were keeping to themselves and rumour had it that he would soon depart, if he had not done so already. Surama had been drawing ever closer to Devadatta and, seemingly now that Krishna was so deeply buried in a funk, the Elder had forgotten his erstwhile friendship with him. Little did Krishna know that Mara considered his work done there – at least for a while.  To the degree that he noticed at all, Krishna was surprised that Surama was still firmly with that group despite the dire rumours that circulated following Matanga’s death and the trial. One might have thought that anyone in their right mind would give Devadatta a wide berth by now however, that right-mindedness was, apparently, still rare in the world and the miscreant Elder continued to draw a substantial coterie of sycophantic followers into his sphere of influence.  The Buddha was now speaking and Krishna was paying close attention.  ‘There are these two extremes that should not be followed, by one who has gone forth and who seeks the good: the Way of Indulgence – which is low, coarse, vulgar, ignoble and unprofitable – and the Way of Self-Torture, Suppression, which is painful, ignoble, and unprofitable.  ‘In the former Way an individual seeks happiness and fulfilment through trying to satiate the senses and maximise sense-pleasure; in the latter Way an individual seeks happiness and fulfilment through trying to negate the senses and to get beyond all feeling; or they follow the mistaken belief that painful, racking, piercing feelings on their own are intrinsically liberating; or they hold that the more pain that is endured, the closer to enlightenment and liberation they are drawing.

169 
‘Both of these are wrong, deluded views. By avoiding these two extremes, the Tathagata realized the Middle Way, which gives vision and understanding, which leads to calm, penetrative insight and enlightenment, to Nirvana.’ Krishna was enraptured, he could not restrain himself from blurting out, ‘This is exactly it! Those are precisely the two paths I got stuck on – the first one for much longer than the second – amazing, Venerable Sir! This is the first time I have heard this expressed so clearly!’ The small group which sat clustered around the Buddha went quiet. The Master himself pausing before continuing. The pause became more pregnant and was drifting into being an uncomfortable silence when one of the younger bhikkhus spoke up.  ‘Venerable Krishna, you do realize that what the Blessed One just said was the words with which he began his first discourse, that which set in motion the Wheel of Dharma over thirty-five Rains ago. We recite those words often.’ Once more Krishna experienced a mixture of both embarrassment at his folly and inattentiveness, and delight that this marvellous teaching – so tailor-made to his own condition, was at last sinking in.  ‘I am shy to admit,’ Krishna replied, ‘that it has taken me twenty-five Rains to put two-and-two together. Please accept my apologies for being a student so slowly wise, Venerable Sir.’ The Master accepted the apology in silence, with a slight inclination of his head. ‘If I might ask,’ Krishna continued, ‘with respect to the Middle Way – that does not just mean being indulgent half the time and suppressive the other half, that much I can understand straight away, but what exactly is this Middle-ness and how is it found and embodied?’ ‘You are not so slow to learn, Krishna,’ the Master smiled wryly, ‘there are those among my disciples who for years have held to the “indulge half the time and austerity the other half” understanding, or mis-understanding of these words. Let me demonstrate.’  Near Ananda there were some offerings that had been made earlier. In amongst these were a few balls of twine. Ananda, being quick on the uptake, had already drawn out a length about a forearm long and had cut it, being familiar with the example that he was sure the Master was about to give. There was a small dried and hollowed gourd that held balm, which had a tight lid, sitting by the Master’s seat. Ananda tied the string around the neck of the gourd so that it made a neat little pendulum. He offered it in both hands to the Master.  In his left hand the Buddha held up the end of the string and in his right, he held the gourd below it. He moved the gourd to the left, holding his upper hand still.  ‘This represents the extreme of Indulgence in Sense-pleasure’; he then moved the pendulum to the right, ‘this represents the extreme of Indulgence in Self-Mortification, Suppression. The Middle Way is not just half-way between the two

170 
extremes’; he now moved the gourd to the nadir of the arc, ‘rather, the Middle Way is represented by the point at the centre, the hub where the pendulum pivots from. He joggled his left hand. ‘This is the mysterious Middle that those who have gone forth are tasked to find.’ Saying nothing further, the Buddha neatly rolled up the twine and gourd and handed them to Ananda. It was plainly the end of this informal gathering so the assembled monks and novices bowed three times and the Master returned to the Gandha-kuti.   * * *  Krishna’s heart was greatly refreshed by this teaching and he resolved to make the realization of this Middle Way his new goal. Still, however, there was a niggle, like a name that he just could not remember, or a verse of the teachings that he knew was getting wrong. ‘This is a clear and wonderful new direction but… what did Gavinna mean? Well, it seems I’m never going to be able to get Dusaka or the Master to explain it and, if I haven’t figured it out after twenty-five Rains, I suppose I should just forget it, or at least leave it aside. Oh well, never mind – begin again!’  ==*==

171 
CHAPTER 19: PUJIKA’S DELIGHT  ujika had moved to a small village, called Katara, to the south of Savatthi, on the occasion of her marriage. She had been very happy about this for, even though she was leaving her family home, this village of her husband was even closer to the Jetavana than the city of Savatthi. It was a fine little place to raise their growing family and it meant that she could easily visit the Jetavana every day, even with her three little boys and her expanded belly. One of her delights was, in the hour before dawn, to rise before the rest of the family, sometimes with her third boy on her hip, and go to the village Assembly Hall to tidy it up and to prepare for possible visitors. Katara was of modest size but, because so many folk would come from far and wide to visit the Jetavana, and would need a place to find and purchase offerings to take to the Master and the Sangha, they were quite prosperous there. The Hall was a finely built shelter where the village council would meet but – and more importantly for Pujika – it was also the place where Elders from the Sangha, (usually the monks rather than the nuns since the monastery of the female community was close to the walls of Savatthi) would sometimes come to sit and receive alms.  Katara was only three or four bow-shots from the gates of the Jetavana so, when any of the agèd or infirm Sangha members wanted to go on the alms-round, or if there was a special occasion in the village such as a marriage or a baby blessing, they would use the Hall of Assembly as a place to gather. On very rare and wonderful days the Master would take this shorter route and come to Katara village himself and stop a while at the Hall.  Pujika had made it her daily custom to sweep the Hall as the light came back into the world, to shake out the mats and set them straight, and to fill the jugs with fresh water. The place was close to her home and the garland workshop, and it would put her heart at ease to know that, should any of the Sangha, on their early morning alms-round, need to stop and rest, the place was always ready. She would customarily bring some flowers to deck the seats and shrine as votive offerings, often draping these around the neck of her snoozing child as she made her way there. On the special days when an event was planned, if they knew the Elders were going to receive an offering there or if the nuns or monks were going to perform a blessing, she would be extra diligent and make abundant flower offerings, to add to those of other devotees.  As time had gone by images had appeared in her mind of celestial beings, wreathed in flowers and smiling at her, most often one bright round face clad all in the reds of the kanavera.

172 
What had begun as a joke to herself that, ‘In my next life I’d like to be kinnari’ was transformed into a powerful feeling of, ‘I know her! She’s my real belovèd! I want to be with her!’ She was married to a good man named Khumbha, after his pot belly, who had given her these three fine children – soon to be four – but she could not say that her heart sang in his company. He was a good garlandier. He knew his trade but he had little faith in the Master, or at least a lack of interest in things beyond food, the family and his business, and he would hit the palm-toddy regularly too. With each additional child his consumption of this liquor had seemed to increase.  She loved her children, as any mother would and should, but somehow she felt that she didn’t really know them, at least not in the way she seemed to know and long for this ruddy being that she experienced in these momentary flashes. She sometimes thought that these visions were a complete delusion, that she was merely caught up in a fantasy on account of the unsatisfactory nature of the world, just as the Master had spoken of so frequently. But then… then… these were so clear! These intuitions had a wholly different tone to other everyday longings, like waiting for the green rice to come in the Rainy season, to make sweet desserts, or the wait for certain blossoms to open up to make a special offering of some kind. This longing, this connection, was of a whole other order – as if her ordinary life was like the greys and sepias of the hour before dawn and her imagined life with this red-clad kinnari was like the full bright array of the colours of broad day.  All this had led her to make the resolution, whenever she made a flower offering or swept the Hall and prepared the seats, that she would declare, ‘May this offering assist me in being reborn with my belovèd.’ The folk from the village and the nuns and monks who came to the Hall, or whom she met in the Jetavana, were impressed by such a devoted wife, as they took it, ‘She’s a veritable Sita, so devoted to her husband! Although so busy and hard-working, still she thinks only of him.’  ‘You should be called Pati-pujika – ‘Husband-honourer’ – not just Pujika!’ ‘If only they knew,’ she reflected, however she never corrected them and sometimes she even affirmed their misunderstanding, saying, ‘Yes! I love him very much,’ to account for her blushes. She didn’t like to be deceitful but somehow, despite the dishonesty, it seemed to be worth it. At first she had conflicted thoughts, doubting whether she was being really fair, or kind, or if it made her a bad mother but, in each moment when making such offerings nothing could quell her heart’s desire: ‘May I be reborn with my belovèd!’   * * *   Devadatta had not yet departed from the Jetavana and his scheming to bring down the Buddha was not over, by any means. As the New Moon day approached, following the trial and execution of his confrères, Devadatta made his way among as many of the monks and novices as he could manage. Despite usually having a

173 
powerful presence that no one could ignore he was able, somehow, to draw himself in and move through the community with great subtlety, like a shadow. Customarily the rumour of his tread rode ahead of him – people knew that he was arriving – but he, at this time, traversed the forest and appeared in the different groups of the monks’ dwellings like a soft, hardly perceptible drift of air.  As he came upon each separate section of the Sangha he told them, ‘Friends, I went to the Blessed One and requested that he institute five reforms that I and my close advisors had compiled. He refused. I and my associates now undertake to live by these five standards. Let anyone among you who agrees, who is in favour of these five reforms, take a voting ticket.’ These tickets had been made from sections of dry ola leaf. Devadatta carried a bagful with him.  Again and again Devadatta endeavoured to use his powers of persuasion on the various assemblies dwelling in the environs of the Jetavana – there were the monks who were expert reciters of the teachings, ones who sat together in meditation, ones who were experts in the discipline; then there were ones who gathered on account of a shared language, a group from Uttarakuru, another from Vamsa, a few from Kamboja in the east, as well as from Sunaparanta; there were many Magadhans and Kosalans, as one would expect, and a large group of Vajjians, from Vesali. Of all these monks it was only those from the Vajjian Confederacy that gave him any attention at all, the rest turned away as one, uninterested or wary of Devadatta’s by now egregious reputation.  The Vajjians were a young crowd and had all gone forth together, more than a hundred of them, and so far had had little instruction or introduction to the discipline. They listened to Devadatta and it all made good sense. They believed, ‘This is the Dharma, this is the teaching, perfectly explained by this great Elder.’ So, taking the voting tickets, they naïvely sided with Devadatta. This group, along with his pair of older cronies and Surama, were the only ones who chose to go with him. He had now caused a schism in the Sangha.  Once he was convinced that he had made as much impact as he could, he addressed all his adherents, old and new, ‘Come friends, let us go to Gayasisa.’   * * *  News of this plan spread rapidly as Devadatta had made a point of them all setting out for the south-east on the Uposatha Day of the Full Moon, before the recitation of the patimokkha had been held. This was a deliberate affront to the Master and the rest of the monastic community as all Sangha members were required to take part in the recitation of the Rule on the New and Full Moon days, unless prevented by fire, flood or sickness, or other extreme circumstances. The schism having been deliberately made by Devadatta, he was now underscoring this by making a public display of the division.  Some time after, when Sariputta and Maha-Moggallana were discussing the issue with the Master, he spoke very directly to them both, ‘Sariputta, Moggallana,

174 
do you feel the same compassion for those young bhikkhus that I do? Go and see if you can help them, before they come to ruin.’  They needed no persuasion and set off for Gayasisa at once.  On their departure, this having been so sudden and not widely discussed, one young bhikkhu, who was sitting near the Buddha the day after they had departed, burst into tears. The Master asked him, ‘Why are you weeping, bhikkhu?’  ‘How could I not weep, Venerable Sir?’ He sobbed, ‘the great disciples Sariputta and Moggallana, having gone to Devadatta, will be won over by his teaching too.’  The Buddha smiled, ‘It is impossible, friend, it cannot happen that Sariputta and Maha-Moggallana will go over to Devadatta’s side. Rather, they will teach the young bhikkhus who have gone with the schismatic and will help them to see the path that leads to the truth, to enlightenment.’  The Master’s clear and quiet, utter confidence that this would be the case brought reassurance to the anxious monk. He knew the Master’s words were to be trusted, not just because of optimistic thinking, or believing an authority figure, but with the wordless ending of a doubt that comes with intuitive insight. As when one puts on a shoe that fits, the knowledge of that fitting is there before one thinks, ‘It’s a good fit,’ so the veracity of the Buddha’s words was known in his young heart.    * * *  At Gayasisa, some Moons later, Devadatta was sitting expounding his version of the Dharma to a large assembly. It was a moonlit night and, to his surprise, he saw Sariputta and Maha-Moggallana emerging from the shadows and appear in a bright patch between the shadows at the edge of the forest glade where his community had gathered.  He was so excited by this unexpected visitation that he halted his talk and declared, ‘Look! The Dharma is so well taught and explicated by me that even the monk Gotama’s chief disciples have come over to my teaching!’ His eyes were aflame and he lifted his arms in welcome.  The words had hardly left his mouth before Katamoraka moved up close beside him and said, ‘Venerable Sir! Do not trust these two! They are up to no good. They would not come here unless they had some evil design in mind!’ ‘Nonsense,’ Devadatta dismissed him with a gesture, ‘they are welcome since they have come over to my teaching.’ He called out to the two great friends, ‘Come, noble ones, Sariputta, please sit beside me.’ He indicated the space next to him as he shifted over on the broad Dharma seat. Sariputta declined the invitation and modestly sat on a mat close to Devadatta’s feet; his companion Moggallana did likewise.  Devadatta then carried on with his discourse but, after a while, mimicking his erstwhile teacher the Buddha, he looked down at Sariputta and said, ‘Friend, the

175 
Sangha is still alert although it is far into the night, please allow a talk on the Dharma to occur to you. My back is paining me, so I will stretch it.’ Sariputta consented. Devadatta climbed down from the seat and went to the place in the forest where he had his sleeping mat. He had eschewed the use of buildings, as part of his five standards, so all these monks lived out under the trees. He spread his robes down on the mat and stretched out in the lion’s posture, on his right side, but he fell asleep unmindfully and was soon deeply lost in slumber.  Back in the glade where the Sangha was gathered, the moon was high in the sky as Sariputta addressed the group. He used all his intuitive powers to discern the interests and needs of his listeners and – being a master of eloquent and analytical speech – most of the crowd remained attentive and drank in all he had to say, after Sariputta had stepped down from the seat, Moggallana followed him and gave a talk as well. While Sariputta was from a refined brahmin family, and was golden-skinned and of gentle countenance, his close friend was swart, big-eyed and of imposing mien. He was from a brahmin family too, and from the same village of Nalanda, but their appearances could not have been much more different.  When Sariputta had been speaking the whole group of younger monks had realized the path to Stream-entry; by the time that Moggallana had finished his teaching every last one of them had reached the full fruition of that stage. They were all beyond doubt, destined to enlightenment and independent of others with respect to their insight.  Sariputta discerned this comprehensive change in the community and, looking to Maha-Moggallana, with his vast psychic powers, to double-check on this, his friend confirmed this state of realization of the assembly with a silent nod. Sariputta then rose and faced the group saying, ‘Bhikkhus, we are going back to the Blessèd One. Whoever upholds the teaching of the Master, let them come with us.’ Thus the two Elders rose up and began to lead the large group of Vajjian bhikkhus on the long walk back to Savatthi.  During the talks being given by Sariputta and Moggallana the two close disciples of Devadatta that remained nearby – Katamoraka-tissaka and Khandadevi-putta – had dozed off, leaning against a rock at the edge of the area. They were both snoring gently through all of this. Surama, meanwhile had made himself scarce, slinking away as soon as he discerned that the noble pair were arriving. He knew that Maha-Moggallana would detect his true nature very easily.  When Katamoraka and Khandadevi-putta awoke, as rosy-fingered dawn was tinting the sky, they quickly divined that all the new recruits had vanished, along with the visiting Elders. Katamoraka raced to Devadatta’s bower and roused him, as he was still fast asleep. ‘Wake up! Wake up Venerable Sir! I told you not to trust them! Those two had indeed come here with evil on their minds. All the new monks have been led away by them – the whole lot!’ Then and there, even though he was just awake, Devadatta was so shamed and enraged, apoplectic, that hot blood burst from his mouth.

176 
   * * *  When the party had completed their journey from Gayasisa, which was a vigorous walk of about twenty days, the Master greeted them all warmly. He insisted that they had not left the Order by siding with Devadatta in this first ever schism in the Sangha, and that, after confessing the offence, and that offence having been acknowledged, they should be considered as members of the community in good standing, like everyone else.  ‘So, Sariputta, how did Devadatta act when he saw the two of you?’ the Buddha enquired.  Sariputta recounted the scene as it had happened, then described how Devadatta had said, aping the expression used so often by the Master himself, ‘Sariputta, my back is paining me, I will go and stretch it. The assembly is wide awake, please let a Dharma teaching occur to you.’ As he recalled an incident from the ancient past, the Master’s countenance took on a soft, compassionate expression. ‘Once, bhikkhus,’ he began, ‘there were some elephants living near a big pond in the forest. The adult elephants would wade into the water, pull up lotus stalks with their trunks, then would carefully rinse the roots of them so that they were free of mud and pebbles, then they would eat them.  ‘Some young calves, baby elephants, not knowing better, also waded into the pond and, similarly plucked up lotus plants. Instead of washing them clean, however, they chewed them up, mud and pebbles and all, so they incurred deadly suffering and died on account of that. So too, bhikkhus, Devadatta will die miserably through imitating me. ‘Devadatta is overcome, his mind so obsessed with gain, fame and honour, and he is badly influenced by his ongoing evil wishes and the friends that echo those with him. He will inevitably go to the states of woe, to hell, for the duration of the aeon, on account of these evil wishes, evil friends and through stopping halfway with the mere worldly distinction of supernormal powers. Indeed now, owing to his various heinous acts, these powers have now been lost to him.’ It was a sobering statement. The Master left it at that for all to ponder.    * * *  Pujika was getting close to the end of her confinement. She was very used to it by now as this was the fourth time in five years that she had gone through the process.  She was delighted to hear that a group of nuns had been invited to receive their meal offering at the Hall of Assembly that day; moreover it was rumoured that the great Arahant Elders Uppalavanna and Khema would be leading them. She went out early in the morning, before dawn, despite her heavy condition, and covered the votive shrine with fragrant garlands of that season’s flowers. She shook out the mats

177 
and surrounded the seats, especially the first two, with strings of marigolds and her favourite kanaveras. As ever she intoned, as she made these offerings, ‘May this act of devotion be a cause for me being reborn with my belovèd.’ Day was dawning, as it did swiftly in those regions. Pujika waited just long enough to see the colours of her garlands in the full light of day since, when one worked by lamplight or candles or in the silver of moon or starlight, one never saw the real shades the flowers had and how they mixed together. In the morning brightness she assessed how it all looked and made a few rearrangements.  A few of the other villagers and regulars from the Jetavana had arrived by now. Pujika had intended to hurry back to her home and to bring some food offerings as well. She sat for a moment as she greeted the others.  ‘Jes’ getting my breath back before I go.’ ‘You don’t look well,’ one of the women observed, ‘you alright dear? You’re close to poppin’ there. Take care.’  Pujika did head home but was sweating in the late Hot Season, early Rains weather. She didn’t want to miss the opportunity to see and make offerings to the enlightened nuns who were due that day so she forced herself to return to the Hall in time for the presentation of the meal offering. When she got there the place was crowded but, as she climbed the steps, the people parted a little to let her find a good spot. She leant against a pillar, glad to have the prop, as she was still sweating. The villagers nearby kept glancing her way.  ‘You alright, dear? You look ’orrid. Shouldn’t you go ’ome?’ ‘In a bit… I want to be here,’ she answered, a little weakly but brooking no discussion.  She joined the other lay folk in determining the Refuges and Precepts and was focusing as best she could through the Dharma talk that the Theri Khema gave. Uppalavanna seemed to notice her; she caught her eye once or twice and a look of interest, concern, mixed with a wry serenity, etched her expression. Pujika wondered for a moment, ‘Why’s she looking at me like that? Like she knows something I don’t. Still,’ as she closed her eyes again, ‘she’s known for having powers like Moggallana so she prob’ly knows mos’ things about everyone.’ She listened to the teaching with all the focus she could, sweat now having drenched her clothing, she stayed this way until the whole event was over.  ‘Here,’ a close by friendly voice said, ‘let’s help you down.’ They descended the few steps from the Hall to the dusty path. ‘I’m gon’ ter get you home girl – y’r’ close to y’r time, ’ave you got help there?’ Pujika was grateful for the arm to lean on, she reassured her, ‘Yes, two of my sisters are here from the city, or they’ll be here soon.’ She smiled, genuinely pleased to have had the help. ‘Thank you! I don’t reckon I would have been good to do that on my own. This one is tougher than my others.’ ‘What number’s this one then, three?!’ ‘Four,’ Pujika gave a half-smile, ‘they seem to keep springin’ up each year, like the annuals.’

178 
She was glad to be home.    * * *  The sun was setting and her sisters had not come. Her contractions were strong and she felt it could be any moment that her next child would be born. Her husband had, as he put it, ‘Jes’ gone to the toddy-shop for a nip.’ Each birth had been slightly easier and quicker than the one before but she had a feeling this one would be different. Her one-year-old was sleeping in the baby hammock, her three and four year-olds were by her bed, unusually quiet, watching their mother’s face in her travails.  ‘Here it comes!’ she cried, ‘you two had better be ready, you’re the only help I’ve got today.’ She was trying to be encouraging through the sweat and tears.
