In her mind, over and over, she dedicated this birth to her now single-pointed aim, ‘May whatever good comes from this birth be dedicated to me being reborn with my belovèd.’ She reached the end of the sentence for the umpteenth time and, on the word ‘belovèd’, the fourth boy was born and Pujika breathed her last.    * * *  She opened her eyes, immediately knowing ‘I’m not in Katara… I’m… where? What…?’ She looked down at her body and saw filmy threads had settled upon her, silken ribbons of flower-petals covered the length of her frame. She was by a lotus pond and there was music in the air, and a loud rhythmic beat, she could feel the throb of drumming.  ‘I’m back, I didn’t go to the realm of my belovèd with the kinnaris after all. I know this place, I think, but I’m tired. Rest, just for a bit.’ She closed her eyes again.  She didn’t know how long she’d been asleep but the sun had moved higher in the sky as if it was now late morning, perhaps the afternoon. She felt well rested and decided to risk standing, then realizing she could float on the air, she drifted, like a strand of spider-web on a breeze, towards the music and the pulse of activity in the near distance.  As she approached she could see hundreds of devas gathered. Half were high up in the scented air, dancing, dodging through the branches of the jewel-flowered trees, picking and tossing the blossoms down. These were being caught by the other female devata who were then decking a princely figure, who was plainly the centre of things, from head to toe with the flowers.  ‘Ninka!’ A familiar voice rang in her ears, piercing through the rhythms and melodies that were so intense here. ‘Ninka! You’re back!’ It was that vermillion clad kinnari from her dreams, her visions; was it her belovèd? The voice said, ‘It’s you! It’s really you.’ The red-robed figure raced towards her.

179 
The newly-arisen one could not fix on a name for this bright, rushing being but it was her. ‘She called me “Ninka”’ – she whispered, knowing the sound of it somehow, and her heart was flooded with joy. She turned to move towards the kanavera-coloured presence now beaming at her when, behind her, the voice of the flower-bedecked deva prince boomed, ‘Ninka my dear – we haven’t seen you since morning. Where have you been!?’    ==*== -*-

180 
CHAPTER 20:  PRINCE VIDUDABHA VISITS  HIS ANCESTRAL HOME   idudhabha had been pestering his mother, and occasionally his father, since he was seven Rains old. He had seen that the other children of the royal household – his half-sister Princess Vajiri and the daughters and sons of the noble families of Kosala – all received presents from their maternal as well as their paternal grandparents whereas he never did. Toy horses, elephants, play-swords and shields, things he envied came to his companions in this way but not to him.  This was not to say that he was not showered with gifts from his royal father’s mother, and his great-grandmother who was still alive although very deaf and a little loose in thought, however these ‘women’s presents’ were not like the ones the other boys got from their grandfathers. King Pasenadi’s father, King Mahakosala, had passed away long ago, so Vidudabha had received no sharp jewelled daggers, or bows with a spring to make arrows go a decent distance, or any keen-pointed arrows for that matter. He was aggrieved.  Over and over again he would complain, ‘Mother, the other boys and girls get many presents from their maternal grandparents but I never do. Are you an orphan? Don’t you have a mother and father?’ Vasabha, who often found it hard to love her petulant and demanding, entitled son had become used to this appeal and always countered him with such as, ‘My dear, my parents are Sakyan royals and they live a long way off, below the Himavant, that’s why they never send you anything.’ The prince was not satisfied with this but it would end the issue, for a time.  When he reached the age of sixteen Rains his status had changed from that of a child to not just an adult but, in a show of confidence in his only royal born son, the King had appointed Prince Vidudabha as commander-in-chief of the army as well.  The Prince was no longer so interested in boyish presents from grandparents – he had plentiful supplies of all the clothes, weapons and ornaments that an heir to the throne of the great kingdom of Kosala could wish for – but he was eager to go out into the world and he was ever intrigued by the family of his royal mother:– Why do they never visit? Why had they never even sent any messages? Weren’t they the family of the Buddha himself? Why were visits from the Sakyan royal family never made? Other folk from the north came by – Koliyans, Sakyans and Kullutans – so the journey could not be so arduous, so why never his grandfather, Prince Mahanama, and his grandmother, the Princess Maddarupi? It was mysterious.

181 
Soon after his installation as the head of the army and his adulthood had been formally declared he asked the King and Queen, ‘Honoured Royal Parents! I have long wished to meet the other half of my Royal Family. I have been more than well provided for, educated and trained in military arts here in Kosala. I am surrounded by admirable family and fine friends, but I have felt an increasingly irksome absence as I have grown up. Please! Mother, Father! Your Majesties! Please allow me to visit the Sakyan kingdom and meet my Royal Mother’s esteemed family there – my own blood and heritage.’ Vidudabha had taken long hours to prepare his speech and, since he was not gifted in oratory or in verbal persuasion beyond raising his voice and grimacing, he had brought in the court poet and had enlisted his aid with the wording of his appeal. It seemed to be working. King Pasenadi and Queen Vasabha leaned into each other to whisper a discussion. The Queen, after a few moments, turned to the Prince with her most charming smile, and said, ‘But Vidu, what would you do there? I don’t think it’s really worth the bother.’ She was a bit desperate as, foolishly, she had become somewhat complacent and, since for quite a while her son had not been asking about her family – when would they visit, or could he go there one day – she had assumed that he had dismissed the idea altogether. She had not realized he had been saving things up for this moment so she was not prepared. As the words left her lips she realized, ‘Well, “something to do” there is beside the point, it’s the going there and meeting the people that matters.’  ‘I would meet my family, of course, mother.’ The Prince suppressed a smirk as this was an argument that would be hard indeed to contend with, family and bloodlines being held so sacred in the Sakyan world, as he had learned in his studies.  His parents leaned into their huddle once again, the King shrugging and the Queen dropping her gaze momentarily. Finally they both turned to face him and the King pronounced, in formal tones and with a smile, ‘Prince Vidudabha, my general, we have long overlooked this aspect of your life and we acknowledge that such a visit to your Royal Sakyan family is long overdue.  ‘Accordingly, not only will we sanction a visit by you to the north-east, to Kapilavatthu the capital of the Sakyan Kingdom, but also we ask you to head a formal embassy to the Royal Court there. You shall travel with a full troop of elephants and horses, and five platoons of foot soldiers will accompany you. I shall offer Royal Gifts to Prince Mahanama and…’ here the memory of King Pasenadi faltered, not being able to recall the Royal Spouse, or Spouses’ names, ‘…er… his wives, and I ask that you bear these offerings from the House of Kosala.’ Prince Vidudabha was pleased, mightily pleased.  * * *

182 
Proud and big, radiant in the armour he had been gifted for the attainment of his rank as head of the army, Vidudabha strode about the barracks and through the palace store-rooms endeavouring to put the stamp of his authority on the embassy in all its dimensions.  Seasoned victuallers, quartermasters, stewards and armourers, to say nothing of the maids, pages and squires, were quick to perk up at his approach even though, once past, they all tended to mock his haughty inflated manner.  ‘’E’s ’ardly shavin’ and ’e thinks ’e knows how to pack a waggon,’ the grizzling head of stables grumbled. ‘You should hear ’im tell me what food for the road we should take,’ the cook was unimpressed, ‘’e’s hardly ever left the city district and ’e’s an expert on campfire cookin’’; the theme was echoed all around.  Despite, rather than because of, the Prince’s ministrations, the embassy was readied and all the regal gifts were wrapped and packed before the next Full Moon. Every sign was there that they would be leaving soon and Vasabha knew that a rider had been sent already to inform the Sakyans of the intended visit. She was anxious about the adventure for all sorts of reasons, fearful of the outcome of her son’s visit when the Sakyan nobility were all clearly aware of who his mother really was, not to mention the widespread gossip among the commonfolk. Her having been a slave was an open secret, the servants and workers around the palace having witnessed a brand-new, full-grown princess suddenly appearing from nowhere. ‘Oh look! Lady Madda jes’ gave birth to a sixteen-y’r-ol’. Tha’s clever.’ She had heard the jokes even before they had left Kapilavatthu.  At a moment of great anxiety, as her thoughts ran freely through a list of tortuous possibilities, there was a sudden earthquake that shook her chambers. In the sudden shock she felt it was her fate coming to meet her; her bad karma ripening and the earth about to open up and swallow her. But the shocks subsided quickly and the palace rang with a vibrant silence. She was still safe, for now. She pulled Kesini in and, in great secrecy, managed to find a messenger who was both discreet and who had a fast horse and good connections along the route. ‘He’s well-used to carrying delicate, private messages, for the merchants as well as the high-ups Ma’am. He’s reliable but expensive,’ Kesini advised. ‘Expense is not a problem,’ Vasabha was pleased with the news, ‘our lives and a lot else is on the line here. Whatever he’s asking, pay it, with an advance and a tip. I don’t care if he’s overcharging – whatever gets the word through, I’ll pay.’  ‘Right you are, Ma’am!’ Kesini was in motion already. When the courier was brought into the stable-yard, with his horse and his provisions readied, Kesini, placing in his palm the weighty pouch of five gold kahapanas said, ‘Here’s your first payment, plus a little extra to speed your way; you’ll get the other half on your return – having delivered the message to His Royal Highness Prince Mahanama, personally. Got it?’ This was a fortune that had been placed in his hand. He realized it meant that this was a highly important contract and so he wanted to make sure he got it right.

183 
‘And the message for the Prince is: “I am living here happily. Let not my lords make any difference in their treatment of him,’ is that correct?’ ‘Well done. That’s it, word for word. You know your job well. Ride fast, be safe and keep your mouth shut along the way. By the way, don’t drink up the gold before you return. We need you sober on horseback and at the way-stations.’  Kesini and Vasabha had discussed the wording at length. Their final version said what it needed to clearly enough without betraying any secrets, also making any interception that might be made, that could be interpreted in a negative way, plausibly deniable as anything untoward. Vasabha felt satisfied that they had done all that was possible and the rest was up to the laws of karma, and the chances of nature to ripen as they would. She relaxed into a heap of cushions, saying to Kesini, ‘We’ve done all we can. There is no point in fretting further.’ As she breathed in to give a sigh of sincere relief a strange subtle fragrance filled her senses for a moment, floral, like her own natural scent but unique and fruity. ‘What’s that incense you’re burning?’ she asked.  ‘There’s no incense Ma’am,’ Kesini sniffed to see if she could tell what Vasabha was referring to. She smelled nothing but her mistress’s natural fragrance and the evening jasmines, blossoming this season. ‘Oh, well never mind, it seems to have gone again.’   * * *  It was the day after the Full Moon and King Pasenadi had insisted on the Prince and the leaders of the embassy coming to the Jetavana to seek a blessing from the Master before departing. Vidudabha had proposed that, instead, the Buddha and some monks should be invited to the palace (mostly because it would be more convenient for His Serene Highness) but the King and the rest of the family disagreed so it was to the monastery that they went.  They made the effort to be very modestly dressed, to the chagrin of the Prince, and made offerings both before and after the mealtime. The Master gave his blessing for the embassy, with a peaceful but sober cast to his visage. He was warm and sincere as usual but there was a cool, almost sombre tone to his expression.  Queen Vasabha requested a Dharma teaching, to inspire and aid her son along his way, so the royal party settled in to listen closely, although Vidudabha was indicating a need to, ‘Carry on with preparations soon,’ despite everything having already been arranged.  The Master sat and looked over the group – at his old friend Pasenadi, at Mallika who had been a flower girl until the King raised her up, at Vasabha, at Vajiri and Vidudabha. He raised his eyes and took in the crowd of senior soldiers, ministers, lords and ladies and servants who had come along for the blessing too. He seemed to be consciously attuning to who was present, what was in store in their imaginations, what would be useful for them all to consider. He then began.

184 
To the surprise of many of them he spoke of the insight into the characteristic of ‘not-self’. This was a subtle topic which even many Elders in the Sangha still found hard to grasp. He explained how the things we customarily take to be who and what we are, and what we consider our possessions, are not in truth that. How, on account of ignorance, we believe the lie that the body, sensations of pleasure and pain, perceptions, thoughts and emotions are ‘mine’, that they are ‘what I am’, and that they are ‘the real me’.  To the puzzlement of quite a few who were gathered, but what was music to the ears of Mallika, Vasabha, Vajiri and the King, he explained how the attitude ‘I am’ was a foolish painful conceit to believe in.  ‘“I am” is a conceiving; “I am this” is a conceiving; “I shall be” is a conceiving; “I shall not be” is a conceiving. Conceiving is a disease, it is a cancerous tumour, it is a poisoned arrow. By overcoming all conceivings one is called “a sage at peace”. And the sage at peace is not born, does not age, does not die; they are not shaken and are not agitated.’ The Master, as he finished this profound statement, paused to let these principles sink in and be reflected upon.  The Prince, meanwhile, had been growing steadily more restless and he was beginning to wonder if all of this talk of selflessness was being aimed at him. He knew he had a bit of a reputation for being pompous and self-aggrandizing, despite high status and being looked up to being the norm for a Crown Prince, so he was getting suspicious. The Buddha didn’t look at him directly – as if to say, ‘This is for you’ – but it was chafing nonetheless.  At last he could not restrain himself any longer and this pause was the opportunity to speak up, ‘Venerable Sir, I’m curious, how can you say “I am” is a poisonous delusion when you yourself, an actual living person, are sitting there talking to us, right in front of you. To me this doesn’t make sense.’ To his delight he saw one or two heads in the party wagging, nodding in agreement that this was a good point. He was encouraged.  ‘Venerable Sir, is there a self?’ The Master remained silent.  ‘Then is there no self?’ Again the Master remained silent. His eyes were now trained upon Vidudabha, to indicate he was not avoiding the question, but still he did not speak. Silence hovered.  Some of those present realized that the silence of the Buddha was the answer to the question. The Prince was waiting for a reply and took the absence of words to mean he had stumped the great wise one with a question that was beyond him. A few others realized ‘The Prince is out of his depth here; the Master does this sometimes when someone has asked the wrong question, or they are asking a question that he sees doesn’t have any value.’ The King cleared his throat, ‘Venerable Sir, the Blessed One has given us a lot of time today and has graced my Prince with a blessing for the journey. We will now take our leave by paying our respects and with offering our gratitude.’

185 
‘It is time now, Great King, to do as you see fit.’  * * *  Vasabha had seen that Krishna was amongst the bhikkhus sitting near the Master. She had also noticed that the now extremely elderly and frail Khujjuttara had joined the group at the back, and was sitting with a couple of the helpers that the Queen had assigned to look after the Elder’s physical needs after Matanga had been so cruelly murdered. Her white hair was thin now and her frame crumpled but her eyes were as bright as ever.  She saw that Dusaka was in attendance too so she asked the King, ‘Sire, as I see that some of my Dharma friends are gathered here to add their well wishing to the embassy, I would like to stay in the Jetavana for a while, to meet and also to discuss this last discourse of the Blessed One. Khujjuttara is learned and wise beyond many of those in robes and I see that she is back there, by the eastern side of the hall.’  King Pasenadi glanced over and saw that the agèd sage was there and readily agreed to let Queen Vasabha linger. ‘Of course, my dear, please find a spot to sit and talk. I’m not sure how much longer Khujjuttara will be with us in this world, she must be close to ninety Rains in age by now.’ The Queen came over to where Khujjuttara was seated and she greeted her warmly. They were old friends by now. They had hardly finished their greetings before Krishna, now getting some grey hairs himself to contrast with his dark complexion, came up and said, ‘I’ve set up a place over there in the shade. We can go and sit where it’s cooler.’ It was a familiar, well-worn pattern by now that, whenever Vasabha had the chance to spend time in the Jetavana, she would seek out Krishna, or he her, the bond between them having become ever stronger. She had lost her family in Kapilavatthu, they were gone from her for good, and the only one she was close to in her official family was Vajiri who, against all odds, had still managed to remain unwed and so was still in the palace. Rumour had it that the King wanted to marry her off to Prince Ajatasattu in Magadha but she was magnificently adept at finding excuses not to comply with the Royal Wishes. There was Kesini, her oldest friend, but to have found Krishna, as her real father, was an unexpected joy.  She had loved the times that they had had together over these nine Rains. He was genuinely warm and caring towards her, even when she teased him for being a bit slow on the uptake when Dusaka or Khujjuttara – their regular companions and wise mentors – had made some observation that he had missed the point of. Vasabha could be prickly too, if Krishna or one of the others said something that wounded her pride, but she was even quicker to remind herself that, in truth, she was no queen, either relatively or ultimately speaking, and that she had nothing much to be that proud about. These were just reactions triggered by self-view and the threads of conceit that most folk seemed to have.

186 
The thing that pained most deeply, and which she liked to get advice on, was the longstanding deception based on her pretence to be a person of rank. She was living a lie. It was like a stone in her shoe that could not be removed.  Dusaka had joined them on a mat, in their shady spot on the north side of the sala and, when Vasabha brought this issue up again, along with her new anxieties around the visit of her arrogant and inflated son to Kapilavatthu, the old monk did his best to clarify the picture as he saw it, in order to give her some comfort.  ‘Great Queen,’ he began, a bit formally, ‘you should be more self-forgiving and put your heart at ease. It is true you are not without blame, you maintain the façade every day, but the motivation for the pretence didn’t come from you, did it? No. It was the proud Sakyans who put you up to it, or should I say, forced you into it,’ he raised a shaggy eyebrow, ‘you didn’t really have much of a choice, as I have heard it, eh?’ He then added, for propriety a, ‘Your Majesty.’ ‘Oh don’t!’ Vasabha’s appreciation of his friendly mocking of her supposed rank was genuine and she giggled. She was grateful for his sincere friendship, and she could discern that the way he liked to use her titles and honorifics playfully was his manner of encouraging self-respect in her, while he simultaneously took a shot at the whole notion of rank and social status.  Krishna felt an unremitting love and delight at being in Vasabha’s company. He had spent twenty-five Rains as an absent father, assuming his fragrant beautiful child was lost to him forever and now – here she was! And so close by! It was something special and a possibility of which he had never dreamed. He did his best to empathize with his daughter’s troubles and anxieties, also being aware of the past traumas and travails of her life in Kosambi as Magandiya, however it was not always easy to do so.  ‘I can’t say that I have deliberately deceived people so much,’ Krishna began, ‘but I feel I have believed the lies of others way too easily, too often and, worse, I have believed the lies I have told myself over and over. That’s the hardest thing for me,’ he looked at Dusaka, recounting old lessons, ‘I continually believe my desires, my fears, my opinions, my grudges… all of it… to be absolutely real. I try to learn but I’m slow.’ ‘“Brave but slowly wise, thus we hail our hero,”’ Dusaka quoted. Kujjuttara gave a wink; Krishna dipped his head, acknowledging the assessment by his mentor, who had been close by and guiding him for nigh on fifty Rains.  ‘So,’ Krishna asked, changing the subject, ‘all that “my-ness” I suffer from. How does the Master’s silence on “self” and “no self” relate to that?’ The Queen and Kesini were very interested in this too. ‘Yes,’ she joined in, ‘can either of you wise Elders illuminate this for us? Why was it, when the Master was asked those questions, that he remained silent?’ ‘If,’ Khujjuttara began, ‘when asked, ‘Is there a self?’ He had answered, “There is a self”, the Master would have been siding with the eternalists, those who believe in an individual entity existing for all time. If, on the other hand, when he was asked “Is there no self?” He had answered, “There is no self”, then he would

187 
have been siding with the annihilationists, those who believe that there is no substantial reality of any kind and that, at the death of the body, an existent being is utterly annihilated, destroyed. Neither of these two extremes is what the Blessed One teaches. Are you still with me?’ Her voice, like her eyes, was clear and energized. There were nods all round.  ‘So, if when he was asked by the Prince, “Is there a self?” he had answered “There is a self” would that have been consistent with the Master’s very common teaching that “all things, both the created and the uncreated, are not self”? To answer my own question: “No it wouldn’t.” Then, when he was asked, “Is there no self”, the Prince, already confused, would have fallen into even greater confusion, thinking, “I am sure I had a self when I came here but now the Master tells me that I don’t exist at all.” So, rather than answer the questions according to the way that they were posed, the Master indicated “Wrong questions!” by what I heard as a very meaningful and resonant silence.’ Krishna, despite his many years as a contemplative, was perplexed and somewhat irritated by this explanation. ‘I don’t mean to be rude…’ ‘But you will be,’ Dusaka cracked.  ‘… but, how can a reasonable question be “wrong” in itself?’ Dusaka cleared his throat, hinting that he was ready to pick up the discussion. Kujjuttara gave him a, ‘Yes! Please! You explain,’ look with a tilt of her snowy head.  ‘Take, for example, a question that you have asked me a few times, such as, “When a being is enlightened, where do they reappear, after the death of the body?”’ The whole group was keenly focused since, when Dusaka was in full explication mode it was a wonder to behold and to be a part of.  ‘Would you say that is a fair question?’ Dusaka looked at each of the four people and included Tingri at his side.  ‘Yes, it’s a fair question,’ Krishna responded. The rest, with a sense of where Dusaka was going with this, restrained any reply or gesture.  ‘Wrong – “reappear” doesn’t apply.’  ‘Then they do not appear in another realm?’ ‘Wrong again – “do not reappear” does not apply either.’ ‘Well, do they both reappear and not reappear?’ ‘Doesn’t apply.’ Krishna’s effort to cover every option then made him ask, ‘So, do they neither reappear, nor not reappear?’ He felt sure that one of these four must be correct:– What other options could there be? ‘Remember,’ Dusaka continued, ‘we are talking about the principle of “wrong questions”. So, imagine we had a little fire burning here, made of grass and some small sticks, and we then let the fire go out. If I then asked you the question, “Where did the fire go? Was it north, south, east or west?” How would you answer?’ ‘That those directions don’t apply. It just went out.’ ‘Exactly!’ Dusaka was alight and Tingri had to position herself to not get knocked by his gesticulations. ‘The way you ask the question – here about “direction” –

188 
presumes a reality that does not exist. With respect to the enlightened, identity, time and location don’t apply, and neither do language and concepts. The heart of such a one has realized perfect attunement with its own fundamental nature which is timeless, unlocated and not a self or belonging to a self. “A being” doesn’t apply, “going” doesn’t apply, “to” doesn’t apply, and “another realm” doesn’t apply either. Got it?!’  * * *  Golden. Everything was golden, rich, bright, fragrant. ‘Where? What?’ There was wakefulness, a clear perception of light and scent, and sound, strange wonderful sounds.  Consciousness continued to expand: petals, flower-petals; feeling, soft feeling; there is music in the air; air; daylight; fingers, face, feet… soft ground beneath. ‘I’m lying on soft ground. It’s day somewhere. What I remember… Who is “I”? I remember the grip on my shoulder, an angry eye… My neck… the half-expected, anticipated cut… almost no pain. I gave my self away… I chose that…’ Soon the fragments of memory were lost in the dazzling light which seemed to hold an extra dimension of substantiality, as if the light were alive. Petals, which had been dressing her eyes, fell away as the new-born deva woke into this fresh life. She looked down the length of her body and saw that the golden petals were also formed into strands that clad her; blossoms and soft gossamer threads formed a draping cobweb about her. She lifted a hand and turned it in wonder.  She breathed deeply in and, more so than the light, the air of this place, wherever it was, was quick with life, it loved her, she loved it. Energy rampaged through the limbs, the chest, the belly, the head she now bore. She smiled – ‘Now, this is what I call being alive!’ Enthralled by the vital power of that breath she took in another and another, as deep as she could. After the third things began to spin, and kept spinning.  ‘Rest, I should rest a bit. Rest. What a place… And what am I?’  The passing of time was lost to this newly-born awareness. There was softness beneath her and the delicious, deeply scented living air, then it was gone.   * * *  Again her eyes opened and she could, now, clearly remember how it had been when she first awakened. She had no sense of time having passed as the light around her was without a form. She looked into the sky above her, there were jewelled, flower-bearing trees and between them the bluest blue she had ever seen. The sun was there but was it higher or lower than before? She couldn’t tell.  Standing, she swayed for a moment then took a few steps. Moss that looked like living emeralds sprang between her toes. ‘Well it seems I have hands and feet here, and I can stand and walk.’ She reached for a cluster of blossoms on a low

189 
branch nearby. As she reached her effort drew her up into the air, which it took a moment to notice. ‘Oh! I can fly too!!’ This was indeed a place of wonders.  The distant music now caught her attention, it was coming from…  where? She looked about and tried to sense where the sound was loudest although it was in the space around her and in her as much, apparently, as it was emanating from a single spot. She followed the intensity, once she had pinned it down, and drifted through the air towards it, feeling like streamers of light and fragrance were billowing out behind her and following in her train.  There was a grove ahead thickly packed with other beings. She could see they all looked like she did, as far as she could tell. Cautiously, not knowing what she was coming upon, or whether she belonged. Gentle resonances from her life before said to her, ‘Beware, there might be danger,’ but she tasted that, knew the feeling, and pushed herself on, ‘Don’t be afraid. No need. I am bigger than this.’ At the edge of the broad glade she paused and looked about her. The rhythmic music was shimmering in the fragrant air and the presence of these hundreds of beings was like the crackling shocks of fur on amber – something she remembered from… Where? When?  Beneath one of the flowering trees, off to one side, she saw a beautiful young being close to a less bright, smaller being, clothed all in carmine and vermillion, a dozen shades of red.  ‘I know them… do I? From where? When? I know…’ She was thinking as she unconsciously floated towards them. ‘The red one, she’s so familiar… there was a yakkha… long ago… who is her friend?’ As her memory stretched to recognize, find a name, she arrived beside them. The two broke off their conversation and turned to her, seeing in her mango-golden raiment and her big-eyed innocence, a friendly face.  ‘Greetings,’ the two said simultaneously. The brighter, beautiful being then spoke, ‘I have just reappeared here.’ The red-clad one added, ‘And I’m only a visitor.’ ‘And I’m new here too.’ She found she had a voice and that she knew their language. ‘I have just arrived, appeared, I’m not sure what, as well.’ She looked closely at the small being, seeing now that most of her garments were made from skilfully woven kanavera blossoms. ‘Do I know you from somewhere?’  * * *  It was with great pomp and a beating of drums that Prince Vidudabha led the procession out of Savatthi. In taking leave of his Royal Father, the pleasure at now standing at least a hand-span taller than the King, if not nearly two, was deeply relished. The King was broader, deeper in the chest by far, but the Prince now looked down upon his father and he would probably grow some more before he was done. His elephant was at the van of the great embassy and he was flushed with pride at how splendid he was sure he looked, arrayed in all his finery at the head of the awesome, powerful assemblage of Kosalans. He was not able to hear the

190 
comments such as, ‘’E’s got more sparklers on ’im than a bride in a double dose of bangles,’ and ‘’E’s gonna be in deep shtook if ’e tries to go into a fight on that tusker. It’s got more paint ’n’ finery all over it than the dancing girls dahn at Sukhavati.’ The journey was, even at a slowish pace, not such a long one, only nine or ten yojanas, but Vidudabha did not want to rush so they only progressed a third or even a quarter of a yojana per day, with a late start, a long lunch pause and an early camp each night. The Prince made out that the lightness of the burden of distance each day was out of compassion for the animals pulling the carts and bearing the horsemen and howdahs of the group but closer to the truth was that he found the whole affair extremely uncomfortable. He had become somewhat nauseous from the steady rocking motion of his elephant and was saddle-sore from riding when he took to horse as an alternative. He was shocked at how thoroughly inconvenient things were – one had to choose the pausing places according to the needs for water, food and shelter for the animals rather than to meet the Royal Preference.  Twenty days passed in this fashion before, to his great pleasure, caparisoned riders from Kapilavatthu met them on the road. He had enjoyed the first sight of the forested hills of the Himavant, beginning in the foothills of the snow-capped distant peaks. He had expressed his pleasure at the sight of wilder creatures than they ever saw in the grounds of the palace or in the villages scattered around Savatthi, beasts such as leopards and herds of deer, and wild buffalo but, in truth, he only made such remarks since he felt he was supposed to. Also the courtiers around him, and some of the more diplomatically-minded army officers, would grunt in agreement, or come up with a cheery ‘Yes, Your Serene Highness, look at that!’ when he spoke thus, so it gave him the cue that this was what was expected of the Crown Prince.  He was shocked at the sight of a pair of broad-winged lammergeiers, coasting on an updraft at the edges of the lowlands but more out of fear of being attacked by one rather than in wonder at their majesty.  In short he was glad to be arriving and he looked forward to the comforts of a settled bedroom and not having to be told what to do on account of the needs of elephants, horses and oxen. It had become profoundly annoying that the beasts seemed to need to eat, sleep and defecate all the time and that he had been expected to arrange his life around that.  The Sakyan riders were soon joined by further troops from Kapilavatthu and horns were blown, drums beaten and banners unfurled, to welcome the Prince into his ancestral home.  ==*==

191 
CHAPTER 21:  THE PRINCE’S FURY   ephyrs carried scents and falling petals gently across the Grove. Ant and Ninka had been locked in an intense conversation before the newly-arrived deva had joined them. Ant tried to bear in mind that her belovèd had just been hatched into this new life but she was aggrieved and sick at heart, ireful too. The beauty of their surroundings was lost to them.  ‘How is it that you didn’t recognize Muñca when he first appeared? The princesses said he was notorious in this, their realm – the devas in the Realm of the Guardians had said nothing but, for all of you here in the Thirty-Three, it sounds like he’s infamous,’ Ant pressed her.  Ninka looked up into her face and could not speak at first.  ‘Did you recognize him?’ Ant was doing her best to read Ninka’s expression, now tear-stained as it was. Her heart sank. ‘You did know. You knew all along. How could you?’ Ant was aghast, feeling a storm of emotion. ‘Why?!?’ A righteous rage flashed through her. ‘Poor Maggot! And the rest of us tagging along, with the nagas and the yakkhas, and all the way up to the Tusita – you let him drag us all behind him on a wild goose chase,’ her words slowed down and softened as she took in Ninka’s sweet face now twisted with tears and anguish. A weeping deva is a wrenching sight to see. A realization began to dawn in Ant’s heart, ‘It was…’ Ninka wiped her eyes, ‘For you, it was that I could not bear to be parted from you. A day at a pointless party in the Nandana Grove and decades will go by in the Realm of Humans, for you in the Realm of Earth-spirits too.’ She took a breath, letting the vitality of the airs of her home clear her head a little.  ‘I confess, and I ask your forgiveness – and of everyone of our friends, especially Maggot – I thought that I knew who he was as soon as he appeared in the Underworld. When he whisked us out of there I was certain, then it all came crashing in. If I called him out and challenged him, our search for the baby would lose direction and the group would be scattered, I wouldn’t have an excuse to stay with you and I would have had to come back here. Now, as I think of it, it doesn’t make sense. Why did I believe those thoughts? What made me choose deception? Anyway, in that flash of recognition, I decided not to reveal who he was, that he had such a deceitful reputation, but instead to pretend. And he gave me a look, as if to say, “I know that you know, but you’re not going to say anything, are you?” He was teasing, provoking, relishing the trap I was in – that I had put myself in.’ Her eyes were a thousand leagues of sadness and regret.

192 
‘Once that moment passed I knew I was now committed to the lie. Can you forgive me?’ Ant was weeping gently too. ‘What you did was terrible, stringing everyone along, but not unforgiveable at all. It was a strange choice for you to make but, when we’re deluded, blinded by love, such things easily happen.’ Ant’s words were cool, serious and loving. ‘Such was the malice and mischief of Muñca, I feel sure he was guided by the Evil One, if not being the Lord of Lies, Mara himself, in disguise. What you said about him relishing your predicament looks like a clear sign to me.’ She took her hand and they sat in silence.  ‘Of course I forgive you; if we can rejoin the others you’ll have to tell them too but I’ll speak up for you.’ Ninka smiled. ‘I love you so much.’ It was just after this, as they sat under the trees and looked out over the blossoms and the dancers, that their new companion had arrived.   * * *  Ninka was now fully back in the Realm of the Tavatimsa. The challenging conversation she had just had with Ant had brought her firmly into this life once again. After having got through the issue of her deception, Ant had then brought her up to date on the word from Hiri and Siri; things had been looking up with respect to the search and Ant was excited to tell her all. Their quiet companion, so far nameless, listened in, trying to follow all the events and places being talked about.  Breathless by the end of this Ant went quiet, noticing that Ninka’s brow had become furrowed. ‘What is it?’ she asked, ‘You look worried. I thought you’d be pleased to hear all of that – we’ve got a lead.’ Ninka replied, ‘Yes, and you and I should be there too. I’m scared that he’ll say no but I’m going to ask the Prince!’ Ninka rose up and headed straight for the heart of the party, which it seemed was now growing a little quieter. Ant followed just behind her and the other deva tagged along too.  ‘Your Divine and Serene Highness,’ Ninka bowed, ‘Although I have but recently returned to this joyous festival, and although you had to ask me to return many times, I humbly request your Divine and Royal Leave to depart once again, before the revels have ended.’ Prince Malabhari looked at her, a slight puzzlement crossed his face, ‘Asked you to return? Did I?’ He looked about, as if to seek out someone nearby. ‘Well, I might have enquired of one of these sublime beings, ‘Where’s the Lady Ninka?’ but, did I send out some sort of command?’ His gaze landed on one of the devas close to him, ‘Ah, you’re in charge of messages. Did I send for this girl?’  ‘Not exactly Sire, but you had noticed she was not about so I took the liberty.’ ‘As I thought, it was merely a passing curiosity, not a demand for your presence, my dear. The Divine and Royal Pleasure invites you to do whatever you like, go wherever you please.’ It was apparent that the hours of partying had been

193 
accompanied by copious draughts of nectar-mead – which had an extraordinary potency when made from flowers of the Tavatimsa Heaven – so the magnanimity of Prince Malabhari was more expansive than usual. ‘If you wish to slum it with some…’ he looked askance and somewhat haughtily at Ant, ‘… kinnaris…’ the word seemed to bring a off-putting taste to his mouth, ‘… no problem.’  * * *  ‘I can’t believe it!’ Ninka was a mixture of relief, resentment, regret and excitement. ‘That whole time there was no need for me to be here at all! This stretch of stressing, hating myself, grinding my teeth in self-justification, fearing to lose you and to be parted for good – the travails of a whole lifetime and having four children in the human world – the suffering, anguishing in all of it was completely unnecessary. If it wasn’t such a tragedy I would laugh.’ Through all of this the amber-golden deva who had just introduced herself stayed close by. When Ant suggested they go to the Human Realm and to Kapilavatthu straight away, to follow the rest of the searchers, she asked, ‘Can I come too? I feel I know you and this world is strange to me, although I’m getting a bit more used to it. Can I follow along? Just tell me what to do, or to not do.’  Ninka took her hand, and Ant’s, and they appeared instantaneously in Kapilavatthu. ‘Nink,’ Ant called out, ‘Come on, let’s find them or see if they’ve left traces if they’ve moved on already.’ Ninka was a skilled tracker and it was not difficult to pick up the scents of their familiar friends, especially the spoor of Gumbiya. ‘Let’s go – this way,’ Ninka motioned them to follow her to the palace. The golden deva was meanwhile fixed to the spot, looking at the hills and distant white-capped peaks to the north, ‘Come on!’ Ant broke the spell and took her hand.  They found a courtyard that was thick with the fragrances of Rhamba and Salassa and the fruity aromas of Maggot and Bee, as well as the notably ripe hum of Gumby. Ninka looked around, breathing in the messages that the place gave her, sorting through the many animal and human smells. ‘I’m not sure,’ she shook her head, it was all a bit thick, heavy and coarse after their long stint passing through the higher, more sensorily refined realms.  ‘’Allo,’ a slight but firm voice sounded nearby, as they glided by the kitchen storerooms on their search.  ‘You look jes’ like them other flower-devas wot woz ’ere a while back.’ Ant, Ninka and their nameless friend looked about them until they saw two small sprites hovering before them. The brownish one introduced himself again. ‘’Allo. I’m Cob, this ere’s Green-Blossom, pleased to meet yer,’ his green-clad partner waved, with a smile and wagged her cap.  ‘You all look like y’r lookin’ for someone or sumfing too.’ ‘Yes,’ Ant was delighted to meet these beings who it appeared had seen at least Bee and Maggot. ‘We’re looking for our friends who came to Kapilavatthu, and

194 
they’re searching for the lost baby of Miss Maggot, one of the ones who looked like me.’  ‘Right you are. They woz ’ere all right, a few Moons ago now, eh?’ He turned to Green-Blossom who then picked it up, ‘They were very nice so we told ’em what we could. By the way, ’oo are you all? We don’t get many visits from high ups ’n’,’ she indicated Ninka and the golden deva, ‘it’s an honour for us hobs and brownies to meet such,’ she searched for the right word, ‘noble and ’oly ones.’ She nudged Cob vigorously while she curtsied and Cob made an awkward bow, ‘Er, Your Highnesses, Holinesses, er…’  ‘Please, I’m Ninka, this is Ant and this is our new friend, whose name is…’ and she realized that she didn’t have her name yet. The new deva looked at her blankly, and affirmed, ‘No one has given me a name yet.’ She looked a little vacantly at Ant and Ninka.  ‘Well,’ Green-blossom spoke up, ‘such a one as you milady should ’ave a real nice name. Since ’e’s cob-brown, and I’m as green as a rice-flower, we could call you Ambaka, on ’count of you bein’ the colour of golden mangoes. ’Ow does that sound?’ Ant was pleased with this; it resonated as the perfect name for her new friend but she was getting impatient. ‘So, what could you tell them, the others who were on the search?’ ‘Oh yea, right,’ Cob cleared his thoughts, ‘What we told ’em was we thought that the girl she was what, sixteen sun-turnings then? She’d gone off to Savatthi. She were a slave-girl from the kitchens ’ere so she’s prob’ly in the kitchens there.’ ‘Savatthi? How long ago?’ Ninka was eager to make up for lost time, feeling that she had been to blame for so much delay already.  ‘Mus’ be a dozen, p’raps fifteen sun-turnin’s at least now’ Green-blossom counted on her tiny fingers.  The three devas wasted no more time before setting off to Savatthi, Ninka carried Ant, as she could fly faster, and Ambaka slipstreamed them, greatly enjoying both the power of flight and the sound of her new name – it was exactly right somehow.  The three of them approached the palace at Savatthi and aimed towards a leafy garden courtyard, close by the main buildings. Tall trees and shrubs were there, amid the structures that filled the area. As they were landing they saw a cluster of very familiar forms, gathered beneath one of the trees. They swiftly descended and joined with their dear companions.  Salassa was the first to greet them, ‘Hey, you’ve got here too!’ He turned to Ninka, asking, ‘So, how was the party?’ ‘Long,’ she replied, hit by a wave of flashbacks from the garland workshop and the births of her four children in Katara village, ‘Long, like you wouldn’t believe.’  * * *

195 
 Prince Vidudabha was received with splendid and colourful ceremony as they marched into Kapilavatthu’s palace grounds. He realized he shouldn’t get too carried away but this was deeply pleasing. He loved to be the centre of attention and he certainly was at this time.  The embassy was given space to unpack and settle and then the Prince and his party were welcomed into the Santhagara, the grand audience chamber. ‘Well, they called this “grand” but it’s not much compared with Kosala; still, they are country cousins, after all, so I should keep quiet on that,’ he reflected. Prince Mahanama and Princess Maddarupi, ostensibly his grandparents, were in the forefront to usher him in and invite him to meet his royal Sakyan family.  Prior to his arrival agitated discussions had been taking place around the halls and homes of the nobles of Kapilavatthu. ‘It is impossible, Sire, for any of us to pay obeisance to this individual, the son of a slave-girl,’ one of the ministers said through a sneer. ‘What are we to do?’ ‘Should we arrange an “accident”?’ ‘Have all the senior royals be busy on a mission elsewhere?’ ‘How by all the gods should we handle this?’  Mahanama, who was shrewd and experienced in matters of diplomacy, was struck with a bright idea, ‘We shall not send away us senior royals, but simply the junior ones. By rights, as a younger person, the Prince should pay respects to his Royal Elders, correct?’ The lords and ladies and members of the royal house nodded or murmured in agreement. ‘So,’ the King continued, ‘if we send away the young princely children into the country on some excuse, then he will pay obeisance to us elders yet none of us will need to pay respects to him. Is this acceptable?’ There were some who baulked at even being on the receiving end of a formal greeting from the son of a slave – despite his father being the mighty King of Kosala – but these few were persuaded that discretion was the better part of pride in this case and they agreed to go along with the ruse.  There was an impressive array of crowned and bejewelled nobles in the audience chamber. The Prince began to chat amiably with Vidudabha, expressing how delighted and impressed he was to meet him, and for the first time, and how glad he also was that the great King Pasenadi had brought their houses closer by his marriage to Queen Vasabha:– Now, by having fathered the Prince and named him as his heir to the throne of Kosala, he had cemented those ties for many generations to come. This was a cause for celebration!  ‘Before we begin any festivities or entertainments, I should introduce you, Your Serene Highness, grandson, to other members of the Sakyan Royal House.’  The Prince was led to the assembled line of nobility and paid respects to each one, until his back ached from the effort. He was sincerely endeavouring to be humble and polite but he was losing patience. Not a one of them had offered him the respects in return – not one – so, at last, once he had come to the end of the long line, he could not restrain himself.

196 
‘How is it,’ he asked, ‘that though I pay obeisance to all of you,’ he looked pointedly along the line of finely-clad and tall-standing Sakyans, ‘none of you offer your respects to me?’ ‘Oh, Your Highness,’ Prince Mahanama put on his most convincing, matter-of-fact, dissembling voice. ‘It is our custom here; only juniors pay respects to elders and not the other way round – never!’ As if such a possibility were unthinkable to any right-minded person. ‘Our younger princes and princesses are all off in the countryside at the moment – it’s a seasonal thing we do – so, at the moment, if you’ll excuse us, Your Serene Highness is the most junior of the kshatriyan royals here.’  His words were honeyed and they did their job. Mahanama then invited him to sit in a seat especially prepared for the entertainments, Prince Vidudabha settled himself down and brushed his concerns aside. Any lingering sense that he was being insulted or not treated with proper honour was washed away by the lavish display of dancers and pulsing music that the Prince then offered, by way of amusements. The dancing was followed by a grand banquet and the Prince was swamped with gifts, fine flavours, exotic smells and soothing textures.  ‘They may be country people from the hills but these Sakyans really know how to hold a feast.’ He was pleased. ‘I believe I’m going to enjoy my days here.’   * * *  Ant and Ninka quickly noticed that Maggot was wreathed in smiles. ‘You look happy,’ Ant remarked.  ‘How could I not be,’ Maggot’s bright face lit up even more.  ‘So…’ Ninka dared to ask.  ‘So we found her!’ Bee proudly announced, ‘but there’s good news and bad news.’  ‘Bad news?’ Ant was now concerned, ‘is she ill? Or dead? No, she can’t be, you’re too happy. What happened?’ Bee settled herself back into Gumbiya’s armpit, leaning against his massive thigh and flank; she loved the fume of him and, now that their search was over, they could all feel more relaxed. ‘You tell the story, Maggot, it’s yours to pass on.’ ‘When we got here, a few Moons ago, we raced about, hither and yon all over the palace grounds: the kitchens, the workshops, the stables, the servants’ quarters, since all we knew was that Tam had grown up in a slave family who mostly worked in the kitchen at Kapilavatthu and that is what she had done before going south.’ ‘Yes, we heard that.’  ‘Shh, Nink,’ Ant scowled.  ‘We picked up traces of her fragrance pretty quickly.’ ‘Even me,’ Gumbiya chipped in; Bee dug him in the ribs.  ‘She was definitely around but we soon came to the conclusion that she did not live with the slave families or in the servants’ quarters.

197 
‘The places where the signs of her were strongest were around the royal chambers. So then, one morning while it was still fresh, I was looking through the windows as the maid opened the shutters and there she was, leaning on some cushions in a parlour room, with some other princess or some high-up lady. I was frozen, couldn’t more or speak for shock, but then Bee came up and hovered beside me.  ‘“It’s her!” Bee shouts out. “She looks like a queen, and twice as old as you!” I thought that was a bit of an exaggeration,’ she tipped Bee a nod, ‘but she was certainly all grown up. We reckon it’s been thirty-five sun-turnings here in the Human Realm, give or take, since she was hatched. And she is certainly no slave girl.’  All five of them who had been in Savatthi for this time were in full agreement on this.  ‘We’ve been asking around the local rukkha-devas and kinnaris of the gardens and all seem to say the same: She’s a queen indeed and mother of the Crown Prince, as well.’ ‘So,’ Ant was enthralled to hear all this, ‘What was your meeting like?’ Maggot went quiet.  ‘That’s the bad news,’ Rhamba broke the silence, ‘She can’t see us and it doesn’t seem like any of the other people in the palace can either.’ ‘Gave it our all, we did,’ Gumbiya joined in, ‘in the palace, rahnd the gardens, jumpin’ ’n’ yellin’. I ’it the grahnd wiv me club a few times – nuffink. They fort it woz an earthquake’ ‘Blowing in her ears, whispering her name, singing her favourite lullabies from when she was a baby, none of it gets through.’ ‘Breathing a full kinnari breath into her face – not a thing, or, maybe she cocked her eyebrow and inhaled a little deeper,’ Bee recounted.  ‘I think that might have been wishful thinking, Bee,’ Maggot smiled appreciatively.  ‘Anyway we’ve found her and it’s wonderful to be with her but, so far, she can’t see me or hear me, or any of us, so she doesn’t know she’s been found.’ ‘Does she know she was lost?’ Ninka reflected.  ‘That’s another story; we don’t have any idea about her past. She certainly behaves like she’s always been a queen, or a princess rather than a slave or an ordinary person, even when she’s just with her maid, who’s a girl with one hand who we think is Kesini from Kosambi.’ ‘I’m sure it is’ said Salassa, ‘the way she walks is even more distinctive than the missing hand.’ ‘Though how she got here from Kosambi…’ ‘No’ a clue,’ finished Gumbiya.  Ant was intrigued. ‘So, what about the Jetavana and the enlightened ones there, could they help?’

198 
‘We haven’t tried that yet,’ Maggot looked a bit sheepish, ‘since she’s my belovèd daughter but she is also the offspring of a novice monk, as he was then, and that’s not a situation that I’m very proud of.’ Again Maggot went quiet.  ‘So,’ Gumbiya cracked half a smile, ‘’ere we all are, ’appily ’sconced in the royal gardens, doin’ our best to come up wiv a way to help young – not-so-young – Tambaka meet ’er mum.’  * * *  The embassy’s visit only lasted for a few days and then it was time to head back to Kosala. Vidudabha felt he had acquitted himself well, made a good impression and had acted very properly, as befitted a prince of high rank. His mother and father would be proud of him and he was now weighted down with rich gifts that his newly-met grandparents had bestowed upon him. It had all gone swimmingly.  His elephant was now prepared and the military escort was drawn up in ranks to depart, the waggons and carriages were ready with their oxen and horses standing in gleaming tack and harnesses. The Himalayan sun shone from a clear bright sky and the road lay ahead.  The drums and trumpets had already sounded when one of the junior lords of Kosala, Viraja by name, noticed that his sword was not strapped at his waist. He began to panic; to lose such a precious item was a shameful thing for any warrior-noble, so he desperately tried to remember where he could have left it. There had been so much drinking and carousing over the previous days it was difficult to recollect anything clearly.  It then struck him that, ‘Aha, bladed weapons were not allowed in that audience hall and we had to unbuckle our belts by the door. It’s there, I’m sure.’ He ducked away from the other young nobles on horseback, saying, ‘Hold my nag a minute, I’ll be right back,’ and thrusting the reins into the hand of his squire he took off.  He passed through the Sakyan folk who had been gathered to give them a good send-off and he made his way to the hall as fast as possible. To his great relief, there to the left of the broad portal, on a shelf, was his sword-belt and his prized weapon, together with a handsome dagger that one of his friends had forgotten too.  He stood by the entrance while he sorted out the buckling on of the scabbard and sword, and was pocketing the knife to return to Sujiva, (after he’s made sure to torment him for having lost it) when his eye caught a strange sight. There were several people in the hall tidying up, doing various tasks, but what struck him was that a slave woman was washing the seat where Prince Vidudabha had been sitting. As he looked closer he saw she seemed to be using an mixture of milk and water to do this. This was strange indeed.  ‘What are you doing?’ He asked.

199 
‘Oo me?’ She was still fixed on the task at hand so didn’t even bother to glance over her shoulder at him. ‘This is the seat that son of a slave girl was sittin’ on so it’s got to be a slave wot washes it, ’n’ with milk an’ water too; don’ ask me why.’  ‘“Son of a slave girl” – you don’t mean Prince Vidudabha?’ ‘’Course. ’E’s the son of Vasabha who was a slave girl here, so, got to wash it down special, otherwise no one else can use it – well, none of the nobs anyway.’ Now, noticing his accent, she glanced his way and realized it was one of those ‘nobs’ she was talking to, ‘Oh, sorry sir, didn’t mean to be rude.’ And, thinking no more of it she caried on with her cleaning.  Viraja scurried out and got back to his horse, his mind teeming with thoughts and confused emotions. The way the woman had spoken implied it was common knowledge that Vasabha was no queen and that the Prince was ill-born. His heart pounded as he fretted:– She was telling the truth I’m sure, and revealing a huge secret without realizing it. She didn’t twig that I was from the party of Kosalans. What on earth should I do? The first thing he did was to seek out his friend Sujiva and return his dagger, quite forgetting to tease him, as he was eager to get some counsel. When he described what he had seen, Sujiva’s dusky face turned ashen.  ‘We can’t keep this secret. We have to tell the Prince. It will be our heads if we delay as we’ll be seen to be complicit.’  * * *  They waited until the next rest stop for the great caravan and rode up to the Prince’s elephant, where he had just dismounted.  ‘Viraja, Sujiva, what’s up? You two chaps look like you’ve just seen a ghost and it’s still broad day.’ ‘A spectre we have encountered in truth, Your Serene Highness,’ Sujiva started, then grew quiet, turning to Viraja to continue. He recounted the incident, reporting it as calmly and clearly as he could, but his pulse was hammering in his head, his chest, and he was sweating rivers; by way of finishing he repeated, ‘“Son of a slave-girl” was what she said Sire, and did so as if it was something that every Sakyan person was aware of.’ As the story unfolded and reached this ending the Prince’s face distorted, reddened and then became empurpled with affronted pride and fury. He raged and yelled, attacking those around him, asking if any of them said the same, had known this sickening truth that shamed him so. As he lashed out he saw the faces of those around him – the nobles, the other princes, the officers of the army, the servants – they all looked equally aghast and shocked. No one knew of this, which was, to him, a terrible truth.  When calm returned he spoke, ‘If these proud Sakyans now see fit to wash the seat whereon I sat with milk and water, here and now before you all, I vow that

200 
when I come into my power I will wash that chair and this city with their heart’s blood, and their halls will be filled with corpses and lamentation.’   ==*==

201 
CHAPTER 22:  THE ATTACK ON THE SAKYANS   ceans of blood will bathe the feet of the Himavant, Father!’ Prince Vidudabha ranted and raged as he brought the shocking news of this deceit to King Pasenadi. He had been determined to be the one to inform his father how the Crown of Kosala had been insulted. To the horror of the Prince, quite unexpectedly, the King responded to this declaration by saying, ‘Yes indeed, bathed in blood they shall be, but not by you, slave-son!’  As Vidudabha heard these words he could not at first comprehend what the King was saying, so assured had he been of his own position and of the King’s role as his Royal Father. The Prince had spent his life getting away with outrageous behaviour and no one had ever spoken back to him. He had never been scolded by his parents and he had hardly ever been on the receiving end of even a harsh word – except occasionally from Vajiri. So at this moment, as he absorbed the meaning of the King’s wrathful declaration, his world came crashing down. Inside, he was shocked, poll-axed, but he was also seething:– How dare His Majesty treat him in this way!  ‘Out! Out of my sight!’ King Pasenadi barked at the Prince. His ire had been steadily building. He turned to his chief minister and shouted, ‘Bring her here! Bring her! I will restrain my Royal Disfavour until the truth of this ugly matter is clear,’ although it was apparent that this mood was already out of its stable and galloping free.  The fierceness of the rap at her door, when the minster’s staff came to get her, had been the sign she had long been dreading. She knew the embassy had returned and was anxiously awaiting word on how things had gone. The sharp knocks at once told her all. ‘Here it comes,’ she exchanged a glance with Kesini and, in silence, with a wry smile, she rose to her feet. ‘Well, we managed it for a while…’, she thought, and then stood before the door, which was opened before she had even said, ‘Come in.’ Queen Vasabha was brought before the King in the throne room. Queen Mallika had picked up the tremors of the unfolding incident and had quietly drawn close to the King, to at least witness what was happening here. The return of the embassy from the Sakyans should have been a joyous and uncomplicated event but something untoward was definitely afoot. Princess Vajiri, even more quietly, joined Mallika from the side of the chamber.  ‘My Royal Wife,’ Pasenadi began through gritted teeth, addressing Vasabha who knelt before him at the bidding of the chief minister. ‘An ill rumour, a report has reached my ears that tells a grim and appalling tale. It pains me that this is news

202 
that you were not of the Sakyan royal house but, rather, were a kitchen-slave who was young enough and pretty enough to pass as a princess of kshatriyan blood. Have you deceived me, your King, your Royal Husband, all these years? Have you betrayed and lied to the good people of Kosala and birthed an ill-gotten son by me, obtained by a false and invalid marriage?’ The King spoke steadily but, beneath the film of control, he was incandescent. He saw by Vasabha’s posture and her tears that, far from being wronged by these accusations, they were tragically, horribly true.  Vasabha, gently weeping, admitted, ‘Yes, Sire, this is sadly true. I have aped the role of princess and queen, a royal mother, these last seventeen Rains. I have deceived you and all the worthy folk of Savatthi, of Kosala. I dare not ask for forgiveness.’ She bowed with her head to the floor and stayed there.  Vidudabha, who was noticeably not sitting by the King, and was not in a position to be making any judgements, could not hold himself back from asking. ‘But, woman, if you were but a slave you could not have worked this treachery on your own. Surely the king of the Sakyans and his family must have been part of this. How could they not be?’ King Pasenadi recalled the embassy he had made all those years ago, and how Vasabha had been presented to him. It was blatantly clear that the Sakyan royals had to have been complicit.  ‘Sit up girl!’ The King commanded. ‘Speak. Was this deception conceived by you or by the Sakyan nobles?’ ‘It was not by me, Sire. I know not who first crafted the idea but I was chosen and the task was put before me.’  ‘Did you have any choice?’ The point was pressed.  Vasabha considered, cast her mind back as best she could. ‘To tell the honest truth, Sire, I had no choice but I wanted to do it too.’ She knew she did not have to say the latter part of this but, in the effort to have no more secrets after years of such deception on her part, it burst forth in this way.  ‘You wanted it? You wanted to cheat the King of Kosala and to live a deceitful life?’ Pasenadi was getting more heated at the very idea of it.  ‘No sire, it was not about your Royal Person at all. It was, if I might say, the selfish, foolish dream of a kitchen-girl who has a chance to be a princess, a fantasy that any girl might have. It’s not an excuse for what I did but it was a way for me to escape the drudgery of the heat and smoke of the kitchen. At the time I didn’t think much beyond that. I was not an educated person.’  A few heads nodded around the throne room at this, conceding that this was a fair point to be made.  Vasabha weighed what she should say further, then, thinking ‘In for a masaka, in for a kahapana’, but perhaps not a whole kahapana as she realized it would not help things to say she’d found out that she was actually the daughter of a kinnari and a very dark-skinned novice monk. They would all assume she had completely lost her wits if she told them that. She added, ‘One small factor that my mother passed on to

203 
me, was that the King, Lord Mahanama, had often called upon my mother’s favours in her youth and he would still come by from time to time as we all grew up, since he enjoyed her company. The fact that she was a slave, and married to someone else, counted for nothing.’ She looked modestly down as she knew the same uninspiring practice pertained in Kosala, as in most kingdoms, and that kings and princes regularly consorted with any low-class girl they pleased, and law and custom protected them.  ‘Prince Mahanama held that he could quite possibly be my actual father and so, when he showed me to you saying, ‘May I introduce my daughter,’ he could justify to himself that he was (possibly) not lying.’ She finished and bowed her head again.  ‘Hrrmph! I don’t know whether that makes it better or worse,’ he bristled. ‘Regardless of the details of your birth, you have been falsely presented to me as a pure-blooded Sakyan, a kshatriyan princess, and you have consciously maintained that deception since our marriage. Exactly what punishment is to be exacted upon the Sakyans is to be decided but you, woman, are herewith deprived of your rank and your possessions, and will go back to the status of slave. You will be kept in the palace dungeons, attending upon my Royal Pleasure, until a decision about your fate has been made.’  Vidudabha, still clad in his finery from the embassy’s journey, cleared his throat.  King Pasenadi turned to him, ‘Oh yes, you too. You are no longer Crown Prince, or Commander-in-Chief, and are demoted to slave as well. You don’t need to be locked up – the crime was not yours – but I can’t have a misbegotten child like you as my heir. Give your,’ here he waved a dismissive hand at his jewellery and fine attire, ‘all this stuff to the stewards and wear something that more befits your station.’    * * *  Queen Mallika and Princess Vajiri had been listening closely to all this and were as shocked as the rest of the court at these revelations and the sudden downfall of Vasabha and Vidudabha. They were both very fond of Vasabha and she had always been friendly, kind and respectful to them both, never wielding clout on account of having borne the King his only son.  After the former queen and the boy had been conducted out, as swiftly as she could, Mallika came closer to the King’s side. She made some comforting, soothing mumbles and led him aside to speak privately.  ‘Of course, Sire, it’s the Royal Prerogative to treat Vasabha and the… and Vidudabha exactly as you choose but might it be a prudent idea to consult with the Master before making any decision?’ Vajiri dared to peek at how the King was taking this suggestion.

204 
He had cooled a bit by now and, collecting his wits, he nodded. It would relieve him of the burden of some difficult choices and the Master so often had a perspective on things that led to benefit for all. ‘Good idea. I think we should go at once.’  With that they made their way to the Jetavana and found that the Master had expected their coming. He met them at the sala and King Pasenadi related all that had occurred.  ‘Venerable Sir, I have heard that your clansmen gave me a slave’s daughter to be my wife. I take this to be a grave insult so I have cut off the wealth of Vasabha and Vidudabha and have deprived them of their position, rendering them both slaves. The woman is in my dungeons since it was she who perpetrated this degrading deception. Do you have any advice for me, Blessèd One, as to how matters should proceed from here?’ ‘Great King, the Sakyans have done grievous wrong, it is true, but have you considered how fickle the matters of birth and rank are?  ‘Not long ago, here in Kosala, a brahmin pandit called Pokkharasati sent a student of his to meet me and to appraise my teaching. When the youth came to me he did not behave politely or respectfully; he referred to me as “a shaven little ascetic, a menial, a grimy off-scouring of Brahma’s foot”, then he described Sakyans as, “fierce, rough-spoken, touchy and violent”. Then, lastly, he stated that of the four castes, three – the kshatriyas, the merchants and the artisans – were entirely subservient to the brahmins, therefore kshatriyas should always pay homage to brahmins and not the other way around.  ‘I then asked this youth, “What is the name of your clan?” To which he replied, “Kanhayan”.  ‘I said to him, “Ambattha,” for that was his name, “the Sakyans regard King Okkaka as their ancestor. That King had a slave-girl called Disa who gave birth to a very dark child. As soon as that child was born he spoke and he was always known as Kanha, meaning ‘The Dark One’. You are descended from him, that is how your clan got its name. Have you heard this story before?” ‘Now Ambattha was abashed and did not want to answer, although I asked him twice. At that time, although I had not called upon such a one to be part of the exchange, the yakkha Vajirapani hovered above the head of the youth bearing a huge iron club, thinking, “If this young man does not answer a question put to him three times by the Master, I’ll split his head into seven pieces.” I had asked Ambattha twice already so the youth, who also saw the yakkha, at last replied, “Yes, I have heard it just as you have said, this is where the Kanhayans come from.”  ‘Further, Great King, in one of my own former lives, a king named Brahmadatta was once strolling in his garden; a young woman had been there, merrily singing away as she picked up sticks, making bundles of kindling wood for the fires. The King was enamoured of her and, after they had spent time together, he gave her his signet ring to acknowledge his parenthood should she bear a child. She

205 
did indeed have a child, who was none other than myself, and in that life I was known as King Katthavahana, “The Kindling Carrier”.  ‘Further, Great King, do you forget that your own belovèd and respected Queen Mallika was a flower girl when you first set eyes upon her? Caste and rank are a great lie. We are purified and exalted, or degraded, by our deeds, words and attitudes, not by our birth: a true warrior faces greed, hatred and delusion fearlessly; a true brahmin is pure of heart; a true merchant is one who is fair and honest in all their dealings; a true craftsperson is one who trains themselves; those “without caste”, who are the most despised and downtrodden in society, they should be honoured most of all since they are close to those who have gone forth into the homeless life of the samana, for we too renounce all caste when we take up the ochre robe. The world does not like us to forget the manner of our physical birth but to be born in the “noble birth” is to reject those foolish values in our hearts. The Dharma has no caste.’ King Pasenadi was sobered and touched by the Master’s words. He turned to Mallika and Vajiri, and the courtiers who had come with them, and said, ‘I will reinstate the Prince, but the army should be under General Digha-Karaniya, not him. As for Queen Vasabha,’ he granted her her title back, ‘I’m… I’m not sure. The deception, the affront by the Sakyans can’t go unpunished. She should stay in her quarters under guard until I’ve decided what should be done.’    * * *  Once all the friends were settled together in the palace garden, Ant brought the golden deva forward, as she had shyly been keeping herself to one side. ‘This is Ambaka,’ the deva made añjali to them all. Bee drew right up close and stared her in the face, ‘Yes it is!’ She clapped and looked about her. ‘Look who it is!’ Ant and Ninka as well as the others looked askance for a moment.  ‘Well I never…’, Gumbiya joined in, ‘you got torched in that fire, back in Kosambi… that little girl by the road, way before. You remember me?’ Ambaka looked as blank as the others for an instant then it came back:– In the woods, beside a stream. A little girl. ‘Amba! Samavati! We lost you! Where have you been?’ Bee was grilling her already.  ‘Oh – of course – how did we miss that?’ Ant looked at Ninka. ‘I suppose we were too taken up with our own stuff.’  ‘And you’ve even got the same name,’ Bee added, ‘well, nearly the same.’ ‘A sprite in… wherever it was we were, gave it to me. I thought it fitted. These colours, they seem to go with me.’  The other kinnaris and their friends did their best to tell her all that had befallen after the burning of Queen Samavati and her companions in Kosambi, and more was added to what Ant and Ninka had told her about their present search and the recent finding of Tambaka. It was a lot to take in.

206 
‘We know where she is but we can’t talk with her yet. She’s a queen but she was raised in a slave family among the Sakyans. We don’t quite know how that happened. Although perhaps the King met her and took a fancy to her and raised her up. She’s very fragrant and pretty too,’ Maggot explained.  Ambaka had the strong feeling that there was something missing. The stories of meeting by the road with the three kinnaris and the yakkha; the burning of the palace building, and her; how strangely proud she had been to be able to die like that, in such an unwavering way; now this deva life… there was a gap.  Bee was continuing the update on their plans and ideas, ‘We haven’t gone to the Jetavana yet and it might be a bit challenging to find someone there who can see us and help connect with Tambaka, and…’ ‘…And there’s the possibility of Krishna being there, assuming he’s still a monk and not gone off on some other inspired jaunt,’ Maggot shook the first pollen out of that particular prickly blossom.  ‘Krishna?’ Ambaka’s brow wrinkled, ‘I’m sure… yes! Yes! He was a good friend, up to the end when…’ and the cut in the dark, at her throat, came back again. The heave on her plait and then the blade, almost painless… It all flooded her senses.  ‘I know Krishna. He’s a good monk but he made some bad friends, who were trying to defame the Master. I was a girl, I met him at a well, I was a candala, an out-caste.’ They could see she was piecing things together as best she could. ‘I felt glad to be able to help the Master – to give my life for such a good cause. I was proud to sacrifice myself.’     * * *  The sudden loss of his rank and to have spent even just a day, half a day, in the life of a slave had been a salutary experience for Vidudabha. His elegant clothes stripped away. His jewellery gone. Pushed into the grungiest quarter of the palace, to share a room with a crowd of other youths, in the grimy clothes he was given – it was a blunt and rude shock to the system.  Up to this point in his life there had never been a time he could remember when he had not been the Crown Prince and a very special person. Such a knowledge can easily wreak havoc with a child’s sensibilities, causing the assumption that the world is arranged principally for your pleasure and convenience. The intoxication of assumed privilege is frighteningly complete in its delusory power, and Vidudabha had had an endless supply of that drug up until now.  For a spell, before word came back with the King from the Jetavana, he was thrown into a reflective state – something that was very rare for him. With his ordinary life suspended he had had the space to consider things. The staff had, thankfully, not assigned him duties as yet so he wasn’t mucking out stables or cleaning lanterns.

207 
‘Snakes and ladders, I guess, that’s what this royal game is – up the ladders, down the snakes all according to the throw of the dice.’ He was curled up, leaning on the wall in the slave’s dormitory. It was peaceful there, and in the rapidly falling light of dusk the eerie pinkish aura that people could sometimes see about him (which had given him the nickname ‘Vidu the Fay’) was clearly visible. ‘Maybe this is where I’ll be for the next ten years or twenty, what will the dice say?’ He opened his hand, as if letting go of everything. ‘If my mother was a slave and cheated the King all these years, what real power could I have? All that indignant raging has just been stupid, youthful folly.’ At these sober reflections he also began to recall the stories he had heard in the interminable classes with the court brahmins. ‘But,’ he clenched his fist, ‘there have always been those who defied the dice, threw the board aside and made their own luck.’ His eyes flared. As with the strange glow he emanated in the half-light, his eyes had a colour and a luminosity that was unique too; they were somewhere between dark-blue and green and they could pierce any dimness with their bright ferocity.  At the doorway of the dormitory the form of a guard appeared. ‘’Is Majesty wants to see you; General Digha is with ’im.’  * * *  The reinstatement as Crown Prince went very quickly; it was enough for the King to summon him in, say, ‘I changed my mind,’ and add not much more than, ‘well, off you go. You can have your rooms and all your things back – most of it should still be there,’ he looked away and shooed his son off with a gesture.  General Digha-Karaniya led the Prince out and walked with him back to his chambers. There was a sly grin back on the Prince’s face, ‘Down the snakes and up the ladders again; that was quick, wasn’t it Digha?’ Vidudabha knew him well from his long hours of military schooling.  ‘Truly, Your Serene Highness. Sir, if I may, I have something of import to discuss. And… er… your current experience with the caprice of your Royal Father’s moods is part of that. Do you have some time?’ Vidudabha was intrigued.  The General began, ‘You no doubt know my uncle, General Bandhula.’  ‘Of course, he has been notable in the field of battle and also in the courts.’ ‘Yes, Sir. He was a man of great qualities and my aunt, his wife Madhu, was similarly blessed. Their marriage, at first, was not happy as they had failed to produce any children. In despair they went to visit the Master and, though he performed no ceremony or made any sign, soon Madhu was expecting, and they were blessed indeed. Over those following years she gave birth to sixteen pairs of twin boys who all followed in their father’s noble footsteps.  ‘When the General was getting too old for the field of conflict he made himself available to perform the role of a judge. Someone who had received a biased

208 
ruling in the court came to him complaining.
