It is to: ‘lie on the right side, placing one foot on the other, mindful and with full awareness, setting in mind the time for wakening.’ It is mentioned, for example, at D 16.4.40, A 3.16.3, A 4.37.4 & A 4.246.3.

325 
 17) ibid:– a master of eloquent and analytical speech: In the list of ‘foremosts’ Ven. Sāriputta is mentioned first. He is named as the monk disciple, ‘foremost among those with great wisdom’.  18) ibid:– the path to Stream-entry … full fruition of that stage: The four levels of enlightenment, in the Buddha’s scheme of things, are each divided into two: ‘magga’, being on the ‘path’ to that level, and ‘phala’, realizing the ‘fruit’ of that level. This structure is the source of the phrase, in the daily chanting, of the qualities of the Sangha Refuge, ‘The four pairs, the eight kinds of noble beings.’  19) p176:– a vigorous walk of about twenty days: The distance from Gayasisa to Sāvatthī is about 310 miles or 500 km.  20) ibid:– He will inevitably go to the states of woe: In the Buddha’s teaching he names five ‘heinous acts’ which, if carried out intentionally, necessarily lead to a stint in the hell realms. The five are: 1) Killing your mother; 2) Killing your father; 3) Killing an Arahant; 4) Shedding the blood of a Buddha; and 5) Deliberately causing a schism in the Sangha. They are listed, as the ‘five lesions’ at A 5.129, the word for this being ‘parikupā’ meaning ‘something with an irritating nature, like an old wound’. The term ‘ānantarika kamma’ is more common, meaning ‘grave deeds that bring as their immediate result a rebirth in hell’. The five are also mentioned at A 6.87 & A 6.94.  21) ibid:– The great Arahant Elders Uppalavanna and Khema: These two great beings were the equivalent, on the women’s side, of Sāriputta and Mahā-Moggallāna – foremost in wisdom was Khemā, and Uppalavaṇṇā in psychic power.   22) p178:– hundreds of devas gathered: This scenario is, again, derived from the commentary to Dhp 48, as found in Buddhist Legends, Vol II, pp52-4.   23) p179:– We haven’t seen you since morning: This is the ending of the story which forms the Commentary to Dhp 48. It is notable that, almost uniquely in Buddhist cosmology and mythology, a being passes away, lives another life and then comes back to their previous place of birth with full knowledge of their history and also with the same physical features. We take this as the poetic license (although, of course, it might have happened that way!) of the author of the Dhammapada Commentary and we are happy to use the same mythic pattern here.

326 
Chapter 20: Prince Vidudabha Visits his Ancestral Home  1) p180:– Vidudabha had been pestering his mother: This long scenario is all based on the Commentary to Dhp 47, as found in Buddhist Legends, Vol II, pp37-52.   2) ibid:– the King had appointed Vidudabha as commander-in-chief of the army: In the original the King does this when his son is no older than seven(!), see Buddhist Legends, Vol II,  p43.  3)  p182:– five gold kahapanas: At gold prices today those five gold pieces would be worth about £1600. A hefty sum indeed for a single job. (See above Ch. 10, note 8)  4) p183:– made offerings both before and after the mealtime: The offerings made in the Southern Buddhist monasteries before the meal and after would be both food and other items, such as cloth, or oil for lamps. If it’s the occasion of a special blessing such as this there would usually be a richer variety and abundance of offerings.   5) ibid:– He seemed to be consciously attuning: This is the way that Dhamma talks are offered in the Southern Buddhist world, particularly in the Thai forest tradition. Talks are not prepared but are, ideally, spontaneous and are a genuine desana (Hindi: darshan) a ‘demonstration’ or embodiment of Dharma, arising from the speaker’s mind and informed by the assembled attendees’ moods, natures and levels of understanding.   6) p184:– the things we customarily take to be who … we are: This passage is based upon the principle first expounded by the Buddha in the ‘Discourse on Not-self’, found at S 22.59, and MV 1.6.38-47, as in The Life of the Buddha, pp45-7. There are comparable teachings found in many other places.   7) ibid:– on account of ignorance: this is ‘avijjā’ in Pali. It means ‘not being aware’ ‘not seeing the reality clearly’ rather than the English usage that has the meaning ‘not possessing certain information’.   8) ibid:– ‘I am’ is a conceiving: This passage is based on M 140.31, the sutta is the ‘Discourse on the Exposition of the Elements’. (See above, Ch. 17, note 20).  9) ibid:– is there a self?: This passage is based on an encounter of the Buddha with Vacchagotta, an adherent of another group of wanderers, who regularly came to ask questions of the Buddha. Eventually he left his original community, became a bhikkhu and

327 
realized full enlightenment (M 73.26). The silent response of the Buddha, and Ānanda’s follow up questions, are found at S 44.10.  10) ibid:– The Master does this sometimes: The Buddha treated questions on the teachings in four different ways; they were 1) Questions deserving a straight answer;  2) Those that need their terms defined before they can be answered; 3) Those that are best responded to by a counter-question; 4) Those that should be put aside.  This list is found at A 4.42. Of the questions ‘to be put aside’, Buddha spoke eloquently of his reasoning for this at M 63.8, the ‘Shorter Discourse to Mālunkyāputta’, and in the famous ‘handful of leaves’ teaching, found at S 56.31.   11) p185:– It is time now: this is an extremely common way for the Buddha to close a discussion or teaching, such as at M 89.20, M 145.6 and S 44.1.  12) ibid:– the King wanted to marry her off to Prince Ajatasattu: The marriage between Vajirī and Ajātasattu is mentioned in commentarial and later literature (e.g. at Jāt 285, Jāt 492 and in the Commentary to Dhp 325, in Buddhist Legends, Vol III, p77) but is never mentioned in the Pali Canon’s suttas or Vinaya texts. Ajātasattu was King of Magadha by the time the marriage was said to have occurred.   13) ibid:– self-view and the threads of conceit: ‘Self-view’, ‘sakkāya-diṭṭhi’ in Pali, is the first of the Ten Obstacles to Enlightenment (saṃyojanā); ‘the conceit of identity’, ‘asmi-māna’ in Pali, is number eight on the list. It is only completely let go of at the realization of Arahantship.  14) p186:– Brave but slowly wise, thus we hail our hero: This phrase comes from the ‘Prologue’ of Wolfram von Eschenbach’s epic Parzival, composed in Middle High German in the first part of the 13th Century.   15) ibid:– If … when asked, ‘Is there a self?’: Again, as above, this comes from S 44.10.  16) p187:– at the death of the body, an existent being is utterly annihilated: The Buddha elucidates this point also at M 22.37: ‘I have been misrepresented baselessly, vainly, falsely and wrongly by some samaṇas and brahmins thus: “The Samaṇa Gotama is one who leads astray; he teaches annihilation, the destruction or the extermination of an existent being.” As I am not; and I do not proclaim this, I have been misrepresented … both formerly and now what I teach is suffering and the ending of suffering.’

328 
 17) ibid:– When a being is enlightened, where do they reappear, after the death of the body?: This passage is derived from another encounter of the Buddha with Vacchagotta, at M 72.16-19, the ‘Discourse to Vacchagotta about Fire’.  18) p190:– lammergeiers: Also known as ‘bearded vulture’, Gypaetus barbatus. They have a wingspan of roughly 2.5m and are scavengers rather than killers. The prince need not have been afraid.    Chapter 21: The Prince’s Fury  1) p194:– hobs and brownies: A ‘hob’ is defined as ‘a kind of sprite’; the name is related to the more familiar word ‘hob-goblin’. It has been speculated that J.R.R. Tolkien’s name for the ‘hobbits’ was informed by this word and the actual word ‘hobbit’ is indeed found in archaic materials. It appears in a book called The Denham Tracts, (published in 1895 but written decades earlier by the Yorkshire tradesman Michael Denham), referring to accounts of witchcraft dating back to the sixteenth century. There are, mentioned there in a list, such beings as: ‘Boggleboes, bogies, redmen, portunes, grants, hobbits, hobgoblins, brown-men, cowies.’  A ‘brownie’ is, similarly, an old word meaning: ‘A benevolent spirit or goblin, of shaggy appearance, supposed to haunt old houses, esp. farmhouses, in Scotland, and sometimes perform useful household work while the family were asleep.’ O.E.D.   2) p195:– Prince Vidudabha was received with splendid and colourful ceremony: This scenario is based upon Jāt 7, Jāt 465 and the Commentary to Dhp 47, as found in Buddhist Legends, Vol II, p44.   3)  p198:– The embassy’s visit only lasted a few days: As above, this is based upon Jāt 7, Jāt 465 and the Commentary to Dhp 47, in Buddhist Legends, Vol II, p44.  4) ibid:– Viraja by name: In the original story, the soldier who goes back to retrieve his sword (or spear, as in Jāt 465) is not named.

329 
Chapter 22: The Attack on the Sakyans  1) p201:– but not by you, slave-son!: In the original story, found in Jāt 7, Jāt 465 and the Commentary to Dhp 47, Buddhist Legends, Vol II, p44, it is said that the King, ‘degraded her from her rank and also degraded her son Viḍūḍabha,’ ‘to the condition of slaves.’ The scenario that follows is informed, as before, by these same texts.   2) p203:– kings and princes regularly consorted with any low-class girl they pleased: This practice, which is recognized as egregious behaviour today, was extremely widespread in the ancient world as well as, unfortunately, up to modern times.   3) ibid:– You will be kept in the palace dungeons: In the original story Vāsabhakhattiyā is not imprisoned.   4) p204:– they made their way to the Jetavana: In the original story the Buddha came to them rather than they going to him: ‘Some few days later the Master came to the palace and took a seat.’  5) ibid:– have you considered how fickle the matters of birth and rank are?: This same issue is explored in Mara and the Mangala I, in a discussion between the Minister Ghosaka and a young Samavati, in Ch. 10, p103.   6) ibid:– a brahmin pandit called Pokkharasati: This passage is based upon D 3, the ‘Ambaṭṭha Sutta’.   7) ibid:– Known as Kanha: The name has been left in the Pali original. The Sanskrit would be ‘Krishna’ so, to avoid confusion as well as to be faithful to sources, it has been kept as per the sutta.   8) ibid:– the yakkha Vajirapani hovered above the head of the youth: The Buddha, and any of the enlightened ones, would never wish harm upon any being, however, it is recognized that certain actions taken, intended to harm a virtuous being, or as in this case, not respond to a direct question when put three times by a Buddha, there will necessarily be a negative consequence. In the scriptures this is called ‘being burnt by the recluse’s fire’, as in the dialogue between the Buddha and King Pasenadi found at S 3.1.   9) ibid:– he gave her his signet ring: This story is from Jāt 7, the ‘Kaṭṭhahāri Jātaka’, it is found in The Jātaka, Vol I, pp27-29.

330 
This arrangement is reminiscent of the ring given by King Dushyanta to Shakuntala, in the Indian legends of the Mahabharata, who had similarly enjoyed a brief relationship and then were parted. In that story Dushyanta is caused to forget Shakuntala and then she loses the ring on the way to being reunited with him. It had been dropped in a pool of water and then swallowed by a fish. A fisherman chances to find it, the ring is produced, and Dushyanta acknowledges their son, who was Bharata, who grew up to ‘conquer the world’. The story is found both in the Mahabharata and in the Abhijnana-Shakuntalam.  A modern equivalent is found in Thailand where a young country-woman, having had a liaison with King Rama II, the King reputedly gave the young woman a silver belt he was wearing and told her, ‘If you have a son, bring him to me at the palace.’ She did and the boy was adopted and raised in the monastery, and became one of the most eminent monks in Thailand, the preceptor and teacher of King Rama IV.   10) p205:– a true warrior faces greed, hatred and delusion: The Buddha consciously transformed the goal of his birth family – warrior nobles – to be spiritual rather than military. He still used the language of battle regularly, however, when describing spiritual endeavours. For example, before his enlightenment he declared, affirming his intent to realize complete liberation, ‘I fly the banner that denies retreat,’ SN 442.  In the Thai forest tradition ‘the warrior heart’ is often used as a phrase to describe the attributes of the dedicated spiritual practitioner.   11) ibid:– a true brahmin is pure of heart: This is spelled out repeatedly in Ch. 26 of the Dhammapada; for example in verses 385, 386, 388, 391, 393 and 396-423.   12) ibid:– a true craftsperson: As described at Dhp 80, and at M 86.18:    ‘Conduit-makers guide the water,  Fletchers straighten out the arrows,  Carpenters shape the timbers  Wise people seek to train themselves.’  13) ibid:– we too renounce all caste: The Buddha was resolute in not allowing the caste system to have any influence on ordination. Thus one can be born into any family and still be eligible to join the monastic order. Those in robes do not observe any caste distinctions, thus a person’s seniority only depends upon the years they have spent in robes.   14) ibid:– I will reinstate the Prince: In the original texts both the Queen and the Prince have their states restored by King Pasenadi, following the encouragement of the Buddha.

331 
15) ibid:– you got torched in that fire: As described in Mara and the Mangala I, Ch. 23, ‘The Perfumed Holocaust’, pp293-304.  16) p206:– I was proud to sacrifice myself: In the Buddha’s teaching he did not praise the sacrifice of one’s physical being for the sake of lofty ideals, or out of mistaken judgements. This did not just relate to dying for spiritual causes but one important aspect of this principle is that, in this, he was also going against the warrior-noble ethic of an assumed virtue to dying in battle, regardless of one’s mind-state. Such values, of dying gloriously in war have had a strong influence in many societies, ancient and modern; for example, in ‘Horatius at the Bridge’:    And how can man die better than facing fearful odds,  For the ashes of his fathers, and the temple of his Gods.       Thomas Babington Macaulay  When asked, by a soldier called Yodhājīva, if it’s true that if one like him dies in battle he will go to the ‘heaven of the battle-slain devas’ the Buddha tells him this is a delusion (S 42.3) and the destination is likely to be one of the lower realms.  Despite this discouragement clearly being the case in the main nikāyas of the Pali Canon, the voluntary relinquishment of one’s life for the sake of others does appear in some post-Canonical stories. For example, the Bodhisattva lived as a hare that threw itself on a fire to provide food for a supposed brahmin, who was actually Indra (in Jāt 316, The Jātaka, Vol III, p37). Another well-known example is ‘The Hungry Tigress’, the first story in the Jātakamāla, written by Aryaśura around the 2nd Century CE. In this story the Bodhisattva sees a starving tigress with cubs at the bottom of a cliff. He throws himself off, to die close to her, in order to provide food for them all.  17) p207:– my uncle, General Bandhula: Again, this follows the scriptural accounts, in Jāt 465 and Commentary to Dhp 47, in Buddhist Legends, Vol II, pp45-49.  18) p208:– King Pasenadi had been sick at heart: This is mentioned in Jāt 465 and in the Commentary to Dhp 47, but not at the related passage at M 89, ‘The Discourse on Monuments to Dhamma’. In the latter text the town near where the Buddha stayed is called Medaḷumpa.  19) ibid:– went up to the door, coughed and knocked: This is the standard way in which visitors approached not only the Buddha’s kuti but it is also how monastics announce their presence at another’s door today. It is found, for example at D 3.1.9.

332 
20) ibid:– the five Symbols of Royalty: These are mentioned in a footnote in The Jātaka, Vol IV, p80.   21) p209:– great mountains … closing in: This motif is mentioned, in part, at S 3.25, with reference to the imminence of ageing and death of King Pasenadi.  His escaping to a place of refuge, having been deposed, is reminiscent of many tales of such vanquished kings. For example, Charles I, after defeat at the Battle of Naseby, on June 14th, 1645, sought refuge at the humble Anglican religious community of Little Gidding. T.S. Eliot refers to this in one of his Four Quartets, also entitled Little Gidding: ‘If you came at night like a broken king’.  In the scriptural accounts Queen Mallikā did not outlive the King but rather passed away before him.  22) p210:– separated Prince Jeta’s head from his shoulders: This is mentioned in Dictionary of Pali Proper Names, p963, quoting Life of the Buddha by A.L. Rockhill (published by Kegan Paul). p121.  23) p211:– her fearless little sister: as mentioned regularly in Mara and the Mangala I.  24) ibid:– our wise friend can walk past all guards: Dusaka displayed this kind of talent in Mara and the Mangala I, Ch. 14, pp 155-7.   25) p213:– train her in deva-vision: This is called the ‘dibbacakkhu’ in Pali, ‘the divine eye’. Dusaka first taught this to Krishna in Mara and the Mangala I , Ch. 15, pp169-71.    Chapter 23: The Buddha in the Shade  1) p215:– her murderer: In their previous lives, as Samāvatī and Māgandiyā, Māgandiyā was responsible for the fire that killed Samāvati, as described in Mara and the Mangala I, Ch. 23, pp293-308.  2) ibid:– bodhi tree, with its sparse leaves … instead of the deep cool shade of the agèd banyan: The story of the Buddha’s attempted intervention, and his choice to sit in the scanty shade of the tree in Sakya, is described in the Commentary to Dhp 47. It is found in Buddhist Legends, Vol II, pp37-52.

333 
3) p216:– that was something your father neglected to do when we met: This refers to the opening scenario of The Pilgrim Kamanita and particularly to Chs. 18-20, pp107-22, (2017).  4) p219:– How has this weakness taken you?: This is adapted from Sir Edwin Arnold’s translation of the Bhagvad Gita, Ch. 2, ‘The Book of Doctrines’.   5) ibid:– Do not become a coward, Arjuna: This is from an alternative translation of the same passage, by Ramananda Prasad.   6) p220:– the worshipful Vajashravas: This is a character invented by Karl Gjellerup for The Pilgrim Kamanita. He features particularly in Ch. 10, ‘Esoteric Doctrine’, pp57-60 ,where he expounds on a theme similar to that stated here.  7) p222: my grandmother was an animal!?: In Jāt 481 a king, frustrated that a pair of captured kinnaris will not sing and dance for him, demands that his staff, ‘Kill these creatures and cook them, and serve them up to me.’ He asked for one to be cooked for his supper and the other for breakfast the next day. In Mara and the Mangala I , Ch. 26, p367, one of the protagonists, Pitipuppha, muses, about Bee, ‘How could a dumb animal like her ever dare to implicate royal devas … she’s practically half plant.’   8) p223:– The Tathagata has exercised his influence three times: See above, Ch. 17, note 18.  9) ibid:– All the warriors of Kapilavatthu: These passages follow closely the narrative of the Commentary to Dhp 47, Buddhist Legends, Vol II, pp50-52.  10) ibid:– His heart was closed to any moral sensitivity: Kilesa-Māra, around whom this collection of plots is woven, is the embodiment of the kilesas = defilements. These are listed as: Greed (lobha), hatred (dosa), delusion (moha), conceit (māna), views (diṭṭhi), skeptical doubt (vicikicchā), sloth (thīna), restlessness (uddhucca), shamelessness (ahirika), lack of a fear of consequences (anottappa). This list of ten is not found in this exact form in the suttas but can be summarized as this group from various sources. ‘Shamelessness’ and the ‘lack of a fear of consequences’ are the kilesa referred to here as indicating being ‘closed to any moral sensitivity’.   11) p225:– We won’t be possessed by the son of a slave-girl: In the Dictionary of Pali Proper Names, p877, the author notes:  ‘According to Chinese records, Viḍūḍabha took five hundred Sakyan maidens into his harem, but they refused to submit to him and abused him and his family. He ordered them to be killed, their hands and feet cut off, and their bodies thrown into

334 
a ditch.’ From The Romantic Legends of the Buddha, S. Beal, published by Kegan Paul, ii, 11f.  In ancient Egypt, the hands of enemies who had fallen in battle were cut off as war trophies and for the practical purpose of accurately counting the casualties.     Chapter 24: Reunions  1) p228:– He was a sincere and dedicated disciple of the Master: This close and beneficial relationship is evidenced in many places, for example: M 89 (which is the ‘Monuments to the Dhamma’ discourse, coincidentally given at the time Pasenadi lost the throne), and the twenty-five suttas of the ‘Connected Discourses with the Kosalan’ S 3.1-25.   2) ibid:– at the local shrines: Such cetiya were frequently visited by both the Buddhist Sangha and many adherents of other faiths, although it is rare for it to be mentioned in the Pali Canon what deity or being those shrines were dedicated to. Examples abound – just near Vesālī alone there are mentioned the: Udena Shrine, Gotamaka Shrine, Saltambaka Shrine, Bahuputta Shrine, Sārandada Shrine and the Cāpāla Shrine (in D 16.3.44-47).  3) ibid:– King Pasenadi had had a number of other, so-called ‘minor wives’: The first three of these wives are mentioned in the texts; the ‘sister of King Bimbisara’, named Vimalā here, does not seem to be named in the suttas and commentaries. Kosaladevī was indeed the name of Pasenadi’s sister and Queen of Magadha.  Ubbirī’s new-born child died and this caused her to leave the palace life and become the Buddha’s disciple. She became an Arahant. Her verses are found at Thig 51-2. Somā and Sakulā were sisters; they are mentioned at M 90.3.  4) p229:– to appoint Queen Mallika as Royally Anointed Regent: These titles, and indeed the regency, are an invention of the author.  5) ibid:– The Princess … as yet unmarried: According to the commentaries and the Jātaka, Princess Vajiri was given, by her father, in marriage to Ajātasattu (in Jāt 283 & Jāt 492).  6) p232:– to live on the Eight Precepts: This is the standard framework of conduct for anyone living in a monastery of the Southern School of Buddhism. Exceptions can be made (usually allowing working staff to eat in the evening) but the Eight Precepts is the general basis for life in any monastery.

335 
7) ibid:– Sister Khema: She was the Arahant nun recognized as ‘Foremost amongst nuns possessed of great wisdom’  – mahāpaññānaṃ aggā (at A 1.236)’ On one occasion she gave teachings to King Pasenadi (at S 44.1); her verses of enlightenment are found at Thig 139-44.   8) ibid:– Oh, what bliss!: This expression is borrowed from the account of the ex-ruler of the Sakyans, Bhaddiya, on having renounced the throne and entered the monastic life (at Ud 2.10).   9) p233:– a lot of layers of resultant karma: This is called ‘vipāka’ in Pali. Often when the word ‘karma’ is employed in common usage, it is meant to mean ‘kamma-vipāka’ i.e. ‘intended action and its result’.   10) ibid:– when he had been a child in the garden in Ujjeni: See Mara and the Mangala I, Ch. 15, pp169-71.  11) ibid:– Let go of the past: The advice given here is a combination of ‘The Discourse on One Fortunate Attachment’ (M 131.3) and the frequently given instruction on learning from our mistakes and transgressions, such as at M 140.33.   12) ibid:– visible world, invisible world: In the Buddha’s teaching he defined ‘the world’ as ‘The eye, the ear, the nose, the tongue, the body, the mind.’ The ‘world’ is thus taken to be the world of our experience, rather than the external objects like the planet and the stars, (see S 35.116).  13) p235:– he clapped his huge hands together: This method is informed by a monk who once told a group of young Buddhists whom he was guiding for a class, ‘I guarantee you will learn to levitate, but you’ve got to keep your eyes closed.’ Once he was sure everyone there was complying, he pulled an oogah horn out of his bag and set it off in the room. All the participants succeeded in levitating but a few took some time to recover. It was intended to be a lesson in perception – saññā.  14) p236:– evil counsel: This is the translation of the name ‘Mordred’, who was the equally ill-starred son of Morgan le Fay and King Arthur, who brought about Arthur’s death and the breaking of the Round Table.   15) p237:– Checking to see there was water: Such duties in preparing the Dhamma seat before a teaching are still carried out today, although electric fans are now ubiquitous in contrast to the paddle-like, classical ones used in electricity-free locations.

336 
 16) ibid:– Evam – thus it is: This is a classical way to close a Dhamma talk. However, this form of formally ending a teaching is not found in the Pali Canon.   17) p239:– The great lay disciple Visakha: She was one of the great patrons of the Sangha in Sāvatthī and the region. She built the Monastery of the Eastern Park and donated it to the Sangha, as well as having provided substantial material support, on a smaller scale, as described at Vin i 290 and MV 8.  18) p240:– For what reason would you pay honour: This dialogue is based on the exchange between the Buddha and the sick monk Vakkali, shortly before his realization of Arahantship and his death (at S 22.87).   19) p241:– the Aviha Brahma World: This is the lowest of the five Pure Abodes, being high brahma realms that only Non-returners are born into. The other four are, in ascending order: Atappā, Sudassā, Sudassī, Akaniṭṭhā. For more information on these realms, see The Buddhist Cosmos, by Ajahn Puṇṇadhammo, 3:6:17-20, pp660-72 in the 1st edition.   20) ibid:– She realized Arahantship even before she reappeared there: There is an interesting set of similes given by the Buddha on the different types of Non-returner (at A 7.55). Of the seven different grades, from the quickest to enlightenment to the slowest, the first three are all described as ‘realizing Nibbāna in the interval’. Khujjuttarā is presented as being amongst these quickest ones. The imagery he uses is of ‘an iron bowl, when heated all day and is struck, a chip which is red-hot might fly off’.   21) ibid:– She was foremost: This refers to the Buddha’s naming her as bahussutanaṃ – the most excellent of his lay women disciples in terms of analytical knowledge.   22) p242:– Patient with my inability to forgive: This is derived from the title of a Dhamma talk, given by Ajahn Sumedho in 1995.  23) ibid:– Like the Bodhisattva Gotama: The story this refers to is found at Jāt 66, ‘The Story of the “Sign of Gentleness” Birth’, in The Jātaka, Vol I, pp161-64.   24) ibid:– to the Himavant: A legendary forest stretching over a large region of the south-facing Himalayan mountains.

337 
 Chapter 25: Lone Wanderings  1) p243:– thereby breaking one of the Precepts: The seventh of the Eight Precepts is, ‘I undertake the Precept to refrain from entertainment, beautification and adornment’. Thus any use of perfume, jewellery, make-up and suchlike are not allowed for monastic residents and those adhering to the Eight Precepts.   2) ibid:– it was Magandiya: See Mara and the Mangala I, Ch. 21, pp268-9.   3) ibid:– it also reminds you that life is very uncertain and undependable: The active contemplation of the fabricated and fragile nature of the body was regularly recommended by the Buddha. This is to support non-identification with the body and the appreciation of the fundamental Law of Uncertainty in nature. See, for example A 5.57, the 'Five Subjects for Frequent Recollection.   4) p244:– flow of floral delight: For a description this action, see Mara and the Mangala I, Ch. 23, pp300-1.   5) ibid:– the profound lessons she had learned: See Mara and the Mangala I , Ch. 23, pp294-307.  6) ibid:– Mindfulness is the path to the Deathless: This verse is Dhp 21. The lengthy ‘Story Cycle of King Udena’ is the commentary to this verse. It is the longest entry in the Dhammapada Commentary.   7) ibid:– forest chickens: These are ubiquitous in India, South-east Asia and China. The most common ones are Grey Jungle fowl – (Gallus sonneratii) and Red Junglefowl (Gallus gallus).   8) ibid:– kukals: This common bird is also known as ‘crow pheasant’ (Centropus sinensis). It has a very deep, sonorous, distinctive call.   9) p246:– defer to the Mahasattva: A Mahāsattva is a great Bodhisattva who has been on the path for a long time. Examples would be the Bodhisattvas Maitreya, Avalokiteshvara, Mañjushrī, Samantabhadra and Ksitigarbha.    10) ibid:– One who is invited to share reflections: These criteria are mentioned at A 8.16 and CV 7.4.

338 
 11) ibid:– If some samana or brahmin asks you: This dialogue is derived from S 22.85 and 22.86, and the Buddha’s interactions with the bhikkhus Yamaka and Anuradha, respectively.   12) ibid:– the amulet you gave me: See Mara and the Mangala I, Ch. 15, pp177-9.  13) p247:– That prophecy: See Mara and the Mangala I, Ch. 26, p355.  14) ibid:– the Eternal Youth, Sanat Kumara: This is a brahma god who is known by this name on account of his ever-young appearance. He is one of the few brahma deities who follows the teachings of the Buddhas. He is mentioned at D 18.17-29, M 53.25, and S 6.11.   15) ibid:– The sickness continued for many Moons: The narrative beginning here, about Devadatta’s last days, is derived from the Commentary to Dhp 17. It is also found at CV 7.4.  16) ibid:– Siddhartha had always been the favourite: This was the Buddha’s name, customarily only used up to the time of his renunciation of palace life.   17) p249:– made it intrinsically impossible for them to meet: There were a number of occasions in the Buddha’s life, or qualities of the natural order that were named, where the Buddha stated in this categorical way: ‘It is impossible, it cannot be.’ This situation with Devadatta is one example. There are many listed at A 1.268-95, as well as other examples in the Pali Canon.   18) p250:– Surama, who looked disconsolate: Surama is not a Canonical figure but an invention of the author. Here his mood echoes the ‘Stanzas of Disappointment’ uttered by Māra, after he has failed to fool the Buddha (as at S 4.24 & SN 446-49):   ‘Step by step for seven years  I have followed the Blessèd One.   The Fully Enlightened One, possessed  Of mindfulness, gave me no chance.   A crow there was who walked around  A stone that seemed a lump of fat;  “Shall I find something in this?   And is there something tasty here?”  He, finding nothing tasty there,   Made off: and we from Gotama

339 
 Depart in disappointment, too,   Just like the crow that tried the stone.’ Full of sorrow he let his vina slip from under his arm; and then the unhappy demon vanished. Now when Māra, the Evil One, had spoken these Stanzas of Disappointment in the Blessèd One’s presence, he left that place and sat down cross-legged on the ground not far from the Blessèd One, silent, dismayed, with shoulders drooping and head down, glum and with nothing to say, scraping the ground with a stick.       (Bhikkhu Ñāṇamoli trans.)  The figure of Surama represents one of the two main ways that Māra appears to operate, this is by him manifesting a living being’s form as a disguise, for example as a nāga (at S 4.6), as a farmer, (S 4.19) or as a huge elephant (S 4.2).  The other main way that Māra influences things is by entering the minds of already existent beings, such as Muñca in this story or, in the Pali Canon, (at S 4.18), where he persuades the villagers not to give alms; or when he enters the mind of a certain youth who then hurls a stone at the attendant of the Buddha Kakusandha, Ven. Vidhura, injuring his head (M 50.21); or when he influenced the mind of one of the Assembly of the brahma god Baka, and then the whole Assembly (at M 49.5).   19) p251:– three-striped palm squirrels: These are common in India (Funambulus palmarum). In Indian myth the stripes are taken to have been caused by the hero Rāma who stroked its back with three fingers after a squirrel helped him to build the bridge to Lanka.   20) ibid:– long ago, Goddess Nü-wa: This opening part of Dusaka’s chanted tale is derived from The Story of the Stone by Cao Xuequin, Ch. 1, David Hawkes trans. (published by Penguin Classics).  21) p253:– Like that of Yeshe Tsogyal: See Mara and the Mangala I, Ch. 20, pp254-5.  22) ibid:– Even the Bodhisattva Gotama: The following narrative is derived from Jāt 66.   23) p256:– His duty as a grateful student: In the texts of monastic discipline, the Vinaya, it describes the duties a teacher has towards their students and, in turn, the duties the student has with respect to their teacher. Amongst these are: ‘If the preceptor is bordering on an offence then, speaking himself, the student should warn him.’ (MV 1.25.10); ‘If wrong views have arisen in the preceptor, the student should dissuade him from them.’ ‘If the preceptor has committed a serious offence, and is resistant to correction by the Sangha, then the student should consider, “How then can the preceptor be persuaded to conduct

340 
himself properly, subdue his bad behaviour, mend his ways so that he can be rehabilitated?”’ (MV 1.25.20-22).  24) p258:– the famed Cauldrons of Youth: This narrative is derived from the Russian fairytale ‘Ivan – Young of Years, Old of Wisdom’, in Vasilisa the Beautiful:– Russian Fairy Tales, Dorian Rottenberg trans., Progress Publishers, (1966), Moscow.   25) p259:– AN APSARA OF THE WATERS: Rhamba, one of the characters of this story, is an apsara, as is her brother Salassa. The most ancient descriptions of apsara portray them as water nymph-like beings (e.g. in the Rigveda). They were originally female spirits of the clouds and waters. The speciality of dancing came later.  26) p260:– That inner voice: As above, see note 18 for this Chapter. In a comparable fashion, the murderer Anna Marie Hahn wrote in her prison letters to the Cinncinati Enquirer, in December 1938, shortly before her execution in the electric chair: ‘I don’t know how I could have done the things I did in my life.’  27) p261– THIS WORKS FOR THE VIRTUOUS: This principle is mentioned in a variety of places in the Pali Canon such as at A 8.35, where the Buddha speaks about: ‘Eight kinds of rebirth on account of generosity’. He describes how, after having made offering food or lodgings, robes etc., that person may set their mind on being reborn amongst wealthy householders, devas of the various levels, up to the Brahma Realms. ‘If they set their mind on that thought, keep to it firmly and foster it, then after the death of the body they will be reborn in the realm according to their wishes. This, however, I declare is only for the virtuous, not for the unvirtuous … Because their hearts are free from unwholesome passions, the heart’s desire of the virtuous succeeds.’  The scenario with the cauldrons is reminiscent of the test Sitā gave herself in the Rāmāyaṇa: After Sitā has been rescued from Lanka by Hanuman, Rāma asks to have her brought to him. She is exceedingly happy to see him at last and to be reunited. Rāma, however, is cold towards her and speaks to her cruelly. He announces to her, and to everyone else, that he cannot accept her as his wife after she has spent such a long time in another man’s company –‘in Ravana’s lap’. Astonishingly, and heartlessly he tells her that the only reason he actioned Hanuman to rescue her ‘… was to save my own honour.’ Sitā proclaims her virtue, her maintenance of the brahmacariya vows, her unwavering devotion to Rāma and her innocence. To prove her virtue she asks Lakshmana to light a -re. She walks into the -re and emerges unscathed – even the lotus in her hair is unscorched. There is sweat upon her brow after the ordeal but this largely due to her indignation about the whole procedure.

341 
Rāma, somewhat unconvincingly, then claims that he had known that she was innocent all along but had wanted her to prove it to the world.   28) p262:– The precious few items a wandering forest monk might need: A forest monastic always has to carry their robes, alms-bowl, needle and thread, a water-strainer (to not harm small creatures in natural water supplies), a belt and a razor. In addition, a good water-canteen or gourd, a length of thin rope (to hang and dry robes after washing or just to air them), a small sharp knife and means of making light are all helpful.   29) ibid:– He had said farewell in gratitude: It is regarded as a good policy to consciously appreciate the feelings of the beings, visible and invisible that one has shared a place with.     Chapter 26: On the Western Shore  1) p263:– Jammu, Swami-ji: This region is in the Indian state of Jammu and Kashmir of today. The address ‘swami-ji’ is a way that ascetic yogis and religious seekers, renunciants are addressed in India today.   2) ibid:– Taxila?: This was an ancient city of great consequence. It is mentioned many times in Buddhist scriptures as a centre of great learning. It is spelled ‘Takkasilā’ in Pali. It was the capital of Gandhara. It is to be found today about 20 miles west of Islamabad in Pakistan.   3) ibid:– River Vitasta: Today this is known as the River Jhelum.   4) ibid:– stone-built houses … layered with wooden timbers: This is still a method used today, in order to build with stone in regions prone to earthquakes – Jammu, Kashmir, Himachal Pradesh and Kulluta are all of this nature. The stones can slide side-to-side along the horizontal wooden beams, thus enabling the buildings to move without falling.   5) ibid:– iron alms-bowl … maintained with great care: Such bowls are still used today. The surface is protected from rusting by baking the bowl in a very hot bonfire or forge, to form an oxide coating, but this coating can be chipped; the training with respect to the alms-bowl – whether it is iron or steel like this, or of clay or even stainless steel these days – is to treat it as if it was the skull of the Buddha.

342 
6) p264:– Bhavatu sabba mangalam, rakkhantu sabba devata: This is the start of the blessing given at the meal-time, customarily in Thailand. It means ‘May there be every blessing, may the devas protect you.’  7) ibid:– Me bhikkhu, bhikshu: The latter word is the Sanskrit spelling of ‘bhikkhu’. This language, and hybrids of it with Pali were often used in North India and the region mentioned in the story here.   8) ibid:– Repaired with woven tree-bark: This method has been used up to the present time, by forest meditation monks in Thailand.   9) p265:– the paddy-field patterns: The Buddha asked Ānanda to design a patterning of monastic robes, to easily include smaller and larger pieces of scrap cloth, and to sew them ‘like these rice-fields of Magadha.’ Since that ancient time, this has been the form used for Budddhist monastic robes, up to the present day (see MV 8.12.1).  10) p266:– Keeping the old for patches: It is common practice, among forest monastics, to salvage what serviceable parts of old robes etc. one can for future repairs as needed. See Mara and the Mangala I , Ch. 21, pp263-66.  11) ibid:– was quite the philosopher: As described in The Pilgrim Kamanita, especially Chs. 9 and 10, pp51-60.  12) ibid:– a bhikkhu of nearly seventy Rains: The practice in the monastic order is for those younger in years of monastic training to make room for, and offer the highest and most comfortable seat, to any Elders they are with. They should also listen respectfully, as one would expect, to what an Elder has to say.   13) p267:– the ‘highest blessings’ of the Teacher: This refers to the ‘Discourse on the Highest Blessings’, as recited to Krishna in Mara and the Mangala I , Ch. 26, pp365-66.   14) ibid:– Gandhara, Ariana: This list of names of countries and regions is probably not as the locals would have referred to those places in all instances in that era. They are listed, dear reader, with these names for convenience of today’s readership.   15) p268:– tents of hazel-rods: Such tents are called ‘benders’ in current times and are still in use in communities of Travellers and other country-dwelling folk inclined to simple living. The name ‘bender’ comes from bending cut hazel saplings in the rails of a fence or, in ancient times, in between the upright trunks of close-standing trees, into U-shapes, then

343 
arranging them to form a small, domed chamber. They are draped in blankets and canvas to keep the bell-shaped space warm. The floor is a bed of hazel twigs covered by canvas, skins or rugs, so rodents etc. can move easily underneath and the human occupant is off the cold damp ground. Skins would have been used as well as waxed cloth in former times.   16) ibid:– land of Gavinna and her fathers: ‘The Land of My Fathers’ is the Welsh national anthem. It was composed by Evan James and his son James James. In Welsh it is called ‘Hen Wlad Fu Nhadan’. See Mara and the Mangala I, Ch. 22, p281.  17) ibid:– the Master’s teachings and stories … to visit the upper realms: Examples of such visits are found, firstly, at M 49, ‘The Discourse on an Invitation to a Brahma’, where the Buddha pays a call on the Brahmā Baka, to help relieve him of a wrong view, the deity having formerly been a teacher of the Buddha in a previous life.  Another example is found at D 11.67-85, ‘The Discourse to Kevaddha’, where a monk skilled in meditation goes, eventually, up to the Heaven of Maha-Brahmā to ask a question that he cannot answer himself.  In neither instance do the visitors to those upper realms experience extreme passing of time in the human world.   18) ibid:– in your etheric bodies: This is a Western term for what is known as ‘sukhumarūpa’ in Pali meaning ‘subtle body’. Other related terms are ‘mano-maya-kāya’ or ‘mind-made body’ and ‘obhāsa-kāya’, a ‘body of light’, which the Buddha used to project an image of himself, in order to offer teachings to his disciples in remote places.  In the Tibetan tradition the term ‘saṃbhoga-kāya’ has some equivalence to these qualities named in the Pali. It is defined as a ‘body of bliss’ and it is used in celestial manifestations. The ‘nirmāna-kāya’ means the physical body.   19) p269:– concentrated on their breath: This type of meditation practice is common in the Southern Buddhist world. It is called ‘ānāpanasati’ or ‘mindfulness of in-and-out breathing’.   20) ibid:– Vasabha and Kesini emerged: Such deep and extensive times spent in complete absorption are well known, even in modern times; for example, both Great Master Hsu Yün and his disciple, Master Hsüan Hua were well-known to have these abilities. Also Dipa Ma, a lay woman practitioner who lived in Calcutta, was fully capable of such absorptions.   21) ibid:– the two of them sitting face-to-face … for months: There is an account, in the Chinese records of one Great Master, describing how ‘the happiest time of my life’ was sitting face-to-face with a monk friend, in samadhi like this, for forty-two days.

344 
22) ibid:– Kesini had grown some more grey hairs: As above, (at note 17 of this Chapter) the canonical accounts do not mention an experience of vast amounts of time passing for the physical bodies (rūpa-kāya) of those visiting the higher realms in their sukhumarūpa, or subtle body.   23) p274:– Look, stranger: Several lines in the following passages are derived from the poem Look, Stranger! by W.H. Auden, published in 1936 in his book of the same name, later entitled On This Island.   24) ibid:– in Gallia: The Central European area was not divided up into distinct nation states in the era of Krishna’s wanderings. ‘Gallia’ here roughly refers to the regions of France and Germany in the current age. Folk mostly seemed to live in clans and tribes, some nomadically, in this mid-iron age, pre-historic time.   25) ibid:– pigyn hendaid: This is the modern Welsh for ‘great-grandfather magpie’.  26) ibid:– now with him for over fifty Rains: This would be a long time to look after an iron bowl and to keep it in good condition, whilst using it every day.   27) p275:– matter we are all fashioned from: In one Celtic creation myth it states:  Once upon a time, when there was not time, no gods or humans walked the surface of the land. But there was the sea, and where the sea met the land, a mare was born, white and made of sea-foam. And her name was Eiocha.  Not far from where the land met the sea, a tree grew, a strong and sturdy oak. On the oak grew a plant whose seeds were formed of the foam tears of the sea. To sustain her, Eiocha ate the seeds, those white berries, and they were transformed within her. Eiocha grew heavy with child and gave birth to the god Cernunnos. So great was her pain in childbirth that she ripped bark from the tree and hurled it into the sea. The bark was transformed by the sea and became the giants of the deep.  Cernunnos was lonely and he saw that the giants of the deep were numerous, so he joined with Eiocha and of their union came the gods Maponos, Tauranis, Tentates and the goddess Epona.  Eiocha soon tired of the land, being a creature of sea-foam, and she returned to the sea, where she was transformed into Tethra, goddess of the deep water, also known as Tethys.  (ochsmrsg.weebly.com)

345 
28) p276:– Do good, refrain from harm: This is part of the ‘Rule of Discipline Exhortation’ one of the earliest teachings of the Buddha. It was first given to a spontaneous gathering of 1250 Arahants at the Bamboo Grove, near Rājagaha.   29) ibid:– The best way to evade the King of Death: This is the method of meditation described by the Bodhisattva Avalokiteshvara (Guan Shi Yin) in The Shurangana Sūtra, Ch. 3, p256 (BTTS/DRBU, 2009). This method is named there by the Buddha as ‘the easiest way to reach enlightenment’, amongst those described to him by the twenty-five Bodhisattvas who visit him during that particular teaching.   30) ibid:– within the bounds of his discipline: The Buddhist monastic Rule forbids the handling, storing or controlling the use of food that one might later consume. One is to be fully dependent on unsolicited free will offerings, that have been out of one’s hands from the get-go.   31) ibid:– it was an inner sound: This sound goes by many names in different traditions. See Inner Listening, by the author, pp49-57.   32) p277:– like a diamond someone had sewn into your clothes: This comes from a parable in the Lotus Sūtra, Ch. 8.  33) p278:– the process of spontaneously appearing: This is frequently the way in which beings are described if they have arisen in the Pure Abodes. That such beings appear ‘spontaneously’ means there is no parentage of any kind, they simply manifest, fully grown and cognizant in one of those Abodes.   34) p279:– the intermediary state: In Pali this is called the antarābhava; in Tibetan such transitional dimensions are known as bardos.  ===*=== *
